| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Psalm 55 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a powerful expression of the pain of betrayal by a close friend.                                                                          | Betrayal by a trusted companion is a profound wound. The faithful may experience deep anguish.               |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 62: "I have sons, I have wealth"—thinking thus, the fool is troubled. But the fool himself is not his own; how can sons or wealth be his?" (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 119): Attachment leads to suffering, including the suffering of betrayal.                                 | Attachment to relationships leads to suffering when those relationships break. The pain of betrayal is real. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): Yudhishthira is betrayed by his own brothers in the dice game.                                 | Betrayal by those closest to you is part of the impermanent world. The pain is real but temporary.           |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Asr (103:1–3): "By time, indeed mankind is in loss, except for those who have believed and done righteous deeds and advised each other to truth and advised each other to patience." (Corollary from Hadith): The Prophet experienced betrayal and rejection from those closest to him. | Betrayal by trusted companions is a test of faith. Patience is required.                                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the righteous may be betrayed by those they trust. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                                                       | Betrayal by trusted companions is a reality. The faithful do not lose faith.                                 |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person may be betrayed by those they trust. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                                              | Betrayal by trusted companions is a reality. The noble person remains virtuous.                              |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the betrayed. Even when those closest to you turn against you, the ancestors know the truth. The faithful are not forgotten."                                                                                                          | Betrayal by trusted companions is painful, but the ancestors see the truth. The faithful are not forgotten.  |

#### **Principle 3: Trust in the Moral Order's Vindication**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                  |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 55:22): "Cast your burden on the LORD, and he will sustain you; he will never permit the righteous to be moved."                                                                | The faithful can cast their burdens on the moral order. It will sustain them and vindicate the righteous. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 125: "Whosoever offends the harmless, faultless, and guiltless person, that very folly comes back upon that fool, even as fine dust thrown against the wind returns upon the one who throws it." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 119): The wicked will suffer the consequences of their deeds. | The moral order will ultimately vindicate the innocent. The wicked will face consequences.                |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 6:40): The sincere striver does not perish.                                                                                         | The moral order preserves and vindicates the devoted. The faithful trust in this vindication.             |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:160): "If Allah should aid you, no one can overcome you; but if He should forsake you, who is there that can aid you after Him?"                                                                                                                                                         | The moral order is the ultimate source of aid and vindication.                                            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the protector of the just. Those who act with truth and integrity will ultimately be vindicated. The wicked may triumph for a time, but the righteous will endure."                                                                                                 | The moral order will vindicate the just. The faithful trust in this vindication.                          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is not anxious about being unknown; they are anxious about not being virtuous. The moral order will eventually recognise virtue."                                                                                                                               | The moral order will eventually recognise and vindicate virtue.                                           |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the heart. Those who are wrongfully betrayed will be vindicated by the ancestors. The truth will come to light."                                                                                                                                                  | The ancestors will vindicate the wrongfully betrayed. The truth will ultimately come to light.            |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the anguish of betrayal and trust in the moral order's vindication. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the anguish of betrayal is a grievous wound.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's description of betrayal by a trusted companion (Psalm 55), as the Jewish teaching that the psalm is a powerful expression of such pain, as the Buddhist teaching that attachment leads to suffering, as Yudhishthira's betrayal by his brothers (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that betrayal is a test of patience, as the Sikh teaching that betrayal is a reality, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may be betrayed, or as the African understanding that even those closest to you may turn against you, all traditions teach that ****betrayal by trusted companions is deeply painful****. It is not a minor wound but a profound source of anguish.

****Second, trust in the moral order's vindication sustains the faithful.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order will sustain the righteous and bring down the wicked (Psalm 55), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order will vindicate the righteous, as the Buddhist teaching that the wicked will face consequences, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is the ultimate source of aid, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine vindicates the just, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order recognises virtue, or as the African understanding that the ancestors vindicate the wrongfully betrayed, all traditions teach that ****the faithful can trust in the moral order's vindication****. Human faithfulness may fail, but the moral order does not.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge the deep pain of betrayal while teaching trust in the moral order's vindication provide a moral framework that prevents despair and revenge in the face of treachery. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to seek personal vengeance and more likely to trust in the moral order's ultimate justice. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust in justice and resilience in the face of betrayal****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 55, Principle 1 ("The anguish of betrayal") and Principle 3 ("Trust in the moral order's vindication") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's anguish over betrayal (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that betrayal is a grievous wound, as the Buddhist teaching that attachment leads to suffering, as Yudhishthira's betrayal (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that betrayal is a test of patience, as the Sikh teaching that betrayal is a reality, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may be betrayed, and as the African understanding that even those closest to you may turn against you.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the anguish of betrayal and trust in the moral order's vindication.**** The ethical lesson—that betrayal is deeply painful, but the faithful can trust in the moral order's ultimate vindication—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 56: The Ethics of Trust Over Fear and the Moral Order's Attention to Suffering**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 56 is a psalm of trust in the face of fear, attributed to David when the Philistines seized him in Gath. The psalmist declares their trust in the moral order despite being pursued by enemies who "hotly pursue" them. The psalm acknowledges fear but affirms that trust in the moral order overcomes fear. The psalmist expresses confidence that the moral order has recorded their tears and will deliver them, enabling them to walk before the moral order in the light of life.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: according to The Dove on Far-off Terebinths. Of David. A Miktam, when the Philistines seized him in Gath.****

****1**** Be gracious to me, O God, for people trample on me; all day long foes oppress me; ****2**** my enemies trample on me all day long, for many fight against me. O Most High, ****3**** when I am afraid, I put my trust in you. ****4**** In God, whose word I praise, in God I trust; I am not afraid; what can flesh do to me? ****5**** All day long they seek to injure my cause; all their thoughts are against me for evil. ****6**** They stir up strife, they lurk, they watch my steps, as they hoped to take my life. ****7**** Do not let them escape; in your anger, O God, bring the peoples down. ****8**** You have kept count of my tossings; put my tears in your bottle. Are they not in your book? ****9**** Then my enemies will retreat in the day when I call. This I know, that God is for me. ****10**** In God, whose word I praise, in the LORD, whose word I praise, ****11**** in God I trust; I am not afraid. What can a mere mortal do to me? ****12**** My vows to you I must perform, O God; I will render thank offerings to you. ****13**** For you have delivered my soul from death, and my feet from falling, so that I may walk before God in the light of life.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                      |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Fear****                  | The psalmist acknowledges their fear of enemies who pursue and oppress them. Fear is a legitimate human response.                     |
| ****Principle 2: Trust in the Moral Order Overcomes Fear**** | The psalmist declares, "In God I trust; I am not afraid." Trust is the antidote to fear.                                              |
| ****Principle 3: The Moral Order Sees Suffering****          | The psalmist declares that the moral order has recorded their tears and kept count of their tossings. The faithful are not forgotten. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 56**

#### **Principle 2: Trust in the Moral Order Overcomes Fear**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                      |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 56:3–4 itself): "When I am afraid, I put my trust in you. In God I trust; I am not afraid."                   | Trust in the moral order is the antidote to fear. When afraid, the faithful turn to trust.    |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 103: "Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, yet he who conquers himself is the greatest warrior." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The one who has conquered themselves has no fear.        | Trust in the path of self-conquest overcomes fear. The greatest warrior conquers fear itself. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:15: "That man who is not disturbed by these and is equal in pleasure and pain, he alone is fit for immortality." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                                            | Equanimity and trust in the moral order overcomes fear. The faithful are told, "Do not fear." |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'" | Faith in the moral order overcomes fear. The faithful say, "Sufficient for us is Allah."      |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee has no fear, for they trust in the Divine. Fear is the enemy of the soul; trust is the shield against fear."                                                                                   | Trust in the Divine overcomes fear. Fear is the enemy; trust is the shield.                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is calm and composed; the small person is perpetually worried and anxious."                                                                                                                   | The noble person, who trusts in the moral order, is calm and without fear.                    |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors protect those who trust in them. The person who is under the protection of the ancestors has no fear."                                                                                              | Trust in the ancestors leads to protection and deliverance from fear.                         |

#### **Principle 3: The Moral Order Sees Suffering**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 56:8): "You have kept count of my tossings; put my tears in your bottle. Are they not in your book?" | The moral order sees and remembers the suffering of the faithful. No tear is forgotten. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 129: "All tremble at violence; all fear death. Comparing oneself to others, one should not kill nor cause to kill." (Corollary from Metta Sutta): The wise person extends compassion to all who suffer.               | Suffering is real and seen by the wise. Compassion is the appropriate response.         |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                        | The moral order sees and responds to the suffering of the devoted. They are preserved.  |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me."                                                                  | The moral order is near and responds to the suffering of the faithful.                  |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine sees all and knows all. The tears of the faithful are not wasted; they are recorded."                                                                                                       | The Divine sees and records the suffering of the faithful. No tear is wasted.           |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order sees and responds to the virtuous. The suffering of the noble person is not unnoticed."                                                                                               | The moral order sees the suffering of the virtuous. It is not unnoticed.                |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see all. The tears of the suffering are not hidden from the ancestors. They are remembered."                                                                                               | The ancestors see and remember the tears of the suffering.                              |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the overcoming of fear through trust and the moral order's seeing of suffering. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, trust in the moral order overcomes fear.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that trust overcomes fear (Psalm 56), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful turn to trust when afraid, as the Buddhist teaching that self-conquest overcomes fear, as the Gita's teaching of equanimity and "do not fear," as the Qur'anic teaching that faith overcomes fear, as the Sikh teaching that trust is the shield against fear, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person is calm and composed, or as the African understanding that the ancestors protect those who trust in them, all traditions teach that ****trust is the antidote to fear****. The faithful are not defined by their fear but by their trust.

****Second, the moral order sees and records the suffering of the faithful.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order has recorded their tears (Psalm 56), as the Jewish teaching that no tear is forgotten, as the Buddhist teaching that the wise person extends compassion to all who suffer, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine sees and records, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order sees the suffering of the virtuous, or as the African understanding that the ancestors see and remember the tears of the suffering, all traditions teach that ****the suffering of the faithful is not ignored****. The moral order is attentive and responsive.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach trust overcomes fear and that the moral order sees suffering provide a moral framework that sustains hope and prevents despair in times of persecution or hardship. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to be paralysed by fear and more likely to trust in the moral order's ultimate justice. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust and hope****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 56, Principle 2 ("Trust in the moral order overcomes fear") and Principle 3 ("The moral order sees suffering") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration of trust (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that trust overcomes fear, as the Buddhist teaching that self-conquest overcomes fear, as the Gita's teaching of equanimity (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that faith overcomes fear, as the Sikh teaching that trust is the shield against fear, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person is calm and composed, and as the African understanding that the ancestors protect those who trust in them.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning trust over fear and the moral order's attention to suffering.**** The ethical lesson—that trust in the moral order overcomes fear, and that the moral order sees and remembers the suffering of the faithful—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 57: The Ethics of Refuge and Praise Despite Danger**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 57 is a psalm of lament and trust, attributed to David when he fled from Saul into a cave. The psalmist takes refuge in the moral order, crying out for mercy and protection from enemies who seek to devour them. The psalm alternates between descriptions of danger and declarations of trust, culminating in a resolve to praise the moral order. The psalm concludes with the affirmation that the moral order's steadfast love is as high as the heavens and its faithfulness reaches to the clouds.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: Do Not Destroy. Of David. A Miktam, when he fled from Saul, in the cave.****

****1**** Be merciful to me, O God, be merciful to me, for in you my soul takes refuge; in the shadow of your wings I will take refuge, until the destroying storms pass by. ****2**** I cry to God Most High, to God who fulfills his purpose for me. ****3**** He will send from heaven and save me; he will put to shame those who trample on me. Selah God will send forth his steadfast love and his faithfulness. ****4**** I lie down among lions that greedily devour human prey; their teeth are spears and arrows, their tongues sharp swords. ****5**** Be exalted, O God, above the heavens. Let your glory be over all the earth. ****6**** They set a net for my steps; my soul was bowed down. They dug a pit in my path, but they have fallen into it themselves. Selah ****7**** My heart is steadfast, O God, my heart is steadfast. I will sing and make melody. ****8**** Awake, my soul! Awake, O harp and lyre! I will awake the dawn. ****9**** I will give thanks to you, O Lord, among the peoples; I will sing praises to you among the nations. ****10**** For your steadfast love is as high as the heavens; your faithfulness extends to the clouds. ****11**** Be exalted, O God, above the heavens. Let your glory be over all the earth.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                      | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                        |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: Taking Refuge in the Moral Order****              | The psalmist takes shelter in the moral order in times of danger. The moral order is a refuge like the shadow of wings. |
| ****Principle 2: The Experience of Being Surrounded by Enemies**** | The psalmist describes being in the midst of danger—lions, nets, pits. The faithful may face grave threats.             |
| ****Principle 3: The Resolve to Praise Despite Danger****          | The psalmist declares a steadfast heart and resolves to sing and give thanks, even in the midst of danger.              |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 57**

#### **Principle 1: Taking Refuge in the Moral Order**

(Corollary from Psalm 57:1 itself): "In the shadow of your wings I will take refuge, until the destroying storms pass by."                                                              | The moral order is a refuge. The faithful take shelter under its protection.            |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): "Health is the greatest gift; contentment is the greatest wealth; a trusted friend is the best relative; Nirvana is the greatest bliss."         | The path of the moral order is a refuge. The well-doer finds shelter in it.             |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Abandon all varieties of dharma and surrender unto me alone. I will deliver you from all sins. Do not fear." | Surrender and trust in the moral order provide refuge and protection.                   |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                                                     | The moral order is a sufficient refuge. The faithful say, "Sufficient for us is Allah." |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the refuge of the faithful. Those who trust in the Divine find shelter and protection."                                                                                                                                                               | The Divine is a refuge for those who trust in it.                                       |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person trusts in the moral order and seeks to align with it. The one who trusts does not fear, for the moral order is a refuge."                                                                                                                         | The moral order is a refuge for those who trust in it. The noble person does not fear.  |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are a refuge for those who honour them. The person who is under the protection of the ancestors is safe."                                                                                                                                               | The ancestors are a refuge. Those who honour them find protection.                      |

#### **Principle 3: The Resolve to Praise Despite Danger**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                            |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:16: "Rabbi Tarfon said: The day is short, the work is great, the workers are lazy, the reward is great, and the Master is pressing." (Corollary from Psalm 57:7): "My heart is steadfast, O God, my heart is steadfast. I will sing and make melody." | The faithful resolve to praise the moral order, even in the midst of danger.                        |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The one who has conquered themselves rejoices in all circumstances.                                             | The well-doer rejoices even in difficulty. Praise and rejoicing are not dependent on circumstances. |

(Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 6:40): The sincere striver does not perish.                                                                         | The wise person remains steadfast and praises the moral order regardless of circumstances.          |
