(Corollary from Surah Al-Imran 3:139): "Do not weaken and do not grieve, for you will be superior if you are \[true\] believers."                       | The faithful do not weaken or grieve but remain steadfast in praise and trust.                      |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee sings praise even in the midst of suffering. The heart is steadfast because it trusts in the Divine."                                                                                                             | Praise and steadfastness are not dependent on circumstances. The faithful praise even in suffering. |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is calm and composed, even in adversity. They do not give in to despair but continue in virtue."                                                                                                                 | The noble person remains composed and steadfast in virtue, even in adversity.                       |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors honour those who praise them even in times of trouble. The person who remains steadfast and grateful is blessed."                                                                                                      | The faithful who praise the ancestors even in trouble are honoured and blessed.                     |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding taking refuge in the moral order and resolving to praise despite danger. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful take refuge in the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's image of taking shelter in the shadow of wings (Psalm 57), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is a refuge, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is a refuge, as the Gita's teaching that surrender provides refuge and protection, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is a refuge, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is a refuge for the noble person, or as the African understanding that the ancestors are a refuge, all traditions teach that ****the faithful find shelter and protection in the moral order****. This refuge is available even when human sources of safety fail.

****Second, the faithful resolve to praise despite danger.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's steadfast heart and resolve to sing and make melody (Psalm 57), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful praise even in the midst of danger, as the Buddhist teaching that the well-doer rejoices in all circumstances, as the Gita's teaching of steadfastness regardless of outcomes, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful do not weaken or grieve, as the Sikh teaching that praise is not dependent on circumstances, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person remains composed in adversity, or as the African understanding that the ancestors honour those who praise in times of trouble, all traditions teach that ****praise and steadfastness are not contingent on favourable circumstances****. The faithful resolve to praise the moral order even when surrounded by danger.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach refuge in the moral order and the resolve to praise despite danger provide a moral framework that sustains hope and prevents despair in times of crisis. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to panic or abandon their values when threatened, and more likely to find strength and hope in the moral order. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust and steadfastness****—qualities that favour long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 57, Principle 1 ("Taking refuge in the moral order") and Principle 3 ("The resolve to praise despite danger") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's refuge in the shadow of wings (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is a refuge, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is a refuge, as the Gita's teaching that surrender provides refuge (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is a refuge, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is a refuge for the noble person, and as the African understanding that the ancestors are a refuge.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning refuge in the moral order and the resolve to praise despite danger.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order is a refuge, and that the faithful resolve to praise it even in the midst of danger—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 58: The Ethics of Justice and the Condemnation of Unjust Rulers**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 58 is a psalm of imprecation—a passionate prayer for divine judgment against corrupt and unjust rulers. The psalmist accuses the rulers of dispensing injustice and weighing out violence on the earth. It condemns the wicked who are corrupt from birth, using vivid imagery of snakes and deaf adders. The psalm calls upon the moral order to break the teeth of the wicked and to bring about their swift destruction. The psalm concludes with the affirmation that the righteous will rejoice when they see justice done.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: Do Not Destroy. Of David. A Miktam.****

****1**** Do you indeed decree what is right, you gods? Do you judge people fairly? ****2**** No, in your hearts you devise wrongs; your hands deal out violence on earth. ****3**** The wicked go astray from the womb; they err from their birth, speaking lies. ****4**** They have venom like the venom of a serpent, like the deaf adder that stops its ear, ****5**** so that it does not hear the voice of charmers or of the cunning enchanter. ****6**** O God, break the teeth in their mouths; tear out the fangs of the young lions, O LORD! ****7**** Let them vanish like water that runs away; like grass let them be trodden down and wither. ****8**** Let them be like the snail that dissolves into slime; like the untimely birth that never sees the sun. ****9**** Sooner than your pots can feel the heat of thorns, whether green or ablaze, may he sweep them away! ****10**** The righteous will rejoice when they see vengeance done; they will bathe their feet in the blood of the wicked. ****11**** People will say, "Surely there is a reward for the righteous; surely there is a God who judges on earth."

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                 | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                     |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Condemnation of Unjust Rulers****        | The psalmist accuses rulers of dispensing injustice and violence. They are corrupt and unresponsive to correction.   |
| ****Principle 2: The Call for Divine Judgment****             | The psalmist calls upon the moral order to break the power of the wicked and to bring about their swift destruction. |
| ****Principle 3: The Confidence that Justice Will Prevail**** | The psalmist trusts that the moral order will bring justice, and the righteous will rejoice when they see it.        |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 58**

#### **Principle 1: The Condemnation of Unjust Rulers**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                             |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 1:18: "Rabban Shimon ben Gamliel said: On three things the world stands: on justice, on truth, and on peace." (Corollary from Proverbs 29:2): "When the righteous are in authority, the people rejoice; but when the wicked rule, the people groan."                                                                           | Unjust rulers are condemned. Justice and truth are the foundations of the world.                     |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Cakkavatti Sihanada Sutta): The ideal ruler rules with righteousness; unjust rulers bring suffering upon themselves and their people. | Unjust rulers will ultimately suffer for their deeds. Righteous rule is the ideal.                   |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 16:18–20: "Given over to egoism, power, arrogance, lust, and anger, the demonic hate Me... I hurl these haters, cruel and the vilest among men, into demonic wombs in the cycle of birth and death." (Corollary from Manusmriti 7.2): The king must rule with justice and dharma; unjust rulers face destruction.            | Unjust rulers who act with arrogance and cruelty will face destruction.                              |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Ma'idah (5:32): "Whoever kills a soul... it is as if he has killed all mankind. And whoever saves a life, it is as if he has saved all mankind." (Corollary from Hadith): "The best of rulers is he who is just; the worst is he who is unjust."                                                                          | Unjust rulers are condemned. Justice is a mark of the best rulers.                                   |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true ruler is one who rules with justice, truth, and humility. They do not oppress the weak but protect the vulnerable. Unjust rulers are condemned."                                                                                                                                                 | Unjust rulers are condemned. The true ruler rules with justice and protects the vulnerable.          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble ruler governs by virtue, not by force. They are just, humble, and truthful. The unjust ruler brings ruin upon themselves and their people."                                                                                                                                                    | Unjust rulers bring ruin upon themselves. The noble ruler governs by virtue.                         |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The chief must rule with justice, truth, and humility, or the ancestors will withdraw their protection. An unjust ruler brings calamity upon the community."                                                                                                                                                | Unjust rulers face the withdrawal of ancestral protection. Justice is essential for legitimate rule. |

#### **Principle 3: The Confidence that Justice Will Prevail**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                             |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 58:11): "People will say, 'Surely there is a reward for the righteous; surely there is a God who judges on earth.'"                                                                                                                               | The faithful trust that the moral order will bring justice and reward the righteous. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | Justice will ultimately prevail. The wicked will suffer; the good will be rewarded.  |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                                                                                                                                    | The moral order will intervene to restore justice and destroy the wicked.            |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:15): "Allah will throw back their mockery on them and leave them in their transgression, wandering blindly." (Corollary from Surah Al-Mu'minun 23:116): "He who conceives mischief, his mischief falls upon him."                                                                                                                                        | The wicked will be repaid for their deeds. Justice will prevail.                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "Those who plant thorns do not harvest flowers. The evil-doer reaps what they have sown. The wicked may triumph for a time, but the righteous will endure."                                                                                                                                                                                          | The wicked will reap what they sow. Justice will prevail.                            |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The person who harms others will eventually be harmed by their own actions. The moral order is not blind; it returns upon the perpetrator what they have done."                                                                                                                                                                                    | Justice will prevail. Harm returns to the one who causes it.                         |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see all; they are not deceived. The one who wishes harm upon another will find that harm comes to their own household. The moral order is self-regulating."                                                                                                                                                                             | The ancestors ensure that justice prevails. Harm returns to the one who causes it.   |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the condemnation of unjust rulers and the confidence that justice will prevail. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, unjust rulers are condemned.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's accusation against rulers who dispense injustice and violence (Psalm 58), as the Proverb's teaching that the people groan when the wicked rule (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that unjust rulers bring suffering upon themselves, as the Gita's teaching that arrogant and cruel rulers face destruction, as the Qur'anic teaching that unjust rulers are the worst, as the Sikh teaching that the true ruler rules with justice, as the Confucian teaching that the unjust ruler brings ruin, or as the African teaching that unjust rulers face the withdrawal of ancestral protection, all traditions teach that ****unjust rule is condemned by the moral order****. Those who rule without justice and truth will ultimately face consequences.

****Second, the faithful trust that justice will prevail.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's confidence that the righteous will be rewarded and that God judges on earth (Psalm 58), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order will bring justice, as the Buddhist teaching that the good will be rewarded and the wicked will suffer, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order manifests to destroy the wicked, as the Qur'anic teaching that mischief falls upon the one who conceives it, as the Sikh teaching that the wicked reap what they sow, as the Confucian teaching that harm returns to the one who causes it, or as the African teaching that the ancestors ensure justice prevails, all traditions teach that ****the faithful can trust that the moral order will ultimately bring justice****. Injustice may prevail for a time, but it will not have the final word.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that condemn unjust rulers and affirm the certainty of justice provide a moral framework that holds rulers accountable and sustains hope for the oppressed. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as rulers are constrained by moral accountability and the oppressed are less likely to despair. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****accountability for rulers and hope for the oppressed****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 58, Principle 1 ("The condemnation of unjust rulers") and Principle 3 ("The confidence that justice will prevail") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's accusation against unjust rulers (Psalm), as the Proverb's teaching that the people groan when the wicked rule (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that unjust rulers bring suffering upon themselves, as the Gita's teaching that arrogant rulers face destruction (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the worst rulers are unjust, as the Sikh teaching that the true ruler rules with justice, as the Confucian teaching that the unjust ruler brings ruin, and as the African teaching that unjust rulers face the withdrawal of ancestral protection.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the condemnation of unjust rulers and the certainty of justice.**** The ethical lesson—that unjust rulers are condemned and that justice will prevail—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 59: The Ethics of Trust in the Face of Persecution**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 59 is a psalm of lament and trust, attributed to David when Saul sent men to watch his house in order to kill him. The psalmist cries out for deliverance from bloodthirsty enemies who lie in wait. The psalm describes the enemies as fierce, prowling like dogs, and snarling. The psalmist expresses confidence that the moral order will intervene, bringing down the wicked and protecting the faithful. The psalm concludes with declarations of trust and praise.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: Do Not Destroy. Of David. A Miktam, when Saul sent men to watch his house in order to kill him.****

****1**** Deliver me from my enemies, O my God; protect me from those who rise up against me. ****2**** Deliver me from those who work evil; from the bloodthirsty save me. ****3**** Even now they lie in wait for my life; the mighty stir up strife against me. For no transgression or sin of mine, O LORD, ****4**** for no fault of mine, they run and make ready. Rouse yourself, come to my help and see! ****5**** You, LORD God of hosts, are God of Israel. Awake to punish all the nations; do not be gracious to any who treacherously plot evil. Selah ****6**** Each evening they come back, howling like dogs, and prowl about the city. ****7**** There they are, bellowing with their mouths, with sharp words on their lips— for "Who," they think, "will hear us?" ****8**** But you laugh at them, O LORD; you hold all the nations in derision. ****9**** O my Strength, I will watch for you; for you, O God, are my fortress. ****10**** My God in his steadfast love will meet me; my God will let me look in triumph on my enemies. ****11**** Do not kill them, or my people may forget; make them totter by your power, and bring them down, O Lord, our shield. ****12**** For the sin of their mouths, the words of their lips, let them be trapped in their pride. For the cursing and lies that they utter, ****13**** consume them in wrath; consume them until they are no more. Then it will be known to the ends of the earth that God rules over Jacob. Selah ****14**** Each evening they come back, howling like dogs, and prowl about the city. ****15**** They wander about for food, and growl if they are not satisfied. ****16**** But I will sing of your might; I will sing aloud of your steadfast love in the morning. For you have been a fortress for me and a refuge in the day of my distress. ****17**** O my Strength, I will sing praises to you, for you, O God, are my fortress, the God who shows me steadfast love.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                   | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                         |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Being Hunted****             | The psalmist describes being pursued by bloodthirsty enemies who lie in wait and prowl like dogs.                        |
| ****Principle 2: The Plea for Deliverance****                   | The psalmist calls upon the moral order to save them from those who work evil.                                           |
| ****Principle 3: Confidence in the Moral Order's Protection**** | The psalmist declares that the moral order is their fortress and refuge, and they resolve to sing of its steadfast love. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 59**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of Being Hunted**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                  |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Psalm 59 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm describes being hunted by bloodthirsty enemies.                                                                                      | The faithful may experience persecution and being hunted. This is a reality.              |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): The good may suffer while the wicked prosper.                       | The good may suffer persecution. This is part of the world.                               |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): The Pandavas are hunted and persecuted by their enemies.                               | The righteous may be hunted and persecuted. Suffering is part of the impermanent world.   |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Asr (103:1–3): "By time, indeed mankind is in loss, except for those who have believed and done righteous deeds and advised each other to truth and advised each other to patience." (Corollary from Surah Al-Baqarah 2:155–156): The faithful will be tested with persecution. | The faithful will be tested with persecution. Patience is required.                       |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the righteous may be hunted and persecuted. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                                                      | Persecution and being hunted are part of life. The faithful do not lose faith.            |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person may be poor and unrecognized, even persecuted. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                            | The noble person may face persecution. The moral order is not always immediately visible. |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the persecuted. Even when you are hunted, the ancestors know the truth. The faithful are not forgotten."                                                                                                                       | The ancestors see the suffering of the persecuted. The faithful are not forgotten.        |

#### **Principle 3: Confidence in the Moral Order's Protection**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                        |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 59:16–17): "But I will sing of your might... for you have been a fortress for me."                            | The moral order is a fortress and refuge. The faithful trust in its protection. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order provides refuge.                                                 | The path of the moral order is a refuge. The faithful trust in it.              |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                 | The moral order preserves the devoted. The faithful trust in its protection.    |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'" | The moral order is sufficient as a refuge. The faithful trust in it.            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the fortress of the faithful. Those who trust in the Divine find protection and refuge."                                                                                                          | The Divine is a fortress and refuge. The faithful trust in it.                  |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person trusts in the moral order and seeks to align with it. The one who trusts does not fear, for the moral order is a refuge."                                                                     | The moral order is a refuge for those who trust in it.                          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are a refuge for those who honour them. The person who is under the protection of the ancestors is safe."                                                                                           | The ancestors are a refuge for those who honour them.                           |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the experience of being hunted and confidence in the moral order's protection. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful may experience persecution and being hunted.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's description of being pursued by bloodthirsty enemies (Psalm 59), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful may be persecuted, as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer while the wicked prosper, as the Pandavas' persecution (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that persecution is part of life, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may face persecution, or as the African understanding that the faithful may be hunted but are not forgotten, all traditions teach that ****the faithful are not immune to persecution****. Being hunted is a reality that the faithful may face.

****Second, confidence in the moral order's protection sustains the faithful.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is their fortress and refuge (Psalm 59), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is a fortress, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is a refuge, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is a fortress, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is a refuge for the noble person, or as the African understanding that the ancestors are a refuge, all traditions teach that ****the faithful find protection and refuge in the moral order****. Trust in the moral order sustains them even when they are hunted.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge the reality of persecution while teaching confidence in the moral order's protection provide a moral framework that sustains hope and prevents despair in the face of persecution. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to abandon their faith in times of persecution and more likely to trust in the moral order's ultimate justice. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust and resilience****—qualities that favour long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 59, Principle 1 ("The experience of being hunted") and Principle 3 ("Confidence in the moral order's protection") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's description of being hunted (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful may be persecuted, as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer, as the Pandavas' persecution (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that persecution is part of life, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may face persecution, and as the African understanding that the faithful may be hunted but are not forgotten.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the experience of persecution and confidence in the moral order's protection.**** The ethical lesson—that the faithful may be hunted, but they trust in the moral order as their refuge and fortress—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 60: The Ethics of National Defeat and Trust in Deliverance**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 60 is a national lament, attributed to David, composed during a time of military defeat. The psalmist acknowledges that the moral order has rejected and scattered the people, but also expresses confidence that victory will come. The psalm includes a oracle of victory, declaring that the moral order will subdue enemies and divide territories. The psalm concludes with a plea for help against enemies, affirming that human help is worthless and that victory comes from the moral order.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: according to the Lily of the Covenant. A Miktam of David; for instruction; when he struggled with Aram-naharaim and with Aram-zobah, and when Joab on his return killed twelve thousand Edomites in the Valley of Salt.****

****1**** O God, you have rejected us, broken our defenses; you have been angry; now restore us! ****2**** You have caused the land to quake; you have torn it open; repair the cracks in it, for it is tottering. ****3**** You have made your people suffer hard things; you have given us wine to drink that made us reel. ****4**** You have set up a banner for those who fear you, to rally to it out of bowshot. Selah ****5**** Give victory with your right hand, and answer us, so that those whom you love may be rescued. ****6**** God has promised in his sanctuary: "With exultation I will divide up Shechem, and portion out the Vale of Succoth. ****7**** Gilead is mine, and Manasseh is mine; Ephraim is my helmet; Judah is my scepter. ****8**** Moab is my washbasin; on Edom I hurl my shoe; over Philistia I shout in triumph." ****9**** Who will bring me to the fortified city? Who will lead me to Edom? ****10**** Have you not rejected us, O God? You do not go out, O God, with our armies. ****11**** O grant us help against the foe, for human help is worthless. ****12**** With God we shall do valiantly; it is he who will tread down our foes.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                     | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                                  |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of National Defeat****            | The psalmist acknowledges that the moral order has rejected and scattered the people. National defeat is a reality.                               |
| ****Principle 2: The Acknowledgment of Divine Discipline****      | The psalmist accepts that the defeat is a form of discipline from the moral order. Suffering is not necessarily punishment but may be discipline. |
| ****Principle 3: The Affirmation that Human Help Is Worthless**** | The psalmist declares that human help is worthless; victory comes from the moral order alone.                                                     |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 60**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of National Defeat**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                   |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Lamentations): The book of Lamentations is a national lament over the destruction of Jerusalem.                                                                             | Nations may experience defeat and destruction. This is a reality of history.                               |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): Good communities may also experience suffering.      | Communities, like individuals, may experience defeat and suffering. This is part of the impermanent world. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): The Pandavas experience exile and defeat before their eventual victory. | The righteous community may experience defeat. Suffering is part of the impermanent world.                 |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:155–156): "And We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger and loss of wealth and lives and fruits, but give good tidings to the patient."                                                                                              | Communities will be tested with defeat and loss. Patience is required.                                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the community of the faithful may experience defeat. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                              | Communities may experience defeat. The faithful do not lose faith.                                         |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble community may be poor and unrecognized, even defeated. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                            | The virtuous community may experience defeat. The moral order is not always immediately evident.           |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the community. Even when the community faces defeat, the ancestors know the truth. The suffering community is not forgotten."                                                                                   | Communities may face defeat, but the ancestors see their suffering. They are not forgotten.                |

#### **Principle 3: The Affirmation that Human Help Is Worthless**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                      |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 60:11): "O grant us help against the foe, for human help is worthless."                                                                                                  | Human help is ultimately unreliable. True help comes from the moral order.                                    |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 103: "Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, yet he who conquers himself is the greatest warrior." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 201): "Victory breeds hatred; the defeated live in pain. The peaceful live happily, having renounced victory and defeat." | Human military victory is not the ultimate good. True victory comes from self-conquest and trust in the path. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:47: "You have the right to perform your prescribed duties, but you are not entitled to the fruits of your actions." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Abandon all varieties of dharma and surrender unto me alone. I will deliver you from all sins. Do not fear."                     | Human effort is important, but ultimate deliverance comes from the moral order.                               |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Anfal (8:17): "You did not kill them, but Allah killed them. And you did not throw when you threw, but Allah threw." (Corollary from Surah Al-Imran 3:160): "If Allah should aid you, no one can overcome you; but if He should forsake you, who is there that can aid you after Him?"     | Human help and military might are ultimately dependent on the moral order. True victory comes from it.        |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true warrior is not the one who fights with weapons but the one who fights with truth. Trust in the Divine, not in the sword. The sword may break, but truth endures."                                                                                                 | Human weapons and help are unreliable. Trust in the Divine is the true source of victory.                     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is not one who wins by force but one who wins by virtue. The ruler who trusts in military might will eventually fall; the ruler who trusts in virtue will endure."                                                                                       | Human force is unreliable. Virtue and trust in the moral order are the true sources of victory.               |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors protect those who walk rightly, not those who carry the largest spear. Trust in the ancestors and in right living, not in weapons. Weapons can be broken, but the ancestors' protection is eternal."                                                           | Human weapons and help are unreliable. Trust in the ancestors and right living brings true victory.           |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the experience of national defeat and the affirmation that human help is worthless. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, nations and communities may experience defeat and suffering.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's acknowledgment that the moral order has rejected and scattered the people (Psalm 60), as the book of Lamentations' lament over Jerusalem's destruction (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that communities may suffer, as the Pandavas' exile and defeat (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that communities will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that communities may face defeat, as the Confucian recognition that virtuous communities may be defeated, or as the African understanding that communities may face defeat but are not forgotten, all traditions teach that ****national defeat is a reality****. No nation or community is immune to suffering and defeat.

****Second, human help is worthless; true victory comes from the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that human help is worthless (Psalm 60), as the Jewish teaching that true help comes from the moral order, as the Buddhist teaching that self-conquest is greater than military victory, as the Gita's teaching that surrender to the moral order brings deliverance, as the Qur'anic teaching that victory comes from the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that truth endures while swords break, as the Confucian teaching that virtue endures while force fails, or as the African teaching that the ancestors' protection is eternal, all traditions teach that ****human power and help are ultimately unreliable****. True victory and deliverance come from the moral order.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge the reality of national defeat while teaching that human help is worthless provide a moral framework that discourages over-reliance on military might and encourages trust in the moral order. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to engage in destructive militarism and more likely to cultivate the virtues that sustain long-term survival. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust in the moral order and caution against militarism****—qualities that favour long-term stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 60, Principle 1 ("The experience of national defeat") and Principle 3 ("Human help is worthless") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's acknowledgment of defeat (Psalm), as the book of Lamentations' lament (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that communities may suffer, as the Pandavas' exile (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that communities will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that communities may face defeat, as the Confucian recognition that virtuous communities may be defeated, and as the African understanding that communities may face defeat but are not forgotten.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the experience of national defeat and the affirmation that human help is worthless.**** The ethical lesson—that nations may be defeated, but true victory comes from the moral order, not from human power—transcends its religious packaging.

Chapter 7 — Psalms 61–70\*\*

## **Psalm 61: The Ethics of Spiritual Distance and Longing for Refuge**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 61 is a psalm of trust and prayer for protection, attributed to David. The psalmist cries out from the ends of the earth, feeling faint and distant from the moral order. The psalmist longs for shelter in the "rock that is higher than I" and seeks refuge in the moral order's presence. The psalm concludes with a vow to praise and a confident affirmation that the moral order will preserve the king and the faithful.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: with stringed instruments. Of David.****

****1**** Hear my cry, O God; listen to my prayer. ****2**** From the end of the earth I call to you, when my heart is faint. Lead me to the rock that is higher than I; ****3**** for you are my refuge, a strong tower against the enemy. ****4**** Let me abide in your tent forever, find refuge under the shelter of your wings. Selah ****5**** For you, O God, have heard my vows; you have given me the heritage of those who fear your name. ****6**** Prolong the life of the king; may his years endure to all generations! ****7**** May he be enthroned forever before God; appoint steadfast love and faithfulness to watch over him! ****8**** So I will sing praises to your name forever, as I pay my vows day after day.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                  | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                                  |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Distance and Faintness****  | The psalmist feels faint and distant from the moral order, crying out from the ends of the earth. The faithful may experience spiritual distance. |
| ****Principle 2: The Longing for Refuge in the Moral Order**** | The psalmist longs for shelter in the rock that is higher than themselves. The moral order is a refuge and strong tower.                          |
| ****Principle 3: The Vow to Praise****                         | The psalmist vows to sing praises and fulfill their vows. Gratitude and praise are the responses to deliverance.                                  |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 61**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of Distance and Faintness**
