(Corollary from Psalm 66:1–4): "Make a joyful noise to God, all the earth... All the earth worships you."                                                                             | All peoples are called to praise the moral order. Praise is a universal duty.              |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order is open to all.                                                                                                    | All who follow the path rejoice and praise. The moral order is worthy of universal praise. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:11: "As people approach me, so I receive them. All paths, Arjuna, lead to me." (Corollary from Rig Veda 1.1.1): The moral order is invoked by all peoples.                                                                                                                         | All peoples, through their various paths, ultimately praise the moral order.               |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:115): "To Allah belongs the east and the west. So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah." (Corollary from Surah Al-Hujurat 49:13): "O mankind, indeed We have created you from male and female and made you peoples and tribes that you may know one another." | The moral order is present everywhere and is praised by all peoples.                       |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The One Divine is praised by all peoples. All are children of the One."                                                                                                                                                                                           | The Divine is praised by all peoples. All are included in this praise.                     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is universal and is honoured by all peoples who seek virtue."                                                                                                                                                                                    | The moral order is universal and worthy of universal honour.                               |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "All peoples praise the ancestors in their own way. The moral order is honoured by all communities."                                                                                                                                                                 | All peoples honour the moral order through their ancestors. Universal praise is a reality. |

#### **Principle 3: The Confidence that the Moral Order Hears Prayer**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                       |
| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 66:19): "But truly God has listened; he has given heed to the words of my prayer."                                                            | The moral order listens to the prayers of the faithful.                                        |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 165: "Evil is done by oneself; by oneself is one defiled. Evil is left undone by oneself; by oneself is one purified. Purity and impurity depend on oneself." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 1): The mind precedes all states; prayer and intention matter. | The moral order responds to the purified mind and sincere intentions.                          |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                                                            | The moral order responds to the devoted. The faithful can trust that their prayers are heard.  |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me."                                                                                                           | The moral order is near and responds to those who call. The faithful can trust in this.        |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine hears the prayers of the faithful. The one who calls with a sincere heart is answered."                                                                                                                                              | The Divine hears and answers sincere prayers.                                                  |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order responds to the virtuous. The prayers of the noble person are heard."                                                                                                                                                          | The moral order responds to the virtuous. The faithful can trust that their prayers are heard. |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors hear the prayers of those who honour them. The faithful are not ignored."                                                                                                                                                           | The ancestors hear the prayers of the faithful. They are not ignored.                          |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the call to universal praise and the confidence that the moral order hears prayer. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the call to universal praise.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's call to all the earth to make a joyful noise (Psalm 66), as the Jewish teaching that all the earth worships, as the Buddhist teaching that all who follow the path rejoice, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order belongs to all peoples, as the Sikh teaching that all peoples praise the One, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is universal, or as the African understanding that all peoples honour the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****praise of the moral order is a universal duty****. It is not the exclusive possession of one nation or tradition.

****Second, the confidence that the moral order hears prayer.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order has listened to their prayer (Psalm 66), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order listens to the faithful, as the Buddhist teaching that the purified mind is heard, as the Gita's teaching that the devoted are preserved, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds, as the Sikh teaching that sincere prayers are heard, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order responds to the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors hear the prayers of the faithful, all traditions teach that ****the faithful can trust that their prayers are heard****. The moral order is not distant or indifferent.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach universal praise and the confidence that prayers are heard provide a moral framework that encourages worship and trust. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to become insular or despairing, and more likely to maintain a sense of connection to the moral order. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****universalism and trust****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 66, Principle 1 ("The call to universal praise") and Principle 3 ("The confidence that the moral order hears prayer") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the call to all the earth to make a joyful noise (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that all the earth worships, as the Buddhist teaching that all who follow the path rejoice, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order belongs to all peoples, as the Sikh teaching that all peoples praise the One, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is universal, and as the African teaching that all peoples honour the ancestors.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the call to universal praise and the confidence that the moral order hears prayer.**** The ethical lesson—that praise is a universal duty and that the faithful can trust that their prayers are heard—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 67: The Ethics of Blessing and Its Missional Purpose**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 67 is a psalm of blessing and universal praise, calling upon the moral order to be gracious to the people and to bless them so that God's ways may be known among all nations. The psalm expresses the hope that all peoples will praise the moral order and that the earth will yield its harvest. It is a prayer for blessing that is not selfish but missional—blessing is sought so that it may be a witness to all the nations.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: with stringed instruments. A Psalm. A Song.****

****1**** May God be gracious to us and bless us and make his face to shine upon us, Selah ****2**** that your way may be known upon earth, your saving power among all nations. ****3**** Let the peoples praise you, O God; let all the peoples praise you. ****4**** Let the nations be glad and sing for joy, for you judge the peoples with equity and guide the nations upon earth. Selah ****5**** Let the peoples praise you, O God; let all the peoples praise you. ****6**** The earth has yielded its increase; God, our God, has blessed us. ****7**** May God continue to bless us; let all the ends of the earth revere him.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                          | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                         |
| ------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Prayer for Blessing****           | The psalmist asks the moral order to be gracious and to bless. Receiving blessing is a legitimate prayer.                                |
| ****Principle 2: The Missional Purpose of Blessing**** | Blessing is sought so that the moral order's ways may be known among all nations. Blessing is not for selfish enjoyment but for witness. |
| ****Principle 3: The Call for Universal Praise****     | The psalmist expresses the hope that all peoples will praise the moral order and that the earth will yield its harvest.                  |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 67**

#### **Principle 1: The Prayer for Blessing**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                              |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Numbers 6:24–26): "The LORD bless you and keep you; the LORD make his face shine upon you and be gracious to you; the LORD lift up his countenance upon you and give you peace." | Praying for blessing is a legitimate and traditional practice.        |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Metta Sutta): "May all beings be happy and secure; may all beings be happy-minded."                                                                                               | The faithful pray for the well-being and blessing of all beings.      |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                                                                              | The faithful pray for blessing and preservation from the moral order. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me." (Corollary from Surah Al-Fatihah 1:6–7): "Guide us to the straight path... not of those who have gone astray."                         | The faithful pray for guidance and blessing.                          |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee prays for the blessings of the Divine. The one who seeks blessing with a sincere heart is blessed."                                                                                                                                                         | Praying for blessing is a mark of the true devotee.                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person prays for the blessing of the moral order and for the well-being of the community."                                                                                                                                                                        | Praying for blessing for the community is a mark of the noble person. |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The faithful pray to the ancestors for blessing upon the family, the community, and the land."                                                                                                                                                                                 | Praying for blessing from the ancestors is a sacred duty.             |

#### **Principle 2: The Missional Purpose of Blessing**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                 |
| --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 1:12: "Hillel said: That which is hateful to you, do not do to your neighbour. That is the whole Torah; the rest is commentary. Go and learn it." (Corollary from Genesis 12:2–3): "I will make you a great nation... and you shall be a blessing... and in you all the families of the earth shall be blessed." | Blessing is not for selfish enjoyment but to be a blessing to all peoples.                               |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from the Metta Sutta): The cultivation of goodwill toward all beings is a blessing to all.                                                                                                            | The blessing of the path is to be shared with all beings, not hoarded.                                   |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 3:10–13: "In the beginning, the Creator created humankind together with sacrifice and said: 'By this shall you multiply... Foster the gods with this, and let them foster you.'"                                                                                                                               | Blessing is received through sacrifice and is part of a cycle of giving and receiving that benefits all. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:143): "And thus we have made you a middle community that you may be witnesses to the people." (Corollary from Surah Al-Imran 3:110): "You are the best nation produced \[as an example\] for mankind."                                                                                           | The community's blessing is for the purpose of being a witness to all peoples.                           |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee prays for blessing not for themselves alone but so that all may benefit. The Divine's blessing is for all."                                                                                                                                                                | Blessing is for the benefit of all, not just the individual.                                             |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person receives blessing so that they may extend it to others. Virtue is not for private gain."                                                                                                                                                                                  | Blessing is received so that it may be extended to others.                                               |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors bless the community so that the community may prosper and be a blessing to others. Blessing is not for one family alone."                                                                                                                                                       | Blessing is for the community and is intended to benefit others.                                         |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the prayer for blessing and the missional purpose of blessing. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful pray for blessing.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's request that the moral order be gracious and bless (Psalm 67), as the priestly blessing (Judaism), as the Buddhist prayer for the well-being of all beings, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order gives what is lacking, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order responds to those who call, as the Sikh teaching that the true devotee prays for blessing, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person prays for the community, or as the African understanding that the faithful pray to the ancestors for blessing, all traditions teach that ****praying for blessing is a legitimate and encouraged practice****.

****Second, blessing has a missional purpose.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's desire that God's ways may be known among all nations (Psalm 67), as the Abrahamic promise to be a blessing to all families of the earth (Judaism), as the Buddhist cultivation of goodwill toward all beings, as the Gita's teaching that blessing is part of a cycle of giving and receiving that benefits all, as the Qur'anic teaching that the community is a witness to the peoples, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine's blessing is for all, as the Confucian teaching that virtue is not for private gain, or as the African understanding that blessing is for the community and is intended to benefit others, all traditions teach that ****blessing is not for selfish enjoyment but for the purpose of benefiting others and making the moral order known****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the prayer for blessing and its missional purpose provide a moral framework that encourages generosity and a sense of responsibility toward others. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to hoard blessing and more likely to share it with others. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****generosity and a sense of mission****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 67, Principle 1 ("The prayer for blessing") and Principle 2 ("The missional purpose of blessing") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's prayer for blessing and its purpose of making God's ways known (Psalm), as the priestly blessing and the Abrahamic promise (Judaism), as the Buddhist prayer for the well-being of all beings, as the Gita's teaching on sacrifice and giving (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the community is a witness to the peoples, as the Sikh teaching that blessing is for all, as the Confucian teaching that virtue is not for private gain, and as the African teaching that blessing is for the community.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the prayer for blessing and its missional purpose.**** The ethical lesson—that blessing is to be received and shared so that the moral order may be known to all—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 68: The Ethics of Protection of the Weak and Provision for the Needy**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 68 is a triumphal psalm of praise, celebrating the moral order as the protector of the weak and the victorious sovereign over all nations. It opens with a call for the enemies to be scattered and the righteous to rejoice. The psalm describes the moral order's power in battle, its care for the orphans and widows, its provision for the needy, and its majestic presence. It concludes with a call to the kingdoms of the earth to sing praise.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader. Of David. A Psalm. A Song.****

****1**** Let God rise up, let his enemies be scattered; let those who hate him flee before him. ****2**** As smoke is driven away, so drive them away; as wax melts before the fire, let the wicked perish before God. ****3**** But let the righteous be joyful; let them exult before God; let them be jubilant with joy. ****4**** Sing to God, sing praises to his name; lift up a song to him who rides upon the clouds— his name is the LORD— be exultant before him. ****5**** Father of orphans and protector of widows is God in his holy habitation. ****6**** God gives the desolate a home to live in; he leads out the prisoners to prosperity, but the rebellious live in a parched land. ****7**** O God, when you went out before your people, when you marched through the wilderness, Selah ****8**** the earth quaked, the heavens poured down rain at the presence of God; the Sinai God quaked at the presence of God, the God of Israel. ****9**** Rain in abundance, O God, you showered abroad; you restored your heritage when it languished; ****10**** your flock found a dwelling in it; in your goodness, O God, you provided for the needy. ****11**** The Lord gives the command; great is the company of those who bore the tidings: ****12**** "The kings of the armies—they flee, they flee!" The women at home divide the spoil, ****13**** though they stay among the sheepfolds— the wings of a dove covered with silver, its pinions with green gold. ****14**** When the Almighty scattered kings there, snow fell on Zalmon. ****15**** O mighty mountain, mountain of Bashan; O many-peaked mountain, mountain of Bashan! ****16**** Why do you look with envy, O many-peaked mountain, at the mount that God desired for his abode, where the LORD will reside forever? ****17**** With mighty chariotry, twice ten thousand, thousands upon thousands, the Lord came from Sinai into the holy place. ****18**** You ascended the high mount, leading captives in your train and receiving gifts from people, even from those who rebel against the LORD God's abiding there. ****19**** Blessed be the Lord, who daily bears us up; God is our salvation. Selah ****20**** Our God is a God of salvation, and to GOD, the Lord, belongs escape from death. ****21**** But God will shatter the heads of his enemies, the hairy crown of those who walk in their guilty ways. ****22**** The Lord said, "I will bring them back from Bashan, I will bring them back from the depths of the sea, ****23**** so that you may bathe your feet in blood, so that the tongues of your dogs may have their portion from the foe." ****24**** Your solemn processions are seen, O God, the processions of my God, my King, into the sanctuary— ****25**** the singers in front, the musicians last, between them girls playing tambourines: ****26**** "Bless God in the great congregation, the LORD, O you who are of Israel's fountain!" ****27**** There is Benjamin, the least of them, in the lead, the princes of Judah in a body, the princes of Zebulun, the princes of Naphtali. ****28**** Summon your might, O God; show your strength, O God, you who have wrought for us. ****29**** Because of your temple at Jerusalem kings bear gifts to you. ****30**** Rebuke the wild beasts that live among the reeds, the herd of bulls with the calves of the peoples. Trample under foot those who lust after tribute; scatter the peoples who delight in war. ****31**** Let bronze be brought from Egypt; let Ethiopia hasten to stretch out its hands to God. ****32**** Sing to God, O kingdoms of the earth; sing praises to the Lord, Selah ****33**** O rider in the heavens, the ancient heavens; listen, he sends out his voice, his mighty voice. ****34**** Ascribe power to God, whose majesty is over Israel; and whose power is in the skies. ****35**** Awesome is God in his sanctuary, the God of Israel; he gives power and strength to his people. Blessed be God!

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                      | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                     |
| -------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Protection of the Weak****    | The moral order is the father of orphans and protector of widows. The vulnerable are under its care. |
| ****Principle 2: The Provision for the Needy****   | The moral order provides for the needy and gives the desolate a home.                                |
| ****Principle 3: The Scattering of the Enemies**** | The enemies of the moral order will be scattered; the righteous will rejoice.                        |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 68**

#### **Principle 1: The Protection of the Weak**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                         |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Exodus 22:22–24): "You shall not afflict any widow or orphan. If you afflict them, and they cry out to Me, I will surely hear their cry."                                        | The moral order protects the vulnerable—widows, orphans, and strangers.                          |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 129: "All tremble at violence; all fear death. Comparing oneself to others, one should not kill nor cause to kill." (Corollary from the Metta Sutta): "May all beings be happy and secure; may all beings be happy-minded."                                                                 | Compassion for the vulnerable is a fundamental virtue. The wise person protects all beings.      |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Manusmriti 4.201): "The king is obliged to protect the weak and the vulnerable."                                                             | Protection of the weak is a duty of the moral order and of those in power.                       |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Ma'un (107:1–7): "Have you seen the one who denies the judgment? That is the one who repulses the orphan and does not encourage the feeding of the poor. So woe to those who pray but are heedless of their prayer—those who make a show of piety and withhold even small acts of kindness." | Denial of care to the orphan and the poor is condemned. The moral order protects the vulnerable. |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine hears the cry of the poor. The true devotee is one who shares with the needy and protects the vulnerable. Do not take advantage of those who cannot defend themselves."                                                                                           | The Divine protects the vulnerable. The true devotee shares with the needy.                      |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who, when in power, ensures that the old are not cast aside, that the young are protected, and that the poor are not neglected."                                                                                                                    | Protection of the vulnerable is a mark of good governance and noble character.                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "In many African societies, the orphan, the widow, and the stranger are under the protection of the community. The ancestors watch over the vulnerable. To harm them is to bring the anger of the ancestors upon oneself."                                                      | The vulnerable are under the protection of the community and the ancestors.                      |

#### **Principle 2: The Provision for the Needy**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                 | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                      |
| --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 68:10): "In your goodness, O God, you provided for the needy."                      | The moral order provides for the needy.                                       |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): "Health is the greatest gift; contentment is the greatest wealth." | The path of the moral order provides well-being for all who follow it.        |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 3:10–13: "In the beginning, the Creator created humankind together with sacrifice and said: 'By this shall you multiply... Foster the gods with this, and let them foster you.'"                         | The moral order provides through the cycle of sacrifice and blessing.         |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:164): "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth... and the rain which Allah sends down from the sky, giving life thereby to the earth after its lifelessness..."               | The moral order provides for creation through rain, growth, and abundance.    |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine provides for all creation. The earth is watered, crops grow, and all beings are nourished by the Divine's bounty."                                                         | The Divine provides for all beings.                                           |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order provides for the earth and all its creatures. The seasons, the rain, and the harvest are signs of its provision."                                                    | The moral order provides for creation through the seasons, rain, and harvest. |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors provide for the community through the fertility of the land and the abundance of the harvest. The land is a gift from the ancestors."                                     | The ancestors provide for the community through the land and its abundance.   |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the protection of the weak and the provision for the needy. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order protects the vulnerable.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is the father of orphans and protector of widows (Psalm 68), as the Exodus command not to afflict widows or orphans (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching of compassion for all beings, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic condemnation of those who repulse the orphan, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine hears the cry of the poor, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person protects the old, young, and poor, or as the African understanding that the ancestors watch over the vulnerable, all traditions teach that ****the vulnerable—orphans, widows, strangers, the poor—are under the special protection of the moral order****. To harm them is to offend the moral order.

****Second, the moral order provides for the needy.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's celebration that in God's goodness, God provided for the needy (Psalm 68), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order provides, as the Buddhist teaching that the path provides well-being, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order provides through the cycle of sacrifice and blessing, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order sends down rain and gives life to the earth, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine nourishes all beings, as the Confucian teaching that the seasons and harvest are signs of provision, or as the African understanding that the ancestors provide through the land, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is the ultimate source of provision for all****. The needy are not forgotten.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the protection of the weak and the provision for the needy provide a moral framework that encourages care for the vulnerable and discourages exploitation. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to neglect the poor and more likely to create systems of care and provision. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****care for the vulnerable and trust in divine provision****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 68, Principle 1 ("The protection of the weak") and Principle 2 ("The provision for the needy") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration that God is father of orphans and protector of widows (Psalm), as the Exodus command not to afflict widows or orphans (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching of compassion for all beings, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic condemnation of those who repulse the orphan, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine hears the cry of the poor, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person protects the old, young, and poor, and as the African understanding that the ancestors watch over the vulnerable.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the protection of the weak and the provision for the needy.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order protects the vulnerable and provides for the needy—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 69: The Ethics of Perseverance Through Suffering and the Cost of Devotion**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 69 is one of the most intense psalms of lament in the Psalter, attributed to David. It describes the psalmist as sinking in deep mire, weary with crying, and surrounded by enemies. The psalmist is suffering on account of their devotion to the moral order, enduring shame, reproach, and even estrangement from family. The psalm includes imprecations against the enemies and a declaration of trust in the moral order's salvation. The psalm concludes with a call to praise, affirming that the moral order hears the needy.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: according to Lilies. Of David.****

****1**** Save me, O God, for the waters have come up to my neck. ****2**** I sink in deep mire, where there is no foothold; I have come into deep waters, and the flood sweeps over me. ****3**** I am weary with my crying; my throat is parched. My eyes grow dim with waiting for my God. ****4**** More in number than the hairs of my head are those who hate me without cause; many are those who would destroy me, my enemies who accuse me falsely. What I did not steal must I now restore? ****5**** O God, you know my folly; the wrongs I have done are not hidden from you. ****6**** Do not let those who hope in you be put to shame because of me, O Lord GOD of hosts; do not let those who seek you be dishonored because of me, O God of Israel. ****7**** It is for your sake that I have borne reproach, that shame has covered my face. ****8**** I have become a stranger to my kindred, an alien to my mother's children. ****9**** It is zeal for your house that has consumed me; the insults of those who insult you have fallen on me. ****10**** When I humbled my soul with fasting, they insulted me for it. ****11**** When I made sackcloth my clothing, I became a byword to them. ****12**** I am the subject of gossip for those who sit in the gate, and the drunkards make songs about me. ****13**** But as for me, my prayer is to you, O LORD. At an acceptable time, O God, in the abundance of your steadfast love, answer me. With your faithful help ****14**** rescue me from sinking in the mire; let me be delivered from my enemies and from the deep waters. ****15**** Do not let the flood sweep over me, or the deep swallow me up, or the Pit close its mouth over me. ****16**** Answer me, O LORD, for your steadfast love is good; according to your abundant mercy, turn to me. ****17**** Do not hide your face from your servant, for I am in distress—make haste to answer me. ****18**** Draw near to me, redeem me; set me free because of my enemies. ****19**** You know the insults I receive, and my shame and dishonor; my foes are all known to you. ****20**** Insults have broken my heart, so that I am in despair. I looked for pity, but there was none; and for comforters, but I found none. ****21**** They gave me poison for food, and for my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink. ****22**** Let their table be a trap for them, a snare for their allies. ****23**** Let their eyes be darkened so that they cannot see, and make their loins tremble continually. ****24**** Pour out your indignation upon them, and let your burning anger overtake them. ****25**** May their camp be a desolation; let no one live in their tents. ****26**** For they persecute those whom you have struck and talk about the pain of those you have wounded. ****27**** Add guilt to their guilt; may they have no acquittal from you. ****28**** Let them be blotted out of the book of the living; let them not be enrolled among the righteous. ****29**** But I am lowly and in pain; let your salvation, O God, protect me. ****30**** I will praise the name of God with a song; I will magnify him with thanksgiving. ****31**** This will please the LORD more than an ox or a bull with horns and hoofs. ****32**** Let the oppressed see it and be glad; you who seek God, let your hearts revive. ****33**** For the LORD hears the needy, and does not despise his own that are in bonds. ****34**** Let heaven and earth praise him, the seas and everything that moves in them. ****35**** For God will save Zion and rebuild the cities of Judah; and his servants shall live there and possess it; ****36**** the children of his servants shall inherit it, and those who love his name shall live in it.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Deep Suffering****        | The psalmist describes sinking in deep mire, being weary with crying, and suffering shame and reproach. Suffering is a reality. |
| ****Principle 2: The Cost of Devotion to the Moral Order**** | The psalmist suffers because of their zeal for the moral order. Devotion may come at a high cost.                               |
| ****Principle 3: The Trust in Deliverance****                | The psalmist trusts that the moral order will deliver them and hears the needy.                                                 |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 69**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of Deep Suffering**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                     |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Job): The book of Job depicts a righteous man who experiences profound suffering.                                                                                                                   | The faithful may experience deep suffering. Suffering is not always a sign of moral failure. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition." | The good may suffer. Suffering is part of the human condition.                               |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): Yudhishthira experiences exile, loss, and war.                                                  | The righteous may experience deep suffering. This is part of the impermanent world.          |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:155–156): "And We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger and loss of wealth and lives and fruits, but give good tidings to the patient."                                                                                                                      | The faithful will be tested with suffering. Patience is required.                            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the righteous may experience deep suffering. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                                                              | Suffering is part of life. The faithful do not lose faith.                                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person may experience suffering. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                                                          | The noble person may suffer. The moral order is not always immediately evident.              |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the faithful. Even when you suffer, the ancestors know the truth. The faithful are not forgotten."                                                                                                                                      | The ancestors see suffering. The faithful are not forgotten.                                 |

#### **Principle 2: The Cost of Devotion to the Moral Order**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                     |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Psalm 69:9): "It is zeal for your house that has consumed me; the insults of those who insult you have fallen on me."                                                     | Devotion to the moral order may lead to suffering and reproach.              |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 103: "Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, yet he who conquers himself is the greatest warrior." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 16): The path requires self-sacrifice and discipline.                                         | Devotion to the path requires self-conquest, which may involve suffering.    |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:47: "You have the right to perform your prescribed duties, but you are not entitled to the fruits of your actions." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 3:19): Performing duty without attachment may lead to suffering.                                              | Devotion to dharma may involve suffering and attachment to results.          |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Asr (103:1–3): "By time, indeed mankind is in loss, except for those who have believed and done righteous deeds and advised each other to truth and advised each other to patience." (Corollary from Surah Al-Baqarah 2:155–156): The faithful will be tested. | Devotion to the moral order involves patience and may involve suffering.     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee may face persecution and suffering for their faith. The Sikh is taught to remain steadfast."                                                                                                                                  | Devotion to the Divine may involve persecution and suffering.                |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who remains steadfast in virtue even in hardship. The small person abandons their principles when it becomes costly."                                                                                                 | Devotion to virtue may involve hardship. The noble person remains steadfast. |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors honour those who remain faithful even in suffering. Devotion may cost much, but the ancestors remember."                                                                                                                           | Devotion to the ancestors may involve suffering, but the ancestors remember. |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the experience of deep suffering and the cost of devotion. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful may experience deep suffering.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's description of sinking in deep mire (Psalm 69), as Job's suffering (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer, as Yudhishthira's exile (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that suffering is part of life, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may suffer, or as the African understanding that the ancestors see the suffering of the faithful, all traditions teach that ****the faithful are not immune to deep suffering****. Suffering is a reality of the human condition.

****Second, devotion to the moral order may come at a high cost.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that zeal for the moral order has consumed them (Psalm 69), as the Jewish teaching that devotion may lead to reproach, as the Buddhist teaching that the path requires self-sacrifice, as the Gita's teaching that performing duty without attachment may involve suffering, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that persecution may come with devotion, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person remains steadfast in hardship, or as the African understanding that the ancestors honour those who remain faithful in suffering, all traditions teach that ****devotion to the moral order is not a guarantee of an easy life****. It may involve suffering, shame, and estrangement.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge the reality of suffering and the cost of devotion provide a moral framework that sustains the faithful through hardship. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to abandon their commitments when they face suffering, and more likely to persevere with hope. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****perseverance and hope****—qualities that favour long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 69, Principle 1 ("The experience of deep suffering") and Principle 2 ("The cost of devotion to the moral order") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's sinking in deep mire (Psalm), as Job's suffering (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer, as Yudhishthira's exile (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that suffering is part of life, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may suffer, and as the African understanding that the ancestors see the suffering of the faithful.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the experience of deep suffering and the cost of devotion.**** The ethical lesson—that the faithful may suffer deeply, and that devotion to the moral order may come at a high cost—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 70: The Ethics of Urgent Prayer and Acknowledgment of Need**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 70 is a brief but urgent psalm of lament and petition, attributed to David. It is a prayer for deliverance from enemies, echoing the themes of Psalm 40. The psalmist cries out for the moral order to make haste to help, asking for the confusion and shame of those who seek their life. The psalm concludes with a declaration of trust: "But I am poor and needy; make haste to me, O God! You are my help and my deliverer; O LORD, do not delay!"

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader. Of David, for the memorial offering.****

****1**** Be pleased, O God, to deliver me. O LORD, make haste to help me! ****2**** Let those be put to shame and confusion who seek my life. Let those be turned back and brought to dishonor who desire my hurt. ****3**** Let those who say, "Aha, Aha!" turn back because of their shame. ****4**** But may all who seek you rejoice and be glad in you. May those who love your salvation say evermore, "God is great!" ****5**** But I am poor and needy; make haste to me, O God! You are my help and my deliverer; O LORD, do not delay!

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                   | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                  |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Urgency of Prayer****      | The psalmist cries out for the moral order to make haste. Prayer is urgent and immediate.         |
| ****Principle 2: The Plea for Deliverance****   | The psalmist asks for deliverance from enemies and for their confusion and shame.                 |
| ****Principle 3: The Acknowledgment of Need**** | The psalmist declares, "I am poor and needy." Acknowledging need is essential for receiving help. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 70**

| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person knows their own limits and acknowledges their need for the moral order. The small person thinks they are self-sufficient."                                                                                                  | Acknowledging need is a mark of the noble person.                                       |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The person who acknowledges their need before the ancestors is heard. The one who is proud and self-sufficient is not."                                                                                                                         | Acknowledging need before the ancestors is essential for being heard.                   |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the urgency of prayer and the acknowledgment of need. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, prayer is urgent and immediate.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's cry for the moral order to make haste (Psalm 70), as the Jewish teaching that prayer should be urgent and heartfelt rather than routine, as the Buddhist teaching of diligence and urgency on the path, as the Gita's teaching of the urgency of surrender, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds urgently, as the Sikh teaching that sincere and urgent prayer is answered, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person is earnest in prayer, or as the African understanding that the ancestors hear the urgent cry, all traditions teach that ****prayer is not to be delayed or treated as routine****. It is an urgent and immediate response to need.

****Second, acknowledging need is essential for receiving help.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration, "I am poor and needy" (Psalm 70), as the Jewish teaching that acknowledging need is essential, as the Buddhist teaching that acknowledging one's condition is the first step toward purification, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order gives what is lacking to those who acknowledge their need, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful acknowledge their need for guidance, as the Sikh teaching that the true devotee humbles themselves and acknowledges their need, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person knows their own limits, or as the African teaching that the person who acknowledges their need before the ancestors is heard, all traditions teach that ****humility and acknowledgment of need are prerequisites for receiving help****. Pride and self-sufficiency are barriers.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach urgent prayer and the acknowledgment of need provide a moral framework that encourages humility, immediacy in seeking help, and dependence on the moral order. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to be paralyzed by pride or delay in seeking help, and more likely to turn to the moral order in times of crisis. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****humility and urgency****—qualities that favour long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 70, Principle 1 ("The urgency of prayer") and Principle 3 ("The acknowledgment of need") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's cry for the moral order to make haste (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that prayer should be urgent and heartfelt, as the Buddhist teaching of diligence and urgency, as the Gita's teaching of the urgency of surrender (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds urgently, as the Sikh teaching that sincere and urgent prayer is answered, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person is earnest in prayer, and as the African understanding that the ancestors hear the urgent cry.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the urgency of prayer and the acknowledgment of need.**** The ethical lesson—that prayer is urgent and that acknowledging need is essential for receiving help—transcends its religious packaging.

Chapter 8 — Psalms 71–80\*\*

## **Psalm 71: The Ethics of Lifelong Trust and Confidence in Old Age**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 71 is a psalm of trust and hope in old age, attributed to an elderly psalmist who has trusted in the moral order from youth. The psalmist prays for deliverance from enemies, declaring that the moral order has been their hope and refuge since childhood. The psalm describes the psalmist's lifelong dependence on the moral order, their current old age and weakness, and their confidence that the moral order will not abandon them. The psalm concludes with a vow to praise the moral order's faithfulness.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** In you, O LORD, I take refuge; let me never be put to shame. ****2**** In your righteousness deliver me and rescue me; incline your ear to me and save me. ****3**** Be to me a rock of refuge, a strong fortress, to save me, for you are my rock and my fortress. ****4**** Rescue me, O my God, from the hand of the wicked, from the grasp of the unjust and cruel. ****5**** For you, O Lord, are my hope, my trust, O LORD, from my youth. ****6**** Upon you I have leaned from my birth; it was you who took me from my mother's womb. My praise is continually of you. ****7**** I have been like a portent to many, but you are my strong refuge. ****8**** My mouth is filled with your praise, and with your glory all day long. ****9**** Do not cast me off in the time of old age; do not forsake me when my strength is spent. ****10**** For my enemies speak concerning me, and those who watch for my life consult together. ****11**** They say, "God has forsaken him; pursue and seize him, for there is no one to deliver him." ****12**** O God, do not be far from me; O my God, make haste to help me! ****13**** Let my accusers be put to shame and consumed; let those who seek to hurt me be covered with scorn and disgrace. ****14**** But I will hope continually, and will praise you yet more and more. ****15**** My mouth will tell of your righteous acts, of your deeds of salvation all day long, though their number is past my knowledge. ****16**** I will come praising the mighty deeds of the Lord GOD, I will praise your righteousness, yours alone. ****17**** O God, from my youth you have taught me, and I still proclaim your wondrous deeds. ****18**** So even to old age and gray hairs, O God, do not forsake me, until I proclaim your might to all the generations to come. Your power ****19**** and your righteousness, O God, reach the high heavens. You who have done great things, O God, who is like you? ****20**** You who have made me see many troubles and calamities will revive me again; from the depths of the earth you will bring me up again. ****21**** You will increase my honor, and comfort me once again. ****22**** I will also praise you with the harp for your faithfulness, O my God; I will sing praises to you with the lyre, O Holy One of Israel. ****23**** My lips will shout for joy when I sing praises to you; my soul also, which you have rescued. ****24**** All day long my tongue will talk of your righteous help, for those who tried to do me harm have been put to shame and disgraced.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                         | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                             |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: Lifelong Trust in the Moral Order****                | The psalmist has trusted in the moral order from youth, from birth. Lifelong trust is a virtue.                                              |
| ****Principle 2: The Experience of Old Age and Weakness****           | The psalmist acknowledges their old age and weakness, praying not to be forsaken.                                                            |
| ****Principle 3: The Confidence in the Moral Order's Faithfulness**** | The psalmist trusts that the moral order will not abandon them in old age and will enable them to proclaim its might to the next generation. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 71**

#### **Principle 1: Lifelong Trust in the Moral Order**

| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Psalm 71:5): "For you, O Lord, are my hope, my trust, O LORD, from my youth."                                       | Lifelong trust in the moral order is a virtue. Trust must be maintained until death. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 168): "Rouse yourself, be diligent! Follow the teaching of the Buddha."     | Diligence and trust in the path must be maintained throughout life.                  |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:47: "You have the right to perform your prescribed duties, but you are not entitled to the fruits of your actions." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 6:40): "The one who strives sincerely does not perish." | Lifelong devotion and striving are essential. The sincere striver does not perish.   |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Asr (103:1–3): "By time, indeed mankind is in loss, except for those who have believed and done righteous deeds and advised each other to truth and advised each other to patience."                     | Lifelong faith and righteous deeds are essential.                                    |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee trusts in the Divine from youth to old age. The one who is faithful to the end is blessed."                                                                             | Lifelong trust in the Divine is a mark of the true devotee.                          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person cultivates virtue throughout life, from youth to old age. The one who is virtuous at the end is the true noble person."                                                | Lifelong cultivation of virtue is essential.                                         |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors honour those who honour them from youth to old age. Lifelong faithfulness is remembered and blessed."                                                                        | Lifelong faithfulness to the ancestors is honoured.                                  |

#### **Principle 3: Confidence in the Moral Order's Faithfulness**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                  |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 71:18): "So even to old age and gray hairs, O God, do not forsake me."                                                                                                               | The moral order will not abandon the faithful, even in old age.                           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | The good person can trust that their good deeds will bear fruit, even if not immediately. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                                                                                        | The moral order preserves the devoted, even in times of weakness.                         |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me."                                                                                                                                                  | The moral order is near and responds, even in old age and weakness.                       |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine does not abandon the faithful. Even in old age, the Divine is with the devotee."                                                                                                                                                                                            | The Divine is with the faithful, even in old age.                                         |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order does not abandon the virtuous. Even in old age, the noble person is supported."                                                                                                                                                                                       | The moral order supports the virtuous, even in old age.                                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors do not abandon the elderly who have honoured them. The faithful are cared for to the end."                                                                                                                                                                                 | The ancestors care for the faithful, even in old age.                                     |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding lifelong trust in the moral order and confidence in its faithfulness. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, lifelong trust in the moral order is a virtue.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that they have trusted in the moral order from youth (Psalm 71), as the Jewish teaching that trust must be maintained until death, as the Buddhist teaching of lifelong diligence on the path, as the Gita's teaching that the sincere striver does not perish, as the Qur'anic teaching that lifelong faith and righteous deeds are essential, as the Sikh teaching that lifelong trust is a mark of the true devotee, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person cultivates virtue throughout life, or as the African understanding that lifelong faithfulness to the ancestors is honoured, all traditions teach that ****faithfulness to the moral order is not a one-time event but a lifelong commitment****. The faithful are called to trust from youth to old age.

****Second, the faithful can trust that the moral order will not abandon them, even in old age and weakness.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's prayer not to be forsaken in old age (Psalm 71), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order will not abandon the faithful, as the Buddhist teaching that the good person's deeds will bear fruit, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is with the faithful in old age, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order supports the virtuous, or as the African understanding that the ancestors care for the faithful to the end, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is faithful to those who are faithful to it****. Old age and weakness are not reasons for despair; they are times when the moral order's faithfulness is especially evident.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach lifelong trust and the moral order's faithfulness provide a moral framework that sustains individuals throughout their lives, including in old age. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as the elderly are not abandoned or forgotten, and individuals are less likely to despair as they age. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****lifelong faithfulness and trust****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 71, Principle 1 ("Lifelong trust in the moral order") and Principle 3 ("Confidence in the moral order's faithfulness") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's trust from youth (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that trust must be maintained until death, as the Buddhist teaching of lifelong diligence, as the Gita's teaching that the sincere striver does not perish (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that lifelong faith is essential, as the Sikh teaching that lifelong trust is a mark of the true devotee, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person cultivates virtue throughout life, and as the African understanding that lifelong faithfulness to the ancestors is honoured.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning lifelong trust in the moral order and confidence in its faithfulness.**** The ethical lesson—that the faithful are called to trust from youth to old age, and that the moral order will not abandon them—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 72: The Ethics of Righteous Rule and Protection of the Poor**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 72 is a royal psalm, attributed to Solomon, praying for the king to rule with righteousness and justice. It envisions a reign in which the king judges the people with righteousness, defends the poor and needy, and brings peace and prosperity to the land. The psalm describes an ideal kingdom where justice flourishes, the oppressed are rescued, and the king is revered. It concludes with a doxology, marking the end of Book II of the Psalter.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****Of Solomon.****

****1**** Give the king your justice, O God, and your righteousness to a king's son. ****2**** May he judge your people with righteousness, and your poor with justice. ****3**** May the mountains yield prosperity for the people, and the hills, in righteousness. ****4**** May he defend the cause of the poor of the people, give deliverance to the needy, and crush the oppressor. ****5**** May he live while the sun endures, and as long as the moon, throughout all generations. ****6**** May he be like rain that falls on the mown grass, like showers that water the earth. ****7**** In his days may righteousness flourish and peace abound, until the moon is no more. ****8**** May he have dominion from sea to sea, and from the River to the ends of the earth. ****9**** May his foes bow down before him, and his enemies lick the dust. ****10**** May the kings of Tarshish and of the isles render him tribute; may the kings of Sheba and Seba bring gifts. ****11**** May all kings fall down before him, all nations give him service. ****12**** For he delivers the needy when they call, the poor and those who have no helper. ****13**** He has pity on the weak and the needy, and saves the lives of the needy. ****14**** From oppression and violence he redeems their life; and precious is their blood in his sight. ****15**** Long may he live! May gold of Sheba be given to him. May prayer be made for him continually, and blessings invoked for him all day long. ****16**** May there be abundance of grain in the land; may it wave on the tops of the mountains; may its fruit be like Lebanon; and may people blossom in the cities like the grass of the field. ****17**** May his name endure forever, his fame continue as long as the sun. May all nations be blessed in him; may they pronounce him happy. ****18**** Blessed be the LORD, the God of Israel, who alone does wondrous things. ****19**** Blessed be his glorious name forever; may his glory fill the whole earth. Amen and Amen. ****20**** The prayers of David son of Jesse are ended.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**
