| **Principle**                                              | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                         |
| ---------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Ideal of Righteous Rule****           | The king is to judge with righteousness and justice. Righteous rule is the foundation of a just society. |
| ****Principle 2: The Protection of the Poor and Needy****  | The king is to defend the poor, deliver the needy, and crush the oppressor.                              |
| ****Principle 3: The Blessings of Peace and Prosperity**** | Righteous rule brings peace, prosperity, and abundance to the land.                                      |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 72**

#### **Principle 1: The Ideal of Righteous Rule**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                 |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 1:18: "Rabban Shimon ben Gamliel said: On three things the world stands: on justice, on truth, and on peace." (Corollary from Deuteronomy 16:20): "Justice, and only justice, you shall pursue."                                                                                                                | The ideal ruler pursues justice. Justice is the foundation of the world. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 103: "Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, yet he who conquers himself is the greatest warrior." (Corollary from the Cakkavatti Sihanada Sutta): The ideal ruler (the wheel-turning monarch) rules in accordance with righteousness and provides protection to all subjects. | The ideal ruler rules with righteousness and self-mastery.               |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:31–33: "Considering your dharma as a warrior, you should not waver." (Corollary from Manusmriti 7.2): "The king is the protector of the people; he must rule with justice and dharma."                                                                                                                      | The ideal ruler rules with justice and dharma, protecting the people.    |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:177): "Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward the east or the west, but \[true\] righteousness is \[in\] one who believes in Allah... and gives wealth... to relatives, orphans, the needy..." (Corollary from Hadith): "The best of rulers is he who is just."                   | The ideal ruler is just and acts with righteousness.                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true ruler is one who rules with justice, truth, and humility. They do not oppress the weak but protect the vulnerable."                                                                                                                                                               | The ideal ruler rules with justice, truth, and humility.                 |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble ruler governs by virtue, not by force. They are just, humble, and truthful."                                                                                                                                                                                                    | The ideal ruler governs by virtue and justice.                           |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The chief must rule with justice, truth, and humility, or the ancestors will withdraw their protection."                                                                                                                                                                                     | The ideal chief rules with justice, truth, and humility.                 |

#### **Principle 2: The Protection of the Poor and Needy**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                         |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 72:12–14): "For he delivers the needy when they call, the poor and those who have no helper."                  | The ruler is to deliver the needy and protect the poor.          |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 129: "All tremble at violence; all fear death. Comparing oneself to others, one should not kill nor cause to kill." (Corollary from the Metta Sutta): "May all beings be happy and secure; may all beings be happy-minded."     | The ideal ruler extends compassion and protection to all beings. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Manusmriti 4.201): "The king is obliged to protect the weak and the vulnerable." | The ruler is obliged to protect the weak and vulnerable.         |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Ma'un (107:1–7): "Have you seen the one who denies the judgment? That is the one who repulses the orphan and does not encourage the feeding of the poor." (Corollary from Hadith): "The best of rulers is he who is just."       | The ruler is to care for the poor and orphan, not repulse them.  |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true ruler protects the vulnerable and shares with the needy. The Divine hears the cry of the poor."                                                                                                         | The ruler protects the vulnerable and shares with the needy.     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who, when in power, ensures that the old are not cast aside, that the young are protected, and that the poor are not neglected."                                                        | The noble ruler protects the old, young, and poor.               |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The chief is the representative of the community before the ancestors. The chief must protect the weak and the poor."                                                                                              | The chief is obliged to protect the weak and poor.               |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the ideal of righteous rule and the protection of the poor and needy. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the ideal ruler is characterized by righteousness and justice.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's prayer for the king to judge with righteousness (Psalm 72), as the Jewish teaching that justice is the foundation of the world, as the Buddhist ideal of the wheel-turning monarch who rules with righteousness, as the Gita's teaching that the king must rule with dharma, as the Qur'anic teaching that the best ruler is just, as the Sikh teaching that the true ruler rules with justice and truth, as the Confucian teaching that the noble ruler governs by virtue, or as the African teaching that the chief must rule with justice and humility, all traditions teach that ****the ideal ruler is one who rules with righteousness and justice****. This is a non-negotiable quality of legitimate authority.

****Second, the ruler is to protect the poor and needy.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the king delivers the needy and crushes the oppressor (Psalm 72), as the Jewish teaching that the ruler is to deliver the needy, as the Buddhist teaching that the ideal ruler extends compassion to all beings, as the Gita's teaching that the king is obliged to protect the weak, as the Qur'anic teaching that the ruler is to care for the poor and orphan, as the Sikh teaching that the true ruler protects the vulnerable, as the Confucian teaching that the noble ruler protects the old, young, and poor, or as the African teaching that the chief must protect the weak and poor, all traditions teach that ****a just ruler is defined by their care for the most vulnerable****. Those who rule must defend the poor, deliver the needy, and protect the weak.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the ideal of righteous rule and the protection of the vulnerable provide a moral framework that holds rulers accountable and ensures that the poor are not forgotten. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as rulers are constrained by moral standards and the vulnerable are protected. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****justice and compassion for the vulnerable****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 72, Principle 1 ("The ideal of righteous rule") and Principle 2 ("The protection of the poor and needy") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's prayer for the king to judge with righteousness (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that justice is the foundation of the world, as the Buddhist ideal of the wheel-turning monarch, as the Gita's teaching that the king must rule with dharma (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the best ruler is just, as the Sikh teaching that the true ruler rules with justice and truth, as the Confucian teaching that the noble ruler governs by virtue, and as the African teaching that the chief must rule with justice and humility.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the ideal of righteous rule and the protection of the poor and needy.**** The ethical lesson—that the ideal ruler is characterized by righteousness and justice, and that they are to protect the vulnerable—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 73: The Ethics of Faith Through Doubt and the Goodness of the Moral Order**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 73 is a wisdom psalm that grapples with the perennial problem of the prosperity of the wicked and the suffering of the righteous. It is attributed to Asaph and begins with a declaration that God is good to the pure in heart, but the psalmist admits to nearly stumbling when they saw the prosperity of the wicked. The psalmist describes the wicked as arrogant, violent, and prosperous, questioning whether righteousness has any value. However, the psalmist then enters the sanctuary and gains a new perspective: the wicked are on slippery ground and will ultimately perish. The psalm concludes with the affirmation that the moral order is the psalmist's portion and refuge.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Psalm of Asaph.****

****1**** Truly God is good to the upright, to those who are pure in heart. ****2**** But as for me, my feet had almost stumbled; my steps had nearly slipped. ****3**** For I was envious of the arrogant; I saw the prosperity of the wicked. ****4**** For they have no pain; their bodies are sound and sleek. ****5**** They are not in trouble as others are; they are not plagued like other people. ****6**** Therefore pride is their necklace; violence covers them like a garment. ****7**** Their eyes swell out with fatness; their hearts overflow with follies. ****8**** They scoff and speak with malice; loftily they threaten oppression. ****9**** They set their mouths against heaven, and their tongues range over the earth. ****10**** Therefore the people turn and praise them, and find no fault in them. ****11**** And they say, "How can God know? Is there knowledge in the Most High?" ****12**** Such are the wicked; always at ease, they increase in riches. ****13**** All in vain I have kept my heart clean and washed my hands in innocence. ****14**** For all day long I have been plagued, and am punished every morning. ****15**** If I had said, "I will talk on in this way," I would have been untrue to the circle of your children. ****16**** But when I thought how to understand this, it seemed to me a wearisome task, ****17**** until I went into the sanctuary of God; then I perceived their end. ****18**** Truly you set them in slippery places; you make them fall to ruin. ****19**** How they are destroyed in a moment, swept away utterly by terrors! ****20**** They are like a dream when one awakes; on waking you despise their phantoms. ****21**** When my soul was embittered, when I was pricked in heart, ****22**** I was stupid and ignorant; I was like a brute beast toward you. ****23**** Nevertheless I am continually with you; you hold my right hand. ****24**** You guide me with your counsel, and afterward you will receive me with honor. ****25**** Whom have I in heaven but you? And there is nothing on earth that I desire other than you. ****26**** My flesh and my heart may fail, but God is the strength of my heart and my portion forever. ****27**** Indeed, those who are far from you will perish; you put an end to those who are false to you. ****28**** But for me it is good to be near God; I have made the Lord GOD my refuge, to tell of all your works.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                       | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                      |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Struggle with the Prosperity of the Wicked**** | The psalmist admits to being troubled by the arrogance, ease, and prosperity of the wicked, and nearly loses faith.                   |
| ****Principle 2: The Transformative Power of Perspective****        | The psalmist gains a new perspective in the sanctuary, recognizing that the wicked are on slippery ground and will ultimately perish. |
| ****Principle 3: The Goodness of the Moral Order****                | Despite the struggle, the psalmist affirms that the moral order is good and is their portion, refuge, and strength.                   |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 73**

#### **Principle 1: The Struggle with the Prosperity of the Wicked**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                                  |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:16: "Rabbi Tarfon said: The day is short, the work is great, the workers are lazy, the reward is great, and the Master is pressing." (Corollary from Psalm 73 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a classic Jewish wrestling with the problem of the prosperity of the wicked.                                                                             | The faithful may struggle with why the wicked prosper and the righteous suffer.                                           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | The apparent prosperity of the wicked is temporary. The faithful may struggle but are called to trust in the moral order. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): Yudhishthira struggles with the suffering he faces while the wicked prosper.                                                                                               | The righteous may suffer while the wicked prosper. This is part of the impermanent world.                                 |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Humazah (104:1–4): "Woe to every backbiter and slanderer, who has gathered wealth and counted it, thinking that his wealth will make him immortal. No! He will surely be thrown into the Crusher."                                                                                                                                                                  | The wicked who prosper in wealth are deluded. The faithful are warned not to envy them.                                   |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The proud and arrogant may flourish for a time, but they are like flowers that wither. The faithful should not envy them."                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | The prosperity of the wicked is temporary. The faithful should not envy them.                                             |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is not envious of the success of the wicked. The moral order will eventually right all wrongs."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | The noble person does not envy the wicked. The moral order will ultimately bring justice.                                 |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The person who oppresses the weak may prosper for a time, but the ancestors see all. Do not envy them; their time will come."                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | The prosperity of the oppressor is temporary. The ancestors see all and will bring justice.                               |

#### **Principle 3: The Goodness of the Moral Order**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                         |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 73:28): "But for me it is good to be near God; I have made the Lord GOD my refuge."                           | The moral order is good and is the refuge of the faithful.                       |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): The path of the moral order provides joy and refuge.                                         | The path of the moral order is good and provides refuge and rejoicing.           |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                            | The moral order is good and preserves the devoted. The faithful can trust in it. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'" | The moral order is sufficient and good. The faithful trust in it.                |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is good and is the refuge of the faithful. Those who trust in the Divine find peace and strength."                                                                                                   | The Divine is good and is the refuge of the faithful.                            |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is good and is the refuge of the virtuous. The noble person trusts in it."                                                                                                                     | The moral order is good and is the refuge of the virtuous.                       |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are good and are the refuge of those who honour them. The faithful trust in the ancestors."                                                                                                         | The ancestors are good and are the refuge of the faithful.                       |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the struggle with the prosperity of the wicked and the affirmation of the goodness of the moral order. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful may struggle with the apparent prosperity of the wicked.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's admission of being envious of the arrogant (Psalm 73), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful may wrestle with this question, as the Buddhist teaching that the wicked may temporarily prosper, as Yudhishthira's struggle with his own suffering (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic warning that the wicked are deluded, as the Sikh teaching that the proud may flourish for a time, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person does not envy the wicked, or as the African teaching that the oppressor may prosper but the ancestors see all, all traditions teach that ****the prosperity of the wicked is a real struggle for the faithful****. It is a test of faith.

****Second, despite struggles, the faithful affirm the goodness of the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is their portion and refuge (Psalm 73), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is the refuge of the faithful, as the Buddhist teaching that the path provides joy and rejoicing, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is the refuge of the faithful, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is the refuge of the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors are the refuge of the faithful, all traditions teach that ****despite the struggle, the faithful ultimately affirm that the moral order is good and trustworthy****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge the struggle with the prosperity of the wicked while affirming the goodness of the moral order provide a moral framework that sustains faith through doubt. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to abandon their faith when they see injustice, and more likely to trust that the moral order will ultimately bring justice. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****faith through doubt****—a quality that favours long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 73, Principle 1 ("The struggle with the prosperity of the wicked") and Principle 3 ("The goodness of the moral order") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's struggle with envy and doubt (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful may wrestle with this question, as the Buddhist teaching that the wicked may temporarily prosper, as Yudhishthira's struggle (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic warning that the wicked are deluded, as the Sikh teaching that the proud may flourish for a time, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person does not envy the wicked, and as the African teaching that the oppressor may prosper but the ancestors see all.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the struggle with the prosperity of the wicked and the affirmation of the goodness of the moral order.**** The ethical lesson—that the faithful may struggle with the apparent success of the wicked, but they ultimately affirm that the moral order is good and trustworthy—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 74: The Ethics of Communal Lament and the Power of Memory**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 74 is a communal lament over the destruction of the sanctuary and the desecration of the holy place. It is attributed to Asaph and cries out to the moral order in the face of national catastrophe. The psalmist asks why the moral order has cast off the people forever and why the enemies have destroyed the sanctuary. The psalm recalls the moral order's mighty acts in creation and in the deliverance of the people, and pleads for intervention. The psalm concludes with a plea for the moral order to remember the covenant and to arise and defend the people.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Maskil of Asaph.****

****1**** O God, why do you cast us off forever? Why does your anger smoke against the sheep of your pasture? ****2**** Remember your congregation, which you acquired long ago, which you redeemed to be the tribe of your heritage. Remember Mount Zion, where you came to dwell. ****3**** Direct your steps to the perpetual ruins; the enemy has destroyed everything in the sanctuary. ****4**** Your foes have roared within your holy place; they set up their emblems there. ****5**** At the upper entrance they hacked the wooden trellis with axes. ****6**** And all its carved wood they broke down with hatchets and hammers. ****7**** They set your sanctuary on fire; they desecrated the dwelling place of your name, bringing it to the ground. ****8**** They said to themselves, "We will utterly subdue them"; they burned all the meeting places of God in the land. ****9**** We do not see our emblems; there is no longer any prophet, and there is no one among us who knows how long. ****10**** How long, O God, is the foe to scoff? Is the enemy to revile your name forever? ****11**** Why do you hold back your hand; why do you keep your hand in your bosom? ****12**** Yet God my King is from of old, working salvation in the earth. ****13**** You divided the sea by your might; you broke the heads of the dragons in the waters. ****14**** You crushed the heads of Leviathan; you gave him as food for the creatures of the wilderness. ****15**** You cut openings for springs and torrents; you dried up ever-flowing streams. ****16**** Yours is the day, yours also the night; you established the luminaries and the sun. ****17**** You have fixed all the bounds of the earth; you made summer and winter. ****18**** Remember this, O LORD, how the enemy scoffs, and an impious people reviles your name. ****19**** Do not deliver the soul of your dove to the wild animals; do not forget the life of your poor forever. ****20**** Have regard for your covenant, for the dark places of the land are full of the haunts of violence. ****21**** Do not let the downtrodden be put to shame; let the poor and needy praise your name. ****22**** Rise up, O God, plead your cause; remember how the impious scoff at you all day long. ****23**** Do not forget the clamor of your foes, the uproar of your adversaries that goes up continually.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                               | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                        |
| ----------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Communal Catastrophe**** | The psalmist laments the destruction of the sanctuary and the suffering of the community. The faithful may experience devastating loss. |
| ****Principle 2: The Plea for Divine Intervention****       | The psalmist asks the moral order to remember the covenant and to act in defense of the people. The faithful cry out for help.          |
| ****Principle 3: The Memory of Past Deliverance****         | The psalmist recalls the moral order's mighty acts in creation and in the deliverance of the people. Memory sustains hope.              |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 74**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of Communal Catastrophe**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                        |
| --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Lamentations): The book of Lamentations is a communal lament over the destruction of Jerusalem and the temple.                                                         | Communities may experience catastrophic destruction. Lament is a legitimate response.                           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): Good communities may also experience suffering. | Communities, like individuals, may experience suffering and destruction. This is part of the impermanent world. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): The Pandavas experience exile, loss, and war.                      | The righteous community may experience catastrophic loss. Suffering is part of the impermanent world.           |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:155–156): "And We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger and loss of wealth and lives and fruits, but give good tidings to the patient."                                                                                         | Communities will be tested with loss and destruction. Patience is required.                                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the community of the faithful may experience destruction. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                    | Communities may experience destruction. The faithful do not lose faith.                                         |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble community may face destruction. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                              | The virtuous community may face destruction. The moral order is not always immediately evident.                 |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the community. Even when the community faces destruction, the ancestors know the truth. The suffering community is not forgotten."                                                                         | Communities may face destruction, but the ancestors see their suffering. They are not forgotten.                |

#### **Principle 3: The Memory of Past Deliverance**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                             |
| --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 74:12–17): "Yet God my King is from of old, working salvation in the earth. You divided the sea by your might..." | Remembering past deliverance sustains hope. The moral order has acted in the past and can act again. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The memory of past good deeds and the path's promises sustains the faithful.                     | The memory of the path's teachings and the fruits of good deeds sustains hope.                       |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                    | The memory of past interventions by the moral order sustains hope for future deliverance.            |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:164): "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth... and the rain which Allah sends down from the sky, giving life thereby to the earth after its lifelessness..."                                             | The memory of creation and past deliverance sustains faith in the moral order's power.               |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee remembers the past acts of the Divine and trusts that the Divine will act again. Memory sustains faith."                                                                                           | Remembering past deliverance sustains trust in future deliverance.                                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person remembers the past and learns from it. The moral order has always sustained the virtuous."                                                                                                        | Remembering the past sustains hope for the future.                                                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors have delivered the community in the past. Remembering their acts sustains hope that they will deliver again."                                                                                           | Remembering past deliverance by the ancestors sustains hope for future deliverance.                  |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the experience of communal catastrophe and the memory of past deliverance. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, communities may experience catastrophic destruction.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's lament over the destruction of the sanctuary (Psalm 74), as the book of Lamentations' lament over the destruction of Jerusalem (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that communities may suffer, as the Pandavas' exile and war (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that communities will be tested with loss, as the Sikh teaching that communities may face destruction, as the Confucian recognition that the virtuous community may face destruction, or as the African understanding that communities may face destruction but the ancestors see their suffering, all traditions teach that ****communal catastrophe is a reality****. No community is immune to destruction and loss.

****Second, remembering past deliverance sustains hope.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's recall of the moral order's mighty acts in creation and deliverance (Psalm 74), as the Jewish teaching that remembering past deliverance sustains hope, as the Buddhist teaching that remembering the path's promises sustains faith, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order has intervened in the past and will again, as the Qur'anic teaching that creation and past deliverance reveal the moral order's power, as the Sikh teaching that remembering past acts sustains trust, as the Confucian teaching that the past teaches hope for the future, or as the African understanding that remembering past deliverance by the ancestors sustains hope, all traditions teach that ****memory is a source of hope****. The faithful are not to forget the moral order's past acts, for they are evidence of its power and faithfulness.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the reality of communal catastrophe and the importance of memory provide a moral framework that sustains hope in times of destruction. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to despair in times of catastrophe and more likely to remember and trust in the moral order's past faithfulness. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****memory and hope****—qualities that favour long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 74, Principle 1 ("The experience of communal catastrophe") and Principle 3 ("The memory of past deliverance") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's lament over the destruction of the sanctuary (Psalm), as the book of Lamentations' lament (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that communities may suffer, as the Pandavas' exile (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that communities will be tested with loss, as the Sikh teaching that communities may face destruction, as the Confucian recognition that the virtuous community may face destruction, and as the African understanding that communities may face destruction but the ancestors see their suffering.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the experience of communal catastrophe and the memory of past deliverance.**** The ethical lesson—that communities may face destruction, but remembering past deliverance sustains hope—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 75: The Ethics of Humility and Trust in Divine Judgment**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 75 is a psalm of thanksgiving and judgment, attributed to Asaph. It begins with a declaration of praise, acknowledging that the moral order is near and that its wondrous works declare it. The psalmist then conveys a divine oracle, warning the boastful and the wicked not to lift up their horns in arrogance, for judgment comes not from east or west but from the moral order. The moral order is the one who brings down one and lifts up another. The psalm concludes with a vow to praise and a declaration that the moral order will cut off the horns of the wicked.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: Do Not Destroy. A Psalm of Asaph. A Song.****

****1**** We give thanks to you, O God; we give thanks; your name is near. People tell of your wondrous works. ****2**** At the set time that I appoint I will judge with equity. ****3**** When the earth totters, with all its inhabitants, it is I who keep its pillars steady. Selah ****4**** I say to the boastful, "Do not boast," and to the wicked, "Do not lift up your horn; ****5**** do not lift up your horn on high, do not speak with insolent neck." ****6**** For not from the east or from the west and not from the wilderness comes lifting up; ****7**** but it is God who executes judgment, putting down one and lifting up another. ****8**** For in the hand of the LORD there is a cup with foaming wine, well mixed; he will pour a draught from it, and all the wicked of the earth shall drain it down to the dregs. ****9**** But I will rejoice forever; I will sing praises to the God of Jacob. ****10**** All the horns of the wicked I will cut off, but the horns of the righteous shall be exalted.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                        | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                |
| ---------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Nearness of the Moral Order**** | The moral order is near and its works are wondrous. The faithful give thanks.                   |
| ****Principle 2: The Warning Against Arrogance****   | The boastful and wicked are warned not to lift up their horns in arrogance. Pride is condemned. |
| ****Principle 3: The Divine Source of Judgment****   | Judgment comes from the moral order, which brings down one and lifts up another.                |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 75**

#### **Principle 2: The Warning Against Arrogance**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                            |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 4:1: "Ben Zoma said: Who is wise? Those who learn from every person. Who is mighty? Those who conquer their impulses." (Corollary from Proverbs 16:18): "Pride goes before destruction, and a haughty spirit before a fall."                           | Arrogance leads to destruction. The boastful are warned to humble themselves.                       |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 103: "Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, yet he who conquers himself is the greatest warrior." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 119): The wicked may prosper for a time, but their arrogance leads to suffering. | Arrogance is a delusion. True strength is self-conquest. The proud will fall.                       |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 16:13–15: "The demonic person says: 'I have obtained this, and I will obtain that; I am powerful, and I am happy. I am wealthy and well-born; who is equal to me?' Thus, deluded by ignorance, they are caught in the net of countless attachments." | Arrogance is characteristic of the demonic nature. The proud are deluded and will face destruction. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Humazah (104:1–4): "Woe to every backbiter and slanderer, who has gathered wealth and counted it, thinking that his wealth will make him immortal. No! He will surely be thrown into the Crusher."                                                | The arrogant who trust in their wealth are deluded. Their arrogance leads to destruction.           |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The proud and arrogant are like dust before the wind. They may rise for a time, but they will be scattered. The humble and the just will endure."                                                                                 | Pride and arrogance are condemned. The proud will be scattered; the humble will endure.             |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is humble; the small person is arrogant. The small person, when in power, becomes oppressive and forgets that the moral order sees all."                                                                        | Arrogance is a mark of the small person. The noble person remains humble.                           |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The person who oppresses the weak and boasts of their power is like a tree that grows tall but has shallow roots. When the storm comes, it will fall."                                                                              | Arrogance leads to a fall. The proud oppressor will not endure.                                     |

#### **Principle 3: The Divine Source of Judgment**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 75:7): "But it is God who executes judgment, putting down one and lifting up another."                                                                                                                                                            | The moral order is the ultimate source of judgment. It humbles the proud and exalts the humble.         |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | The moral order brings about the fruits of deeds. Judgment is inherent in the order of things.          |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                                                                                                                                    | The moral order intervenes in history to bring judgment, delivering the good and destroying the wicked. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:160): "If Allah should aid you, no one can overcome you; but if He should forsake you, who is there that can aid you after Him?"                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Judgment and victory come from the moral order. It brings down and lifts up.                            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the judge of all. The proud will be humbled, and the humble will be exalted. Judgment belongs to the Divine."                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | The moral order judges, humbling the proud and exalting the humble.                                     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is the ultimate judge. It brings down the arrogant and exalts the virtuous."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | The moral order judges, bringing down the arrogant and exalting the virtuous.                           |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors judge the community. The proud and oppressive will be brought down; the humble and just will be lifted up."                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | The ancestors judge, bringing down the proud and exalting the just.                                     |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the warning against arrogance and the divine source of judgment. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, arrogance is condemned and the proud are warned.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's warning to the boastful not to lift up their horns (Psalm 75), as the Proverb's teaching that pride precedes destruction (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that arrogance is a delusion that leads to suffering, as the Gita's teaching that the demonic person's arrogance leads to destruction, as the Qur'anic warning that the arrogant who trust in wealth are deluded, as the Sikh teaching that the proud are like dust before the wind, as the Confucian teaching that arrogance is a mark of the small person, or as the African teaching that the proud oppressor will fall, all traditions teach that ****arrogance is condemned by the moral order****. The proud will be humbled.

****Second, judgment comes from the moral order, which brings down one and lifts up another.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that it is God who executes judgment (Psalm 75), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order humbles the proud and exalts the humble, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order brings about the fruits of deeds, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order intervenes to deliver the good and destroy the wicked, as the Qur'anic teaching that victory and judgment come from the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is the judge, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order brings down the arrogant and exalts the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors judge, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is the ultimate source of justice****. It has the power to bring down the proud and lift up the humble.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that warn against arrogance and affirm divine judgment provide a moral framework that discourages pride and encourages humility. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to become arrogant and oppressive, and more likely to trust that the moral order will ultimately bring justice. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****humility and trust in justice****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 75, Principle 2 ("The warning against arrogance") and Principle 3 ("The divine source of judgment") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's warning to the boastful (Psalm), as the Proverb's teaching that pride precedes destruction (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that arrogance is a delusion, as the Gita's teaching that the demonic person's arrogance leads to destruction (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic warning that the arrogant are deluded, as the Sikh teaching that the proud are like dust, as the Confucian teaching that arrogance is a mark of the small person, and as the African teaching that the proud oppressor will fall.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the warning against arrogance and the divine source of judgment.**** The ethical lesson—that arrogance is condemned and that the moral order brings down the proud and lifts up the humble—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 76: The Ethics of Divine Protection and the Defeat of the Wicked**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 76 is a psalm of praise celebrating the moral order's power and majesty, particularly in defending the community and defeating its enemies. It is attributed to Asaph and describes the moral order as known in Judah and Israel, with its dwelling place in Salem and Zion. The psalm depicts the moral order's victory over the wicked, its terrible majesty, and its role as protector of the oppressed. The psalm concludes with a call to make vows and bring gifts, and a declaration that the moral order is fearsome to the rulers of the earth.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: with stringed instruments. A Psalm of Asaph. A Song.****

****1**** In Judah God is known, his name is great in Israel. ****2**** His abode has been established in Salem, his dwelling place in Zion. ****3**** There he broke the flashing arrows, the shield, the sword, and the weapons of war. Selah ****4**** Glorious are you, more majestic than the everlasting mountains. ****5**** The stouthearted were stripped of their spoil; they sank into sleep; none of the troops was able to lift a hand. ****6**** At your rebuke, O God of Jacob, both rider and horse lay stunned. ****7**** But you indeed are awesome! Who can stand before you when once your anger is roused? ****8**** From the heavens you uttered judgment; the earth feared and was still ****9**** when God rose up to establish judgment, to save all the oppressed of the earth. Selah ****10**** Human wrath serves only to praise you; you gird yourself with the remnant of wrath. ****11**** Make vows to the LORD your God, and perform them; let all who are around him bring gifts to the one who is awesome, ****12**** who cuts off the spirit of princes, who inspires fear in the kings of the earth.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                            | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                       |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Principle 1: The Moral Order's Power and Majesty**** | The moral order is powerful, majestic, and fearsome. Its power is evident in its victory over enemies. |
| ****Principle 2: The Defeat of the Wicked****            | The moral order defeats the wicked and breaks their weapons. The proud and powerful are humbled.       |
| ****Principle 3: The Protection of the Oppressed****     | The moral order rises to establish judgment and to save the oppressed of the earth.                    |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 76**

#### **Principle 2: The Defeat of the Wicked**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                               |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 76:3): "There he broke the flashing arrows, the shield, the sword, and the weapons of war."                                                                              | The moral order defeats the wicked and breaks their weapons.                                           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 103: "Though one may conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, yet he who conquers himself is the greatest warrior." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 201): "Victory breeds hatred; the defeated live in pain. The peaceful live happily, having renounced victory and defeat." | The moral order's true victory is over the self. The proud and aggressive will not ultimately prevail. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 16:18–20: "Given over to egoism, power, arrogance, lust, and anger, the demonic hate Me... I hurl these haters, cruel and the vilest among men, into demonic wombs in the cycle of birth and death."                                                                                          | The arrogant and wicked will face destruction. The moral order defeats them.                           |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:15): "Allah will throw back their mockery on them and leave them in their transgression, wandering blindly." (Corollary from Surah Al-Mu'minun 23:116): "He who conceives mischief, his mischief falls upon him."                                                               | The wicked will be repaid for their deeds. The moral order defeats them.                               |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "Those who plant thorns do not harvest flowers. The evil-doer reaps what they have sown. The wicked will be defeated."                                                                                                                                                      | The wicked will reap what they sow. The moral order defeats them.                                      |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The person who harms others will eventually be harmed by their own actions. The moral order is not blind; it returns upon the perpetrator what they have done."                                                                                                           | The wicked will be defeated by their own actions. The moral order brings justice.                      |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see all; they are not deceived. The one who wishes harm upon another will find that harm comes to their own household. The wicked will be defeated."                                                                                                           | The wicked will be defeated. The ancestors bring justice.                                              |

#### **Principle 3: The Protection of the Oppressed**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                      |
| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 76:9): "When God rose up to establish judgment, to save all the oppressed of the earth."                              | The moral order saves the oppressed. It rises to establish justice.                           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 129: "All tremble at violence; all fear death. Comparing oneself to others, one should not kill nor cause to kill." (Corollary from the Metta Sutta): "May all beings be happy and secure; may all beings be happy-minded."            | The moral order calls for compassion and protection of all beings, especially the vulnerable. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Manusmriti 4.201): "The king is obliged to protect the weak and the vulnerable."        | The moral order preserves the devoted and protects the vulnerable.                            |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Ma'un (107:1–7): "Have you seen the one who denies the judgment? That is the one who repulses the orphan and does not encourage the feeding of the poor. So woe to those who pray but are heedless of their prayer..."                  | The moral order protects the poor and orphan. Those who repulse them are condemned.           |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine hears the cry of the poor. The true devotee is one who shares with the needy and protects the vulnerable."                                                                                                   | The Divine protects the vulnerable. The true devotee shares with the needy.                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who, when in power, ensures that the old are not cast aside, that the young are protected, and that the poor are not neglected."                                                               | Protection of the vulnerable is a mark of good governance and noble character.                |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "In many African societies, the orphan, the widow, and the stranger are under the protection of the community. The ancestors watch over the vulnerable. To harm them is to bring the anger of the ancestors upon oneself." | The vulnerable are under the protection of the community and the ancestors.                   |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the defeat of the wicked and the protection of the oppressed. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order defeats the wicked and humbles the proud.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's celebration of the moral order breaking weapons of war (Psalm 76), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order defeats the wicked, as the Buddhist teaching that self-conquest is true victory and the proud will not ultimately prevail, as the Gita's teaching that the arrogant and wicked face destruction, as the Qur'anic teaching that mischief falls upon the one who conceives it, as the Sikh teaching that the wicked reap what they sow, as the Confucian teaching that harm returns to the one who causes it, or as the African teaching that the wicked will be defeated, all traditions teach that ****the moral order will ultimately defeat the wicked****. Those who trust in their own power and oppress others will not ultimately succeed.

****Second, the moral order protects the oppressed.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order saves the oppressed (Psalm 76), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order rises to save the oppressed, as the Buddhist teaching of compassion for all beings, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted and protects the vulnerable, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order protects the poor and orphan, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine hears the cry of the poor, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person protects the old, young, and poor, or as the African understanding that the ancestors watch over the vulnerable, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is the protector of the oppressed****. The vulnerable are not forgotten.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the defeat of the wicked and the protection of the oppressed provide a moral framework that discourages oppression and encourages care for the vulnerable. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to tolerate oppression and more likely to protect the weak. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****justice and compassion for the vulnerable****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 76, Principle 2 ("The defeat of the wicked") and Principle 3 ("The protection of the oppressed") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's celebration of the moral order breaking weapons (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order defeats the wicked, as the Buddhist teaching that self-conquest is true victory, as the Gita's teaching that the arrogant face destruction (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that mischief falls upon the one who conceives it, as the Sikh teaching that the wicked reap what they sow, as the Confucian teaching that harm returns to the one who causes it, and as the African teaching that the wicked will be defeated.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the defeat of the wicked and the protection of the oppressed.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order defeats the wicked and protects the oppressed—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 77: The Ethics of Doubt and the Restoration of Faith Through Memory**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 77 is a psalm of lament and remembrance, attributed to Asaph. It begins with a cry of distress, describing a sleepless night of anguish and questioning whether the moral order has abandoned the psalmist forever. The psalmist struggles with doubt, asking if the moral order's steadfast love has ceased and if its promises have failed. However, the psalm shifts dramatically when the psalmist remembers the mighty deeds of the moral order in the past—the wonders of creation and the deliverance of the people. The psalm concludes with a meditation on the moral order's power and faithfulness.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: according to Jeduthun. Of Asaph. A Psalm.****

****1**** I cry aloud to God, aloud to God, that he may hear me. ****2**** In the day of my trouble I seek the Lord; in the night my hand is stretched out without wearying; my soul refuses to be comforted. ****3**** I think of God, and I moan; I meditate, and my spirit faints. Selah ****4**** You keep my eyelids from closing; I am so troubled that I cannot speak. ****5**** I consider the days of old, and remember the years of long ago. ****6**** I commune with my heart in the night; I meditate and search my spirit: ****7**** "Will the Lord spurn forever, and never again be favorable? ****8**** Has his steadfast love ceased forever? Are his promises at an end for all time? ****9**** Has God forgotten to be gracious? Has he in anger shut up his compassion?" Selah ****10**** And I say, "It is my grief that the right hand of the Most High has changed." ****11**** I will call to mind the deeds of the LORD; I will remember your wonders of old. ****12**** I will meditate on all your work, and muse on your mighty deeds. ****13**** Your way, O God, is holy. What god is so great as our God? ****14**** You are the God who works wonders; you have displayed your might among the peoples. ****15**** With your strong arm you redeemed your people, the descendants of Jacob and Joseph. Selah ****16**** When the waters saw you, O God, when the waters saw you, they were afraid; the very deep trembled. ****17**** The clouds poured out water; the skies thundered; your arrows flashed on every side. ****18**** The crash of your thunder was in the whirlwind; your lightnings lit up the world; the earth trembled and shook. ****19**** Your way was through the sea, your path through the mighty waters; yet your footprints were unseen. ****20**** You led your people like a flock by the hand of Moses and Aaron.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                            | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                                 |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Doubt and Despair**** | The psalmist cries out in distress, questioning whether the moral order has abandoned them. Doubt and despair are legitimate human experiences.  |
| ****Principle 2: The Struggle with God's Silence****     | The psalmist asks if the moral order's steadfast love has ceased and if its promises have failed. The faithful may struggle with divine silence. |
| ****Principle 3: The Remembrance of Past Deeds****       | The psalmist remembers the moral order's mighty acts in creation and deliverance. Memory restores hope and trust.                                |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 77**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of Doubt and Despair**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                           |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Job): Job experiences profound doubt and despair, questioning the moral order's justice.                                                                                                            | The faithful may experience doubt and despair. This is a legitimate part of the spiritual journey. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition." | The good may experience doubt and despair. Suffering is part of the human condition.               |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): Yudhishthira experiences profound doubt and despair during his exile.                           | The righteous may experience doubt and despair. This is part of the impermanent world.             |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Ash-Sharh (94:5–6): "For indeed, with hardship comes ease. Indeed, with hardship comes ease." (Corollary from Surah Al-Baqarah 2:155–156): The faithful will be tested, and may experience doubt and despair.                                                                               | Doubt and despair are tests. The faithful are called to patience.                                  |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee may experience doubt and despair, but they do not lose faith. The soul longs for the Divine."                                                                                                                                                           | Doubt and despair are a reality. The faithful do not lose faith.                                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person may experience doubt and despair. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                                                  | The noble person may experience doubt. The moral order is not always immediately evident.          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the doubt and despair of the faithful. Even when you doubt, the ancestors know the truth. The faithful are not forgotten."                                                                                                                               | Doubt and despair are real, but the ancestors see and remember.                                    |

#### **Principle 3: The Remembrance of Past Deeds**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                |
| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..."
