(Corollary from Psalm 83:16): "Fill their faces with shame, so that they may seek your name, O LORD."                                                                                                                                                                                            | The desire is that even enemies may come to know the moral order.                |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 129: "All tremble at violence; all fear death. Comparing oneself to others, one should not kill nor cause to kill." (Corollary from the Metta Sutta): "May all beings be happy and secure; may all beings be happy-minded."                                                                                                                                                                 | The faithful desire that all beings, including enemies, may be happy and secure. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:11: "As people approach me, so I receive them. All paths, Arjuna, lead to me."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | The moral order receives all who seek it, including enemies who may repent.      |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:177): "Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward the east or the west, but \[true\] righteousness is \[in\] one who believes in Allah... and gives wealth... to relatives, orphans, the needy..." (Corollary from Surah Al-Hujurat 49:13): "O mankind, indeed We have created you from male and female and made you peoples and tribes that you may know one another." | The desire is that all peoples, even enemies, may come to know the moral order.  |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the father of all. The true devotee prays that all may come to know the Divine, even enemies."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | The faithful pray that even enemies may come to know the Divine.                 |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "Within the four seas, all are brothers."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | The desire is that all peoples, even enemies, may be united in the moral order.  |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are the guardians of all peoples. The faithful pray that even enemies may come to honour the ancestors."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | The faithful pray that even enemies may come to honour the ancestors.            |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the experience of enemy coalitions and the desire for enemies to know the moral order. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, communities may face enemy coalitions.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's description of a coalition of nations plotting to destroy Israel (Psalm 83), as the story of Esther (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that communities may face opposition, as the Pandavas' enemy coalition (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that communities will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that communities may face conflict, as the Confucian recognition that the virtuous community may face opposition, or as the African understanding that the community may be surrounded by enemies, all traditions teach that ****enemy coalitions are a reality****. The faithful are not immune to opposition.

****Second, the faithful desire that even enemies may come to know the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's desire that the enemies may seek the moral order's name (Psalm 83), as the Jewish teaching that the desire is for enemies to know God, as the Buddhist teaching of universal compassion for all beings, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that all peoples are to know one another, as the Sikh teaching that all may come to know the Divine, as the Confucian teaching that all within the four seas are brothers, or as the African understanding that even enemies may come to honour the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****the ultimate goal is not merely the destruction of enemies but the recognition of the moral order by all peoples****. The faithful pray that even those who oppose them may come to know the truth.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge the reality of enemy coalitions while desiring that even enemies may come to know the moral order provide a moral framework that sustains hope and prevents hatred. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to be consumed by cycles of vengeance and more likely to hope for the eventual reconciliation of all peoples under the moral order. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****hope and universalism****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 83, Principle 1 ("The experience of enemy coalitions") and Principle 3 ("The desire for the enemies to know the moral order") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's description of enemy coalitions (Psalm), as the story of Esther (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that communities may face opposition, as the Pandavas' enemy coalition (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that communities will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that communities may face conflict, as the Confucian recognition that the virtuous community may face opposition, and as the African understanding that the community may be surrounded by enemies.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the experience of enemy coalitions and the desire for enemies to know the moral order.**** The ethical lesson—that communities may face opposition, but the faithful desire that even enemies may come to know the moral order—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 84: The Ethics of Longing for the Moral Order and the Blessedness of Trust**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 84 is a psalm of longing for the presence of the moral order, attributed to the Korahites. It expresses a deep yearning to dwell in the courts of the moral order, describing the blessedness of those who live there. The psalmist speaks of the pilgrimage to Zion, the journey through the valley of Baca, and the joy of being in the presence of the moral order. The psalm concludes with the affirmation that the moral order is a sun and shield, bestowing favor and honor, and that those who trust in it are blessed.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader: according to The Gittith. Of the Korahites. A Psalm.****

****1**** How lovely is your dwelling place, O LORD of hosts! ****2**** My soul longs, indeed it faints for the courts of the LORD; my heart and my flesh sing for joy to the living God. ****3**** Even the sparrow finds a home, and the swallow a nest for herself, where she may lay her young, at your altars, O LORD of hosts, my King and my God. ****4**** Happy are those who live in your house, ever singing your praise. Selah ****5**** Happy are those whose strength is in you, in whose heart are the highways to Zion. ****6**** As they go through the valley of Baca they make it a place of springs; the early rain also covers it with pools. ****7**** They go from strength to strength; the God of gods will be seen in Zion. ****8**** O LORD God of hosts, hear my prayer; give ear, O God of Jacob! Selah ****9**** Behold our shield, O God; look on the face of your anointed. ****10**** For a day in your courts is better than a thousand elsewhere. I would rather be a doorkeeper in the house of my God than live in the tents of wickedness. ****11**** For the LORD God is a sun and shield; he bestows favor and honor. No good thing does the LORD withhold from those who walk uprightly. ****12**** O LORD of hosts, happy is everyone who trusts in you.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                    | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                               |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: Longing for the Presence of the Moral Order**** | The psalmist's soul longs and faints for the courts of the moral order. The presence of the moral order is the deepest desire of the faithful. |
| ****Principle 2: The Blessedness of Dwelling in the Courts****   | Blessed are those who dwell in the moral order's house; they are ever praising. A day in the courts is better than a thousand elsewhere.       |
| ****Principle 3: The Blessedness of Trust****                    | The moral order is a sun and shield, bestowing favor and honor. Blessed are those who trust in the moral order.                                |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 84**

#### **Principle 1: Longing for the Presence of the Moral Order**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                               |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:13: "Rabbi Shimon said: Be careful with the recitation of the Shema and with the Amidah. When you pray, do not make your prayer routine, but rather supplication and entreaty before the Holy One, blessed be He." (Corollary from Psalm 84:2): "My soul longs, indeed it faints for the courts of the LORD." | Longing for the presence of the moral order is a legitimate and essential aspect of faith.             |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 1: "Mind precedes all mental states. Mind is their chief; they are all mind-wrought." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): "He whose conquest is not conquered again, into whose conquest no one in this world enters, by what path can you lead that awakened one, whose conquest is limitless?"      | The longing for liberation and the ultimate truth is a natural part of the spiritual path.             |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 7:16: "Four kinds of people who perform good deeds worship me: the afflicted, the seeker of knowledge, the seeker of wealth, and the wise." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 7:17): "Among them, the wise person, who is always devoted to me and worships me with single-minded devotion, is the best."         | Longing for the moral order is a mark of wisdom. The wise person seeks it with single-minded devotion. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:191): "Those who remember Allah standing, sitting, and lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth, \[say\]: 'Our Lord, You have not created this in vain.'"                                                                                                   | Longing for and remembering the moral order is a mark of faith.                                        |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The soul longs for union with the Divine, as the bride longs for her groom. This longing is the essence of devotion."                                                                                                                                                                      | Longing for the Divine is the essence of devotion.                                                     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person seeks the Way and longs for it. The person who does not long for the Way is not a noble person."                                                                                                                                                                         | Longing for the moral order (the Way) is a mark of the noble person.                                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The person who does not seek the ancestors is like a person who is thirsty and does not drink. Longing for the ancestors and the spiritual realm is a natural part of being human."                                                                                                          | Longing for the ancestors is a natural part of human existence.                                        |

#### **Principle 3: The Blessedness of Trust**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                               |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 84:12): "O LORD of hosts, happy is everyone who trusts in you."                                                                                                                                                                      | Trust in the moral order leads to happiness and blessing.              |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences. He rejoices and is delighted, having observed the purity of his own deeds." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): "Health is the greatest gift; contentment is the greatest wealth; a trusted friend is the best relative; Nirvana is the greatest bliss." | Trust in the path of the moral order leads to rejoicing and happiness. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Trust in the moral order leads to preservation and blessing.           |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                                                                                                                        | Trust in the moral order leads to sufficiency and blessing.            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The one who trusts in the Divine is blessed. Trust is the anchor of the soul."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | Trust in the Divine leads to blessing.                                 |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person trusts in the moral order and is blessed. The one who trusts does not fear."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | Trust in the moral order leads to blessing.                            |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors bless those who trust in them. Trust brings protection and blessing."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Trust in the ancestors leads to blessing and protection.               |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the longing for the presence of the moral order and the blessedness of trust. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful deeply long for the presence of the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's soul fainting for the courts of the LORD (Psalm 84), as the Jewish teaching that prayer should be heartfelt and not routine, as the Buddhist longing for liberation, as the Gita's teaching that the wise person seeks the moral order with single-minded devotion, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful remember and reflect on the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that longing is the essence of devotion, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person longs for the Way, or as the African understanding that longing for the ancestors is natural, all traditions teach that ****longing for the moral order is not a sign of weakness but a mark of wisdom and devotion****. The faithful are called to cultivate this longing.

****Second, those who trust in the moral order are blessed.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that happy is everyone who trusts in the moral order (Psalm 84), as the Jewish teaching that trust leads to happiness, as the Buddhist teaching that trust in the path leads to rejoicing, as the Gita's teaching that trust leads to preservation and blessing, as the Qur'anic teaching that trust leads to sufficiency, as the Sikh teaching that trust leads to blessing, as the Confucian teaching that trust leads to blessing and freedom from fear, or as the African teaching that trust in the ancestors leads to blessing and protection, all traditions teach that ****trust in the moral order is the source of true happiness and blessing****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that cultivate longing for the moral order and trust in it provide a moral framework that sustains hope and prevents despair. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to be driven by material desires and more likely to find contentment in trust and longing for the moral order. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****longing and trust****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 84, Principle 1 ("Longing for the presence of the moral order") and Principle 3 ("The blessedness of trust") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's soul fainting for the courts of the LORD (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that prayer should be heartfelt, as the Buddhist longing for liberation, as the Gita's teaching of single-minded devotion (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful remember and reflect, as the Sikh teaching that longing is the essence of devotion, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person longs for the Way, and as the African understanding that longing for the ancestors is natural.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning longing for the moral order and the blessedness of trust.**** The ethical lesson—that longing for the moral order is a mark of wisdom, and that trust in it leads to blessing—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 85: The Ethics of Memory, Hope, and Peace**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 85 is a communal lament that looks back on past deliverance and forward to future restoration. It is attributed to the Korahites and begins by recalling how the moral order restored the fortunes of the people and forgave their sins. The psalmist then pleads for renewed restoration, asking if the moral order's anger will continue. The psalm concludes with a vision of peace and prosperity, describing the convergence of steadfast love and faithfulness, righteousness and peace, and the promise that the land will yield its harvest.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****To the leader. Of the Korahites. A Psalm.****

****1**** LORD, you were favorable to your land; you restored the fortunes of Jacob. ****2**** You forgave the iniquity of your people; you pardoned all their sin. Selah ****3**** You withdrew all your wrath; you turned from your hot anger. ****4**** Restore us again, O God of our salvation, and put away your indignation toward us. ****5**** Will you be angry with us forever? Will you prolong your anger to all generations? ****6**** Will you not revive us again, so that your people may rejoice in you? ****7**** Show us your steadfast love, O LORD, and grant us your salvation. ****8**** Let me hear what God the LORD will speak, for he will speak peace to his people, to his faithful, to those who turn to him in their hearts. ****9**** Surely his salvation is at hand for those who fear him, that his glory may dwell in our land. ****10**** Steadfast love and faithfulness will meet; righteousness and peace will kiss each other. ****11**** Faithfulness will spring up from the ground, and righteousness will look down from the sky. ****12**** The LORD will give what is good, and our land will yield its increase. ****13**** Righteousness will go before him, and will make a path for his steps.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                         | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                                            |
| ----------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Memory of Past Deliverance****   | The psalmist recalls how the moral order restored the people and forgave their sins. Memory of past deliverance sustains hope.                              |
| ****Principle 2: The Plea for Renewed Restoration**** | The psalmist asks if the moral order's anger will continue and pleads for renewed favor and salvation.                                                      |
| ****Principle 3: The Hope for Peace****               | The psalmist envisions a time when steadfast love and faithfulness meet, and righteousness and peace kiss. The moral order will bring peace and prosperity. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 85**

#### **Principle 1: The Memory of Past Deliverance**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 85:1–3): "LORD, you were favorable to your land; you restored the fortunes of Jacob. You forgave the iniquity of your people." | Remembering past deliverance sustains hope for future restoration.                      |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The memory of past good deeds and the path's promises sustains the faithful.                                  | Remembering the path's teachings and past good deeds sustains hope.                     |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                 | Remembering past interventions by the moral order sustains hope for future deliverance. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:164): "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth... and the rain which Allah sends down from the sky, giving life thereby to the earth after its lifelessness..."                                                          | Remembering creation and past deliverance sustains faith in the moral order's power.    |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee remembers the past acts of the Divine and trusts that the Divine will act again. Memory sustains faith."                                                                                                        | Remembering past deliverance sustains trust in future deliverance.                      |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person remembers the past and learns from it. The moral order has always sustained the virtuous."                                                                                                                     | Remembering the past sustains hope for the future.                                      |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors have delivered the community in the past. Remembering their acts sustains hope that they will deliver again."                                                                                                        | Remembering past deliverance by the ancestors sustains hope for future deliverance.     |

#### **Principle 3: The Hope for Peace**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                            |
| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 1:18: "Rabban Shimon ben Gamliel said: On three things the world stands: on justice, on truth, and on peace." (Corollary from Psalm 85:10): "Steadfast love and faithfulness will meet; righteousness and peace will kiss each other."                                                                                                                   | Peace is the fruit of righteousness and faithfulness. The moral order brings peace. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 201: "Victory breeds hatred; the defeated live in pain. The peaceful live happily, having renounced victory and defeat." (Corollary from Metta Sutta): "May all beings be happy and secure; may all beings be happy-minded."                                                                                                                       | Peace comes from renunciation, compassion, and goodwill toward all beings.          |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:70: "The person who is not disturbed by the flow of desires—just as the ocean receives the rivers and remains unperturbed—attains peace." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 5:29): "When one knows that I am the enjoyer of all sacrifices and austerities, the supreme Lord of all worlds, and the friend of all beings, one attains peace."            | Peace comes from recognizing the moral order and renouncing attachment.             |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Fath (48:4): "It is He who sent down tranquility into the hearts of the believers that they would increase in faith along with their \[existing\] faith." (Corollary from Surah Al-Ra'd 13:28): "Those who have believed and whose hearts are assured by the remembrance of Allah. Unquestionably, by the remembrance of Allah hearts are assured." | Peace and tranquility come from remembrance of the moral order.                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine brings peace to the faithful. Those who trust in the Divine find peace in their hearts and in the world."                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Peace comes from trust in the Divine.                                               |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person seeks harmony, not uniformity. They promote peace and avoid conflict."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Peace and harmony are the goals of the noble person.                                |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors bless the community with peace when the people walk rightly. Peace is a sign of the ancestors' favor."                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Peace is a blessing from the ancestors.                                             |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the memory of past deliverance and the hope for peace. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, remembering past deliverance sustains hope.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's recall of how the moral order restored the people (Psalm 85), as the Jewish teaching that memory sustains hope, as the Buddhist teaching that remembering the path sustains faith, as the Gita's teaching that remembering past interventions sustains hope, as the Qur'anic teaching that remembering creation and past deliverance sustains faith, as the Sikh teaching that memory sustains trust, as the Confucian teaching that remembering the past sustains hope, or as the African understanding that remembering past deliverance by the ancestors sustains hope, all traditions teach that ****the memory of past deliverance is a source of hope****. The faithful are not to forget what the moral order has done.

****Second, the moral order brings peace.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's vision of steadfast love and faithfulness meeting and righteousness and peace kissing (Psalm 85), as the Jewish teaching that the world stands on justice, truth, and peace, as the Buddhist teaching that peace comes from renunciation and compassion, as the Gita's teaching that peace comes from recognizing the moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that peace comes from remembrance, as the Sikh teaching that peace comes from trust in the Divine, as the Confucian teaching that peace and harmony are the goals of the noble person, or as the African teaching that peace is a blessing from the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****true peace comes from the moral order and is the fruit of righteousness and trust****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the memory of past deliverance and the hope for peace provide a moral framework that sustains hope and encourages the pursuit of peace. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to despair in times of trouble and more likely to seek peace through righteousness. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****hope and peacemaking****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 85, Principle 1 ("The memory of past deliverance") and Principle 3 ("The hope for peace") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's recall of past restoration (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that memory sustains hope, as the Buddhist teaching that remembering the path sustains faith, as the Gita's teaching that remembering past interventions sustains hope (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that remembering creation sustains faith, as the Sikh teaching that memory sustains trust, as the Confucian teaching that remembering the past sustains hope, and as the African understanding that remembering past deliverance by the ancestors sustains hope.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the memory of past deliverance and the hope for peace.**** The ethical lesson—that remembering past deliverance sustains hope, and that the moral order brings peace—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 86: The Ethics of Acknowledgment of Need and Trust in Steadfast Love**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 86 is a psalm of lament and trust, attributed to David. It is a prayer for deliverance from enemies, but it is also a meditation on the character of the moral order—its steadfast love, faithfulness, mercy, and incomparability. The psalmist acknowledges their own weakness and need, declaring that they are "poor and needy," while affirming that the moral order is "abounding in steadfast love." The psalm concludes with a plea for a sign of favor and a declaration that the moral order has delivered the psalmist from the depths of Sheol.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Prayer of David.****

****1**** Incline your ear, O LORD, and answer me, for I am poor and needy. ****2**** Preserve my life, for I am devoted to you; save your servant who trusts in you. You are my God; ****3**** be gracious to me, O Lord, for to you do I cry all day long. ****4**** Gladden the soul of your servant, for to you, O Lord, I lift up my soul. ****5**** For you, O Lord, are good and forgiving, abounding in steadfast love to all who call on you. ****6**** Give ear, O LORD, to my prayer; listen to my cry of supplication. ****7**** In the day of my trouble I call on you, for you will answer me. ****8**** There is none like you among the gods, O Lord, nor are there any works like yours. ****9**** All the nations you have made shall come and bow down before you, O Lord, and shall glorify your name. ****10**** For you are great and do wondrous things; you alone are God. ****11**** Teach me your way, O LORD, that I may walk in your truth; give me an undivided heart to revere your name. ****12**** I give thanks to you, O Lord my God, with my whole heart, and I will glorify your name forever. ****13**** For great is your steadfast love toward me; you have delivered my soul from the depths of Sheol. ****14**** O God, the insolent rise up against me; a band of ruffians seeks my life, and they do not set you before them. ****15**** But you, O Lord, are a God merciful and gracious, slow to anger and abounding in steadfast love and faithfulness. ****16**** Turn to me and be gracious to me; give your strength to your servant; save the child of your serving girl. ****17**** Show me a sign of your favor, so that those who hate me may see it and be put to shame, because you, LORD, have helped me and comforted me.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                  | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                              |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Acknowledgment of Need****                | The psalmist declares, "I am poor and needy." Acknowledging one's need is essential for receiving help.       |
| ****Principle 2: Trust in the Moral Order's Steadfast Love**** | The psalmist declares that the moral order is abounding in steadfast love and faithful to all who call on it. |
| ****Principle 3: The Commitment to Praise****                  | The psalmist promises to give thanks and praise with their whole heart.                                       |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 86**

#### **Principle 1: The Acknowledgment of Need**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                            |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 86:1): "Incline your ear, O LORD, and answer me, for I am poor and needy."                                                                  | Acknowledging one's need is essential for receiving help.                           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 165: "Evil is done by oneself; by oneself is one defiled. Evil is left undone by oneself; by oneself is one purified. Purity and impurity depend on oneself." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 1): Acknowledging the state of one's mind is the first step. | Acknowledging one's condition—including need—is the first step toward purification. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                                                          | Acknowledging need and dependence on the moral order is essential.                  |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Fatihah (1:6–7): "Guide us to the straight path... not of those who have gone astray."                                                                                                                                                                        | Acknowledging need for guidance is essential.                                       |

The one who humbles themselves is lifted up."                                                                                                                         | Humility and acknowledgment of need are essential.                                  |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person knows their own limits and acknowledges their need for the moral order. The small person thinks they are self-sufficient."                                                                                                  | Acknowledging need is a mark of the noble person.                                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The person who acknowledges their need before the ancestors is heard. The one who is proud and self-sufficient is not."                                                                                                                         | Acknowledging need before the ancestors is essential for being heard.               |

#### **Principle 2: Trust in the Moral Order's Steadfast Love**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                 |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 86:5): "For you, O Lord, are good and forgiving, abounding in steadfast love to all who call on you."                                                          | The moral order is abounding in steadfast love and faithful to all who call on it.       |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences. He rejoices and is delighted, having observed the purity of his own deeds." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): The path of the moral order is a source of joy and steadfast love. | The path of the moral order is a source of joy and steadfast love for all who follow it. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                                                                  | The moral order preserves and provides for the devoted.                                  |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                                                  | The moral order is sufficient and faithful to those who call on it.                      |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is abounding in love and mercy. Those who call on the Divine are heard."                                                                                                                                                                              | The Divine is loving and merciful and responds to those who call.                        |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is faithful and responds to the virtuous. The noble person trusts in its steadfast love."                                                                                                                                                       | The moral order is faithful and responds to the virtuous.                                |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are abounding in love and faithfulness. Those who call on them are heard."                                                                                                                                                                           | The ancestors are loving and faithful and respond to those who call.                     |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the acknowledgment of need and trust in the moral order's steadfast love. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, acknowledging one's need is essential for receiving help.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration, "I am poor and needy" (Psalm 86), as the Jewish teaching that acknowledging need is essential, as the Buddhist teaching that acknowledging one's condition is the first step toward purification, as the Gita's teaching that acknowledging dependence on the moral order is essential, as the Qur'anic teaching that acknowledging need for guidance is essential, as the Sikh teaching that the true devotee humbles themselves and acknowledges their need, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person knows their own limits, or as the African teaching that the person who acknowledges their need before the ancestors is heard, all traditions teach that ****humility and acknowledgment of need are prerequisites for receiving help****. Pride and self-sufficiency are barriers.

****Second, the moral order is abounding in steadfast love and faithful to all who call on it.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is good and forgiving, abounding in steadfast love to all who call (Psalm 86), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is faithful to those who call on it, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is a source of joy and steadfast love, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves and provides for the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is loving and merciful, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order responds to the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors are loving and faithful, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is characterized by steadfast love and faithfulness****. It responds to those who call on it.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the importance of acknowledging need and the moral order's steadfast love provide a moral framework that encourages humility and trust. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to be paralyzed by pride and more likely to trust in the moral order's love and faithfulness. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****humility and trust****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 86, Principle 1 ("The acknowledgment of need") and Principle 2 ("Trust in the moral order's steadfast love") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration of poverty and need (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that acknowledging need is essential, as the Buddhist teaching that acknowledging one's condition is the first step, as the Gita's teaching of dependence on the moral order (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that acknowledging need for guidance is essential, as the Sikh teaching that the true devotee humbles themselves, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person knows their limits, and as the African teaching that the person who acknowledges their need before the ancestors is heard.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the acknowledgment of need and trust in the moral order's steadfast love.**** The ethical lesson—that acknowledging one's need is essential, and that the moral order is abounding in steadfast love—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 87: The Ethics of Inclusion and the Unity of the Faithful**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 87 is a psalm of Zion, celebrating Jerusalem as the city of the moral order and the spiritual home of all peoples. It is attributed to the Korahites and declares that the moral order loves the gates of Zion more than all the dwellings of Jacob. The psalm then lists various nations—Rahab (Egypt), Babylon, Philistia, Tyre, and Ethiopia—as being born in Zion, indicating that even foreigners are counted among the people of the moral order. The psalm concludes with the declaration that all of the faithful find their source in Zion.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****Of the Korahites. A Psalm. A Song.****

****1**** On the holy mount stands the city he founded; ****2**** the LORD loves the gates of Zion more than all the dwellings of Jacob. ****3**** Glorious things are spoken of you, O city of God. Selah ****4**** Among those who know me I mention Rahab and Babylon; Philistia too, and Tyre, with Ethiopia— "This one was born there," they say. ****5**** And of Zion it shall be said, "This one and that one were born in it"; for the Most High himself will establish it. ****6**** The LORD records, as he registers the peoples, "This one was born there." Selah ****7**** Singers and dancers alike say, "All my springs are in you."

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                        | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                       |
| ---------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Moral Order's Love for Zion**** | The moral order loves the gates of Zion. Zion is the chosen dwelling place.                                                            |
| ****Principle 2: The Inclusion of All Peoples****    | Various nations—Egypt, Babylon, Philistia, Tyre, and Ethiopia—are counted as being born in Zion. The moral order includes all peoples. |
| ****Principle 3: The Unity of the Faithful****       | All the faithful find their source in Zion. Singers and dancers alike declare that all their springs are in the moral order.           |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 87**

#### **Principle 2: The Inclusion of All Peoples**
