(Corollary from Isaiah 56:7): "For my house shall be called a house of prayer for all peoples."                                    | The moral order's house is a house of prayer for all peoples. Inclusion is central.                |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order is open to all, regardless of origin.                           | The path of the moral order is universal and open to all peoples.                                  |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:11: "As people approach me, so I receive them. All paths, Arjuna, lead to me." (Corollary from Rig Veda 1.1.1): The moral order is invoked by all peoples.                                                                      | The moral order receives all who approach it, regardless of their origin.                          |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Hujurat (49:13): "O mankind, indeed We have created you from male and female and made you peoples and tribes that you may know one another. Indeed, the most noble of you in the sight of Allah is the most righteous of you." | All peoples are created by the moral order. Righteousness, not origin, is the measure of nobility. |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The One Divine is the father of all. There is no Hindu, no Muslim; all are children of the One."                                                                                                               | The moral order includes all peoples without distinction.                                          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "Within the four seas, all are brothers."                                                                                                                                                                      | All peoples are connected under the universal moral order.                                         |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "All peoples have their ancestors, and all peoples are connected through the moral order. The ancestors are the guardians of all peoples."                                                                        | The moral order is universal, and all peoples are connected through their ancestors.               |

#### **Principle 3: The Unity of the Faithful**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                         |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 1:18: "Rabban Shimon ben Gamliel said: On three things the world stands: on justice, on truth, and on peace." (Corollary from Psalm 87:7): "Singers and dancers alike say, 'All my springs are in you.'"  | All the faithful find their source and unity in the moral order. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): All who follow the path reach the same goal and are united in it. | The path of the moral order unites all who follow it.            |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:11: "As people approach me, so I receive them. All paths, Arjuna, lead to me."                                                                                                                        | All paths lead to the same moral order, uniting all who seek it. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:64): "Say, 'O People of the Scripture, come to a common word between us and you: that we will not worship except Allah.'"                                                                   | The faithful are called to a common unity in the moral order.    |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "All are gathered in the One. There is no outsider; all are united in the Divine."                                                                                                    | All are united in the One Divine.                                |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "Within the four seas, all are brothers."                                                                                                                                            | All peoples are brothers, united under the moral order.          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "All peoples are connected through the ancestors. The community of the ancestors is one."                                                                                               | All peoples are united in the community of the ancestors.        |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the inclusion of all peoples and the unity of the faithful. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order includes all peoples.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that Rahab, Babylon, Philistia, Tyre, and Ethiopia are born in Zion (Psalm 87), as the Isaiah's teaching that the moral order's house is a house of prayer for all peoples (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that the path is open to all, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that all peoples are created by the moral order and that righteousness is the measure of nobility, as the Sikh teaching that all are children of the One, as the Confucian teaching that all within the four seas are brothers, or as the African understanding that all peoples are connected through their ancestors, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is universal and inclusive****. It does not belong to one nation or people alone.

****Second, all the faithful are united in the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that singers and dancers alike say, "All my springs are in you" (Psalm 87), as the Jewish teaching that all the faithful find their source in the moral order, as the Buddhist teaching that all who follow the path reach the same goal, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the same moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful are called to a common word, as the Sikh teaching that all are gathered in the One, as the Confucian teaching that all within the four seas are brothers, or as the African teaching that all peoples are united in the community of the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****the faithful are ultimately united in the moral order****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the inclusion of all peoples and the unity of the faithful provide a moral framework that transcends tribalism, nationalism, and sectarianism. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less prone to ethnic or religious conflict and more open to cooperation across group boundaries. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****universalism and inclusivity****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 87, Principle 2 ("The inclusion of all peoples") and Principle 3 ("The unity of the faithful") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration that various nations are born in Zion (Psalm), as the Isaiah's teaching that the moral order's house is a house of prayer for all peoples (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that the path is open to all, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that all peoples are created by the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that all are children of the One, as the Confucian teaching that all within the four seas are brothers, and as the African teaching that all peoples are connected through their ancestors.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the inclusion of all peoples and the unity of the faithful.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order is universal and inclusive, and that the faithful are ultimately united—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 88: The Ethics of Unrelieved Suffering and the Persistence of Prayer**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 88 is the darkest psalm in the Psalter, a lament of unrelieved despair. It is attributed to Heman the Ezrahite and describes the psalmist's deep suffering, isolation, and sense of abandonment by the moral order. The psalmist cries out day and night, describing themselves as being in the depths of the Pit, surrounded by terrors, and cut off from friends. Unlike other laments, Psalm 88 contains no shift to hope or praise—it ends in darkness.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Song. A Psalm of the Korahites. To the leader: according to Mahalath Leannoth. A Maskil of Heman the Ezrahite.****

****1**** O LORD, God of my salvation, when, at night, I cry out in your presence, ****2**** let my prayer come before you; incline your ear to my cry. ****3**** For my soul is full of troubles, and my life draws near to Sheol. ****4**** I am counted among those who go down to the Pit; I am like those who have no help, ****5**** like those forsaken among the dead, like the slain that lie in the grave, whom you remember no more, and who are cut off from your hand. ****6**** You have put me in the depths of the Pit, in the regions dark and deep. ****7**** Your wrath lies heavy upon me, and you overwhelm me with all your waves. Selah ****8**** You have caused my companions to shun me; you have made me a thing of horror to them. I am shut in so that I cannot escape; ****9**** my eye grows dim through sorrow. Every day I call on you, O LORD; I spread out my hands to you. ****10**** Do you work wonders for the dead? Do the shades rise up to praise you? Selah ****11**** Is your steadfast love declared in the grave, or your faithfulness in Abaddon? ****12**** Are your wonders known in the darkness, or your saving help in the land of forgetfulness? ****13**** But I, O LORD, cry out to you; in the morning my prayer comes before you. ****14**** O LORD, why do you cast me off? Why do you hide your face from me? ****15**** Wretched and close to death from my youth up, I suffer your terrors; I am desperate. ****16**** Your wrath has swept over me; your dread assaults destroy me. ****17**** They surround me like a flood all day long; from all sides they close in on me. ****18**** You have caused friend and neighbor to shun me; my companions are in darkness.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                               | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                     |
| ----------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Principle 1: The Experience of Unrelieved Suffering**** | The psalmist describes being in the depths of the Pit, surrounded by terrors, and cut off from friends. Suffering may be unrelieved. |
| ****Principle 2: The Cry of Despair****                     | The psalmist cries out in despair, asking why the moral order has cast them off and hidden its face. Honest despair is legitimate.   |
| ****Principle 3: The Persistence of Prayer****              | The psalmist continues to pray and cry out, even in the face of darkness. Prayer persists even when no answer seems to come.         |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 88**

#### **Principle 1: The Experience of Unrelieved Suffering**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                       |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:4: "Hillel said: Do not trust in yourself until the day of your death." (Corollary from Job): The book of Job depicts a righteous man who experiences profound, unrelieved suffering and despair.                                                                                           | The faithful may experience profound and unrelieved suffering. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition." | The good may experience profound suffering.                    |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): Yudhishthira experiences profound suffering and loss.                                           | The righteous may experience profound suffering.               |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:155–156): "And We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger and loss of wealth and lives and fruits, but give good tidings to the patient."                                                                                                                      | The faithful will be tested with suffering.                    |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the righteous may experience profound suffering. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                                                          | Suffering is part of life. The faithful do not lose faith.     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person may experience suffering. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                                                          | The noble person may suffer.                                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the faithful. Even when you suffer profoundly, the ancestors know the truth. The faithful are not forgotten."                                                                                                                           | The ancestors see suffering. The faithful are not forgotten.   |

#### **Principle 3: The Persistence of Prayer**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                        |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 2:13: "Rabbi Shimon said: Be careful with the recitation of the Shema and with the Amidah. When you pray, do not make your prayer routine, but rather supplication and entreaty before the Holy One, blessed be He." (Corollary from Psalm 88:13): "But I, O LORD, cry out to you; in the morning my prayer comes before you." | The faithful persist in prayer, even in darkness.                               |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 168: "Rouse yourself, be diligent! Follow the teaching of the Buddha. Shake off the dust of death, as an elephant shakes off dust." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 16): The well-doer rejoices in both existences.                                                                                                    | Persistence in diligence and prayer is essential.                               |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 18:66: "Abandon all varieties of dharma and surrender unto me alone. I will deliver you from all sins. Do not fear."                                                                                                                                                                                                         | The faithful persist in surrender and prayer, even in fear.                     |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me."                                                                                                                                                                     | The faithful persist in calling upon the moral order, trusting that it is near. |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee cries out to the Divine, even in the darkest night. The one who persists in prayer is heard."                                                                                                                                                                                            | Persistence in prayer, even in darkness, is a mark of the true devotee.         |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is earnest in prayer and does not delay. The moral order responds to those who are sincere."                                                                                                                                                                                            | Earnest and persistent prayer is a mark of the noble person.                    |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors hear the persistent cry of those who call upon them. Do not stop calling, even when the answer seems slow."                                                                                                                                                                                   | Persistence in calling upon the ancestors is essential.                         |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the experience of unrelieved suffering and the persistence of prayer. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful may experience profound and unrelieved suffering.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's description of being in the depths of the Pit (Psalm 88), as Job's suffering (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer, as Yudhishthira's loss (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that suffering is part of life, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may suffer, or as the African understanding that the ancestors see suffering, all traditions teach that ****the faithful are not immune to profound suffering****. Unrelieved suffering is a reality.

****Second, the faithful persist in prayer even in the face of darkness.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's persistence in crying out day and night (Psalm 88), as the Jewish teaching that prayer should be heartfelt and persistent, as the Buddhist teaching of diligence and persistence, as the Gita's teaching of surrender even in fear, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds to those who call, as the Sikh teaching that the true devotee cries out even in darkness, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person is earnest in prayer, or as the African understanding that persistence in calling upon the ancestors is essential, all traditions teach that ****prayer persists even when no answer seems to come****. The faithful do not stop crying out.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that acknowledge unrelieved suffering and teach persistence in prayer provide a moral framework that sustains the faithful through the darkest times. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to abandon their faith in times of despair and more likely to persist in seeking the moral order. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****resilience and persistence****—qualities that favour long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 88, Principle 1 ("The experience of unrelieved suffering") and Principle 3 ("The persistence of prayer") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's cry from the depths (Psalm), as Job's suffering (Judaism), as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer, as Yudhishthira's loss (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that suffering is part of life, as the Confucian recognition that the noble person may suffer, and as the African understanding that the ancestors see suffering.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the experience of unrelieved suffering and the persistence of prayer.**** The ethical lesson—that the faithful may suffer profoundly, but they persist in prayer even in the face of darkness—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 89: The Ethics of Steadfast Love and the Reality of Defeat**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 89 is a royal psalm and a communal lament, attributed to Ethan the Ezrahite. It is the final psalm of Book III of the Psalter. The psalm begins with a celebration of the moral order's steadfast love and faithfulness, and the covenant made with David—a promise that his dynasty would endure forever. However, the psalm then shifts dramatically to a lament over the apparent failure of that promise: the king has been rejected, the covenant seems broken, and the people are suffering defeat. The psalmist cries out, "How long, O LORD? Will you hide yourself forever?" The psalm concludes with a plea for the moral order to remember its covenant and to restore the people.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Maskil of Ethan the Ezrahite.****

****1**** I will sing of your steadfast love, O LORD, forever; with my mouth I will proclaim your faithfulness to all generations. ****2**** I declare that your steadfast love is established forever; your faithfulness is as firm as the heavens. ****3**** You said, "I have made a covenant with my chosen one; I have sworn to my servant David: ****4**** 'I will establish your descendants forever, and build your throne for all generations.'" Selah ****5**** Let the heavens praise your wonders, O LORD, your faithfulness in the assembly of the holy ones. ****6**** For who in the skies can be compared to the LORD? Who among the heavenly beings is like the LORD, ****7**** a God feared in the council of the holy ones, great and awesome above all that are around him? ****8**** O LORD God of hosts, who is as mighty as you, O LORD? Your faithfulness surrounds you. ****9**** You rule the raging of the sea; when its waves rise, you still them. ****10**** You crushed Rahab like a carcass; you scattered your enemies with your mighty arm. ****11**** The heavens are yours, the earth also is yours; the world and all that is in it—you have founded them. ****12**** The north and the south—you created them; Tabor and Hermon joyously praise your name. ****13**** You have a mighty arm; strong is your hand, high your right hand. ****14**** Righteousness and justice are the foundation of your throne; steadfast love and faithfulness go before you. ****15**** Happy are the people who know the festal shout, who walk, O LORD, in the light of your countenance; ****16**** they exult in your name all day long, and extol your righteousness. ****17**** For you are the glory of their strength; by your favor our horn is exalted. ****18**** For our shield belongs to the LORD, our king to the Holy One of Israel. ****19**** Then you spoke in a vision to your faithful one, and said: "I have set the crown on one who is mighty, I have exalted one chosen from the people. ****20**** I have found my servant David; with my holy oil I have anointed him; ****21**** my hand shall always remain with him; my arm also shall strengthen him. ****22**** The enemy shall not outwit him, the wicked shall not humble him. ****23**** I will crush his foes before him and strike down those who hate him. ****24**** My faithfulness and steadfast love shall be with him; and in my name his horn shall be exalted. ****25**** I will set his hand on the sea and his right hand on the rivers. ****26**** He shall cry to me, 'You are my Father, my God, and the Rock of my salvation!' ****27**** I will make him the firstborn, the highest of the kings of the earth. ****28**** Forever I will keep my steadfast love for him, and my covenant with him will stand firm. ****29**** I will establish his line forever, and his throne as long as the heavens endure. ****30**** If his children forsake my law and do not walk according to my ordinances, ****31**** if they violate my statutes and do not keep my commandments, ****32**** then I will punish their transgression with the rod and their iniquity with scourges; ****33**** but I will not remove from him my steadfast love, or be false to my faithfulness. ****34**** I will not violate my covenant, or alter the word that went forth from my lips. ****35**** Once and for all I have sworn by my holiness; I will not lie to David. ****36**** His line shall continue forever, and his throne endure before me like the sun. ****37**** It shall be established forever like the moon, an enduring witness in the skies." Selah ****38**** But now you have spurned and rejected him; you are full of wrath against your anointed. ****39**** You have renounced the covenant with your servant; you have defiled his crown in the dust. ****40**** You have broken down all his walls; you have laid his strongholds in ruins. ****41**** All who pass by despoil him; he has become the scorn of his neighbors. ****42**** You have exalted the right hand of his foes; you have made all his enemies rejoice. ****43**** Moreover, you have turned back the edge of his sword, and you have not supported him in battle. ****44**** You have brought his glory to an end, and cast his throne to the ground. ****45**** You have cut short the days of his youth; you have covered him with shame. Selah ****46**** How long, O LORD? Will you hide yourself forever? How long will your wrath burn like fire? ****47**** Remember how short my time is— for what vanity you have created all mortals! ****48**** Who can live and never see death? Who can escape the power of Sheol? Selah ****49**** Lord, where is your steadfast love of old, which by your faithfulness you swore to David? ****50**** Remember, O Lord, how your servant is taunted, how I bear in my bosom the insults of the peoples, ****51**** with which your enemies taunt, O LORD, with which they taunted the footsteps of your anointed. ****52**** Blessed be the LORD forever. Amen and Amen.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                               | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                                   |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Steadfast Love and Faithfulness of the Moral Order**** | The psalmist declares that the moral order's steadfast love is established forever and its faithfulness is as firm as the heavens. |
| ****Principle 2: The Experience of Rejection and Defeat****                 | The psalmist laments that the king has been rejected, the covenant seems broken, and the people are suffering defeat.              |
| ****Principle 3: The Plea for Remembrance and Restoration****               | The psalmist pleads for the moral order to remember its covenant and to restore the people.                                        |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 89**

#### **Principle 1: The Steadfast Love and Faithfulness of the Moral Order**

| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 89:1–2): "I will sing of your steadfast love, O LORD, forever; with my mouth I will proclaim your faithfulness to all generations." | The moral order's steadfast love and faithfulness are enduring and reliable.          |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order is reliable and leads to rejoicing.                                                    | The path of the moral order is reliable and enduring.                                 |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                                       | The moral order preserves and provides for the devoted. Its faithfulness is reliable. |

But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                       | The moral order is sufficient and faithful.                                           |

Those who trust in the Divine are never abandoned."                                                                                                                                | The Divine's love and faithfulness are enduring.                                      |

Its steadfast love is enduring."                                                                                                                                           | The moral order's steadfast love is enduring.                                         |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors' love and faithfulness endure. Those who honour the ancestors are never forgotten."                                                                                                                                       | The ancestors' love and faithfulness are enduring.                                    |

(Corollary from Psalm 89:38–45): The psalmist laments that the king has been rejected and the people are suffering defeat.                                                                                          | The faithful may experience the apparent failure of promises and the suffering of defeat. |

But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition." | The good may experience defeat and suffering.                                             |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Mahabharata): The Pandavas experience defeat and exile.                                                       | The righteous may experience defeat and suffering.                                        |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:155–156): "And We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger and loss of wealth and lives and fruits, but give good tidings to the patient."                                                                                                                      | The faithful will be tested with defeat and loss.                                         |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a place of suffering. Even the community of the faithful may experience defeat. The true devotee does not lose faith."                                                                                                                                      | The faithful may experience defeat.                                                       |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble community may face defeat. The moral order is not always immediately visible."                                                                                                                                                                                | The virtuous community may face defeat.                                                   |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors see the suffering of the community. Even when the community faces defeat, the ancestors know the truth. The suffering community is not forgotten."                                                                                                           | The community may face defeat, but the ancestors see their suffering.                     |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the steadfast love and faithfulness of the moral order and the experience of rejection and defeat. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order's steadfast love and faithfulness are enduring and reliable.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that steadfast love is established forever and faithfulness is as firm as the heavens (Psalm 89), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order's love and faithfulness endure, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is reliable, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine's love endures, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order's steadfast love is enduring, or as the African teaching that the ancestors' love and faithfulness endure, all traditions teach that ****the moral order's steadfast love and faithfulness are reliable and enduring****. They are a foundation for hope.

****Second, the faithful may experience the apparent failure of promises and the suffering of defeat.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's lament over the rejection of the king and the suffering of defeat (Psalm 89), as the Jewish teaching that the faithful may experience defeat, as the Buddhist teaching that the good may suffer, as the Pandavas' defeat (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful will be tested, as the Sikh teaching that the faithful may experience defeat, as the Confucian recognition that the virtuous community may face defeat, or as the African understanding that the community may face defeat but the ancestors see their suffering, all traditions teach that ****the faithful are not immune to defeat and the apparent failure of promises****. This is a testing of faith.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the steadfast love of the moral order while acknowledging the reality of defeat provide a moral framework that sustains hope through apparent failure. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as they are less likely to abandon their faith in times of defeat and more likely to trust in the moral order's enduring love. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****hope through apparent failure****—a quality that favours long-term survival.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 89, Principle 1 ("The steadfast love and faithfulness of the moral order") and Principle 2 ("The experience of rejection and defeat") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration of steadfast love (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order's love endures, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is reliable, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine's love endures, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order's steadfast love is enduring, and as the African teaching that the ancestors' love and faithfulness endure.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the steadfast love of the moral order and the reality of defeat.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order's love and faithfulness endure, even when the faithful experience defeat—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 90: The Ethics of Wisdom and the Brevity of Life**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 90 is a wisdom psalm attributed to Moses, making it the oldest psalm in the Psalter. It reflects on the eternal nature of the moral order and the brevity and fragility of human life. The psalmist declares that the moral order has been the dwelling place of the people for all generations, while human life is like a dream, like grass that flourishes in the morning and withers in the evening. The psalmist asks for wisdom to number their days and to gain a heart of wisdom, and pleads for the moral order to have compassion and to establish the work of their hands.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Prayer of Moses, the man of God.****

****1**** Lord, you have been our dwelling place in all generations. ****2**** Before the mountains were brought forth, or ever you had formed the earth and the world, from everlasting to everlasting you are God. ****3**** You turn us back to dust, and say, "Turn back, you mortals." ****4**** For a thousand years in your sight are like yesterday when it is past, or like a watch in the night. ****5**** You sweep them away; they are like a dream, like grass that is renewed in the morning; ****6**** in the morning it flourishes and is renewed; in the evening it fades and withers. ****7**** For we are consumed by your anger; by your wrath we are overwhelmed. ****8**** You have set our iniquities before you, our secret sins in the light of your countenance. ****9**** For all our days pass away under your wrath; our years come to an end like a sigh. ****10**** The days of our life are seventy years, or perhaps eighty, if we are strong; even then their span is only toil and trouble; they are soon gone, and we fly away. ****11**** Who considers the power of your anger? Your wrath is as great as the fear that is due you. ****12**** So teach us to count our days that we may gain a wise heart. ****13**** Turn, O LORD! How long? Have compassion on your servants! ****14**** Satisfy us in the morning with your steadfast love, so that we may rejoice and be glad all our days. ****15**** Make us glad as many days as you have afflicted us, and as many years as we have seen evil. ****16**** Let your work be manifest to your servants, and your glorious power to their children. ****17**** Let the favor of the Lord our God be upon us, and prosper for us the work of our hands— O prosper the work of our hands!

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                  |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Eternal Nature of the Moral Order****   | The moral order is from everlasting to everlasting; human life is fleeting in comparison.         |
| ****Principle 2: The Brevity and Fragility of Human Life**** | Human life is like a dream, like grass that flourishes in the morning and withers in the evening. |
| ****Principle 3: The Prayer for Wisdom and Compassion****    | The psalmist asks for wisdom to number their days and for the moral order's compassion and mercy. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 90**

#### **Principle 2: The Brevity and Fragility of Human Life**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                                       |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin: Know from where you came, where you are going, and before whom you are destined to give account." (Corollary from Psalm 90 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a meditation on human mortality.                                              | Human life is brief and fragile. Reflection on mortality leads to wisdom.                                      |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 62): "I have sons, I have wealth"—thinking thus, the fool is troubled. But the fool himself is not his own; how can sons or wealth be his?" | Life is fleeting and possessions are temporary. The wise person does not become attached.                      |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 2:14: "The contacts of the senses with the sense objects... are the source of heat and cold, and of pleasure and pain. They come and go and are impermanent." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 2:27): "For one who has taken birth, death is certain; and for one who has died, birth is certain."                                                      | Life is impermanent. The wise person does not become attached to fleeting things.                              |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Hadid (57:20): "Know that the life of this world is but amusement and diversion and adornment and boasting to one another and competition in increase of wealth and children... And what is the worldly life except the enjoyment of delusion."                                                                                                   | Life is temporary and ultimately a delusion. The wise person does not become attached to it.                   |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The world is a passing show. Life is like a dream. The wise person does not become attached to worldly things, for they are temporary."                                                                                                                                                                                           | Life is like a dream; worldly things are temporary.                                                            |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is not attached to wealth or status, for these are fleeting. They are attached to virtue, which endures."                                                                                                                                                                                                       | Wealth and status are fleeting; virtue endures.                                                                |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "Life is like a river; it flows and does not return. The ancestors teach that what you accumulate in this life stays behind. Value what is eternal: the community and the ancestors."                                                                                                                                                | Life is temporary; material possessions do not follow. The wise person values the community and the ancestors. |

#### **Principle 3: The Prayer for Wisdom and Compassion**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                              |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 90:12): "So teach us to count our days that we may gain a wise heart."                                        | The faithful pray for wisdom to live rightly and for compassion from the moral order. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 168): "Rouse yourself, be diligent! Follow the teaching of the Buddha."                            | The faithful pray for diligence and wisdom on the path.                               |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                            | The faithful pray for preservation and compassion from the moral order.               |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me." (Corollary from Surah Al-Fatihah 1:6–7): "Guide us to the straight path." | The faithful pray for guidance and compassion from the moral order.                   |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee prays for wisdom and for the Divine's compassion. The one who seeks wisdom is blessed."                                                                                                        | The faithful pray for wisdom and compassion from the Divine.                          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person prays for wisdom and for the moral order's favor. The one who seeks wisdom is virtuous."                                                                                                      | The faithful pray for wisdom and favor from the moral order.                          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The faithful pray to the ancestors for wisdom and for compassion. The ancestors bless those who seek wisdom."                                                                                                     | The faithful pray for wisdom and compassion from the ancestors.                       |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the brevity of human life and the prayer for wisdom and compassion. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, human life is brief and fragile.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's meditation on the fleetingness of human life like grass that withers (Psalm 90), as the Jewish teaching that reflection on mortality leads to wisdom, as the Buddhist teaching that life is fleeting and possessions are temporary, as the Gita's teaching that pleasure and pain are impermanent, as the Qur'anic teaching that worldly life is a delusion, as the Sikh teaching that life is like a dream, as the Confucian teaching that wealth and status are fleeting, or as the African teaching that life is like a river that flows and does not return, all traditions teach that ****human life is brief and fragile****. The wise person does not become attached to temporary things.

****Second, the faithful pray for wisdom and compassion.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's prayer to count their days and gain a wise heart (Psalm 90), as the Jewish teaching that wisdom is sought through prayer, as the Buddhist teaching of diligence and seeking the path, as the Gita's teaching of seeking preservation and compassion, as the Qur'anic teaching of seeking guidance, as the Sikh teaching of seeking wisdom from the Divine, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person seeks wisdom and favor, or as the African teaching that the faithful pray for wisdom and compassion from the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****the faithful actively seek wisdom and compassion from the moral order****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the brevity of life and the importance of seeking wisdom and compassion provide a moral framework that encourages humility, reflection, and dependence on the moral order. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to be driven by the pursuit of temporary wealth and status, and more likely to seek wisdom and compassion. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****humility and wisdom-seeking****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 90, Principle 2 ("The brevity and fragility of human life") and Principle 3 ("The prayer for wisdom and compassion") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's meditation on the fleetingness of life (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that reflection on mortality leads to wisdom, as the Buddhist teaching that life is fleeting, as the Gita's teaching that pleasure and pain are impermanent (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that worldly life is a delusion, as the Sikh teaching that life is like a dream, as the Confucian teaching that wealth and status are fleeting, and as the African teaching that life is like a river that flows and does not return.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the brevity of human life and the prayer for wisdom and compassion.**** The ethical lesson—that human life is brief and fragile, and that the faithful pray for wisdom and compassion—transcends its religious packaging.

Chpater 10 — Psalms 91–100\*\*

## **Psalm 91: The Ethics of Trust in Divine Protection**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 91 is a psalm of trust and confidence in the moral order's protection. It is a powerful declaration of security for those who dwell in the shelter of the Most High. The psalm describes protection from terror, plague, and danger, with the assurance that the moral order will command angels to guard the faithful. The psalm concludes with the moral order's promise to deliver, protect, and satisfy those who love it and call upon it.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** You who live in the shelter of the Most High, who abide in the shadow of the Almighty, ****2**** will say to the LORD, "My refuge and my fortress; my God, in whom I trust." ****3**** For he will deliver you from the snare of the fowler and from the deadly pestilence; ****4**** he will cover you with his pinions, and under his wings you will find refuge; his faithfulness is a shield and buckler. ****5**** You will not fear the terror of the night, or the arrow that flies by day, ****6**** or the pestilence that stalks in darkness, or the destruction that wastes at noonday. ****7**** A thousand may fall at your side, ten thousand at your right hand, but it will not come near you. ****8**** You will only look with your eyes and see the punishment of the wicked. ****9**** Because you have made the LORD your refuge, the Most High your dwelling place, ****10**** no evil shall befall you, no scourge come near your tent. ****11**** For he will command his angels concerning you to guard you in all your ways. ****12**** On their hands they will bear you up, so that you will not dash your foot against a stone. ****13**** You will tread on the lion and the adder, the young lion and the serpent you will trample under foot. ****14**** Those who love me, I will deliver; I will protect those who know my name. ****15**** When they call to me, I will answer them; I will be with them in trouble, I will rescue them and honor them. ****16**** With long life I will satisfy them, and show them my salvation.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                              | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                               |
| ---------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: Trust in the Moral Order's Protection**** | Those who dwell in the shelter of the moral order find safety and refuge. Trust is the foundation of security. |
| ****Principle 2: Deliverance from Fear and Danger****      | The moral order delivers from terror, plague, and danger. The faithful need not fear.                          |
| ****Principle 3: The Assurance of Deliverance****          | The moral order promises to deliver and protect those who love it and call upon it.                            |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 91**

(Corollary from Psalm 91 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is recited as a prayer for protection and trust.              | Those who trust in the moral order find safety and protection.  |

(Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): The path of the moral order provides refuge and joy.                                         | The path of the moral order provides refuge and protection.     |

But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'" | The moral order is sufficient as a refuge and protector.        |

When they call to me, I will answer them." | The moral order promises deliverance and protection to those who love it and call upon it. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order leads to deliverance and rejoicing.                                                 | The path of the moral order leads to deliverance and joy for those who follow it.          |

(Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                                               | The moral order preserves and protects the devoted.                                        |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me."                                                                                              | The moral order responds to those who call upon it and promises deliverance.               |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine hears the cries of the faithful and promises deliverance. Those who love the Divine are protected."                                                                                                                     | The Divine promises deliverance and protection to those who love it.                       |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order responds to the virtuous. The noble person who trusts in the moral order will be delivered."                                                                                                                      | The moral order delivers and protects the virtuous who trust in it.                        |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors respond to those who honour them and promise protection. The faithful who call upon the ancestors are heard."                                                                                                          | The ancestors promise protection to those who honour and call upon them.                   |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding trust in the moral order's protection and the assurance of deliverance. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, those who trust in the moral order find safety and protection.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that those who dwell in the shelter of the Most High find refuge (Psalm 91), as the Jewish teaching that trust leads to protection, as the Buddhist teaching that the path provides refuge, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is a refuge, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is a refuge for the noble person, or as the African teaching that the ancestors are a refuge, all traditions teach that ****trust in the moral order brings safety and protection****.

****Second, the moral order promises deliverance to those who love it and call upon it.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order will deliver those who love it and protect those who know its name (Psalm 91), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order responds to those who call, as the Buddhist teaching that the path leads to deliverance and rejoicing, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine promises deliverance, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order delivers the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors respond to those who honour them, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is faithful to those who love it and call upon it****. It promises deliverance and protection.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach trust in the moral order's protection and the assurance of deliverance provide a moral framework that sustains hope and reduces fear. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to be paralyzed by fear and more likely to trust in the moral order's protection. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust and hope****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 91, Principle 1 ("Trust in the moral order's protection") and Principle 3 ("The assurance of deliverance") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration of trust in the shelter of the Most High (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that trust leads to protection, as the Buddhist teaching that the path provides refuge, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is a refuge, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is a refuge, and as the African teaching that the ancestors are a refuge.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning trust in the moral order's protection and the assurance of deliverance.**** The ethical lesson—that trust in the moral order brings safety and protection, and that the moral order promises deliverance to those who love it—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 92: The Ethics of Thanksgiving and the Contrast Between the Wicked and the Righteous**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 92 is a psalm of thanksgiving and praise, designated as a song for the Sabbath. It declares that it is good to give thanks to the moral order and to proclaim its steadfast love in the morning and its faithfulness by night. The psalm reflects on the prosperity of the wicked, which is temporary, and the flourishing of the righteous, which is enduring like the palm tree and the cedar. The psalm concludes with the affirmation that the moral order is upright and there is no unrighteousness in it.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Psalm. A Song for the Sabbath Day.****

****1**** It is good to give thanks to the LORD, to sing praises to your name, O Most High; ****2**** to declare your steadfast love in the morning, and your faithfulness by night, ****3**** to the music of the lute and the harp, to the melody of the lyre. ****4**** For you, O LORD, have made me glad by your work; at the works of your hands I sing for joy. ****5**** How great are your works, O LORD! Your thoughts are very deep! ****6**** The dullard cannot know, the stupid cannot understand this: ****7**** though the wicked sprout like grass and all evildoers flourish, they are doomed to destruction forever, ****8**** but you, O LORD, are on high forever. ****9**** For your enemies, O LORD, for your enemies shall perish; all evildoers shall be scattered. ****10**** But you have exalted my horn like that of the wild ox; you have poured over me fresh oil. ****11**** My eyes have seen the downfall of my enemies; my ears have heard the doom of my evil assailants. ****12**** The righteous flourish like the palm tree, and grow like a cedar in Lebanon. ****13**** They are planted in the house of the LORD; they flourish in the courts of our God. ****14**** In old age they still produce fruit; they are always green and full of sap, ****15**** showing that the LORD is upright; he is my rock, and there is no unrighteousness in him.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                  | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                           |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Goodness of Thanksgiving and Praise****   | It is good to give thanks and praise the moral order for its works and faithfulness.                       |
| ****Principle 2: The Temporary Prosperity of the Wicked****    | The wicked may sprout and flourish like grass, but they are doomed to destruction forever.                 |
| ****Principle 3: The Enduring Flourishing of the Righteous**** | The righteous flourish like palm trees and cedars; they bear fruit even in old age and remain full of sap. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 92**

(Corollary from Psalm 92:7): "Though the wicked sprout like grass and all evildoers flourish, they are doomed to destruction forever."                                                                                                                                              | The prosperity of the wicked is temporary. They will ultimately be destroyed.          |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the temporary prosperity of the wicked and the enduring flourishing of the righteous. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the prosperity of the wicked is temporary.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the wicked sprout like grass but are doomed to destruction (Psalm 92), as the Jewish teaching that the wicked will ultimately be destroyed, as the Buddhist teaching that the evil-doer will eventually suffer, as the Gita's teaching that the arrogant and wicked face destruction, as the Qur'anic teaching that the wicked who trust in wealth are deluded, as the Sikh teaching that the proud wither like flowers, as the Confucian teaching that harm returns to the one who causes it, or as the African teaching that the oppressor's prosperity is temporary, all traditions teach that ****the prosperity of the wicked is temporary and will not endure****.

****Second, the righteous flourish and endure.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's image of the righteous flourishing like palm trees and cedars (Psalm 92), as the Jewish teaching that the righteous endure and bear fruit even in old age, as the Buddhist teaching that the good person ultimately rejoices, as the Gita's teaching that the sincere striver does not perish, as the Qur'anic teaching that the righteous are like a good tree firmly rooted, as the Sikh teaching that the faithful flourish like fruitful trees, as the Confucian teaching that virtue brings its own reward, or as the African teaching that the righteous are blessed and flourish, all traditions teach that ****the righteous endure and flourish****. Their flourishing is not temporary but enduring.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the temporary prosperity of the wicked and the enduring flourishing of the righteous provide a moral framework that sustains hope and encourages righteousness. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to envy the wicked and more likely to trust in the moral order's ultimate justice. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****hope and patience****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 92, Principle 2 ("The temporary prosperity of the wicked") and Principle 3 ("The enduring flourishing of the righteous") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration that the wicked sprout like grass but are doomed to destruction (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the wicked will ultimately be destroyed, as the Buddhist teaching that the evil-doer will eventually suffer, as the Gita's teaching that the arrogant face destruction (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the wicked who trust in wealth are deluded, as the Sikh teaching that the proud wither like flowers, as the Confucian teaching that harm returns to the one who causes it, and as the African teaching that the oppressor's prosperity is temporary.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the temporary prosperity of the wicked and the enduring flourishing of the righteous.**** The ethical lesson—that the prosperity of the wicked is temporary, while the righteous endure and flourish—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 93: The Ethics of Sovereignty and the Surety of Divine Decrees**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 93 is a royal psalm celebrating the moral order's sovereign reign over creation. It declares that the moral order is king, robed in majesty and armed with strength. The psalm affirms that the world is established and cannot be moved, and that the moral order's throne is established from of old. The psalm concludes with the declaration that the moral order's decrees are very sure, and that holiness adorns its house forever.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** The LORD is king; he is robed in majesty; the LORD is robed; he is girded with strength. He has established the world; it shall never be moved; ****2**** your throne is established from of old; you are from everlasting. ****3**** The floods have lifted up, O LORD, the floods have lifted up their voice; the floods lift up their roaring. ****4**** More majestic than the thunders of mighty waters, more majestic than the waves of the sea, majestic on high is the LORD! ****5**** Your decrees are very sure; holiness befits your house, O LORD, forevermore.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                      |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Sovereignty of the Moral Order****      | The moral order is king, robed in majesty and armed with strength. It is sovereign over all creation. |
| ****Principle 2: The Established Creation****                | The world is established and cannot be moved. The moral order's creation is stable and enduring.      |
| ****Principle 3: The Surety of the Moral Order's Decrees**** | The moral order's decrees are very sure and reliable. The moral order's word is trustworthy.          |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 93**

#### **Principle 1: The Sovereignty of the Moral Order**

| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 93 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a declaration of God's kingship. | The moral order is sovereign. It is king over all creation. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 1: "Mind precedes all mental states. Mind is their chief; they are all mind-wrought." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 16): The moral order of karma is sovereign over all beings.                  | The moral order of cause and effect is sovereign over all.  |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 10:20: "I am the Self, O Gudakesha, seated in the hearts of all creatures. I am the beginning, the middle, and the end of all beings."                                                                   | The moral order is sovereign, present in all beings.        |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:115): "To Allah belongs the east and the west. So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah."                                                                                          | The moral order is sovereign, and all belongs to it.        |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The One Divine is the sovereign of all. There is no other."                                                                                                                           | The Divine is the sovereign of all.                         |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is universal and sovereign over all."                                                                                                                                | The moral order is sovereign over all.                      |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are the guardians of the moral order for their people, but the moral order itself is sovereign over all."                                                                 | The moral order is sovereign over all peoples.              |

#### **Principle 3: The Surety of the Moral Order's Decrees**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | **Reduced Ethical Core**                             |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 93:5): "Your decrees are very sure; holiness befits your house, O LORD, forevermore."                                                                                                                                                                         | The moral order's decrees are reliable and sure.     |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | The moral order of karma is sure and reliable.       |
