I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                                                                                                                                                | The moral order's intervention is sure and reliable. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:164): "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth... are signs for a people who reason."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | The moral order's signs and decrees are sure.        |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine's word is true and reliable. The decrees of the Divine are sure."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | The Divine's decrees are sure and reliable.          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order's way is sure and reliable. The noble person trusts in it."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | The moral order's way is sure and reliable.          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors' teachings are sure and reliable. The community trusts in them."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | The ancestors' teachings are sure and reliable.      |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the sovereignty of the moral order and the surety of its decrees. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order is sovereign over all creation.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the LORD is king, robed in majesty and strength (Psalm 93), as the Jewish teaching that God is king, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order of karma is sovereign, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order is present in all beings, as the Qur'anic teaching that all belongs to the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is the sovereign of all, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is universal, or as the African teaching that the moral order is sovereign over all peoples, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is sovereign over all creation****. It is not limited to one nation or people.

****Second, the moral order's decrees are sure and reliable.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order's decrees are very sure (Psalm 93), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order's decrees are reliable, as the Buddhist teaching that the law of karma is sure, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order's intervention is reliable, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order's signs are sure, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine's word is reliable, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order's way is sure, or as the African teaching that the ancestors' teachings are sure and reliable, all traditions teach that ****the moral order's decrees are reliable****. The faithful can trust in its word.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the sovereignty of the moral order and the surety of its decrees provide a moral framework that encourages trust and obedience. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are more likely to trust in the moral order's reliability and to follow its decrees. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****trust and obedience****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 93, Principle 1 ("The sovereignty of the moral order") and Principle 3 ("The surety of the moral order's decrees") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration of God's kingship (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that God is king, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order of karma is sovereign, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order is present in all beings (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that all belongs to the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is sovereign, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is universal, and as the African teaching that the moral order is sovereign over all peoples.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the sovereignty of the moral order and the surety of its decrees.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order is sovereign and its decrees are reliable—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 94: The Ethics of Divine Awareness and Protection of the Faithful**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 94 is a psalm of lament and imprecation, calling upon the moral order to act as the judge of the earth and to repay the wicked for their deeds. The psalmist laments the arrogance and oppression of the wicked, who boast that the moral order does not see or care. The psalmist affirms that the moral order is the one who planted the ear and formed the eye, and that it will not ignore injustice. The psalm concludes with a declaration that the moral order will give respite to the faithful and that the wicked will be cut off.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** O LORD, you God of vengeance, you God of vengeance, shine forth! ****2**** Rise up, O judge of the earth; give to the proud what they deserve! ****3**** O LORD, how long shall the wicked, how long shall the wicked exult? ****4**** They pour out their arrogant words; all the evildoers boast. ****5**** They crush your people, O LORD, and afflict your heritage. ****6**** They kill the widow and the stranger; they murder the orphan, ****7**** and they say, "The LORD does not see; the God of Jacob does not perceive." ****8**** Understand, O dullest among the people; fools, when will you be wise? ****9**** He who planted the ear, does he not hear? He who formed the eye, does he not see? ****10**** He who disciplines the nations, he who teaches knowledge to humankind, does he not chastise? ****11**** The LORD knows our thoughts, that they are but an empty breath. ****12**** Happy are those whom you discipline, O LORD, and whom you teach out of your law, ****13**** giving them respite from days of trouble, until a pit is dug for the wicked. ****14**** For the LORD will not forsake his people; he will not abandon his heritage; ****15**** for justice will return to the righteous, and all the upright in heart will follow it. ****16**** Who rises up for me against the wicked? Who stands up for me against evildoers? ****17**** If the LORD had not been my help, my soul would soon have lived in the land of silence. ****18**** When I thought, "My foot is slipping," your steadfast love, O LORD, held me up. ****19**** When the cares of my heart are many, your consolations cheer my soul. ****20**** Can wicked rulers be allied with you, those who contrive mischief by statute? ****21**** They band together against the life of the righteous, and condemn the innocent to death. ****22**** But the LORD has become my stronghold, and my God the rock of my refuge. ****23**** He will repay them for their iniquity and wipe them out for their wickedness; the LORD our God will wipe them out.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                  | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                            |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Call for Divine Judgment****              | The psalmist calls upon the moral order to judge the earth and repay the wicked for their deeds.            |
| ****Principle 2: The Moral Order's Knowledge and Awareness**** | The moral order planted the ear and formed the eye; it sees, hears, and knows all.                          |
| ****Principle 3: The Protection of the Faithful****            | The moral order will not forsake its people; it will give respite and become a stronghold for the faithful. |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 94**

He who formed the eye, does he not see?"                                                                         | The moral order sees and hears all. Nothing is hidden from it.  |

(Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 119): The moral order of karma knows all actions and intentions.                                                                                            | The moral order of cause and effect sees and knows all actions. |

I am the beginning, the middle, and the end of all beings."                                                                                                                                                  | The moral order is present in all, knowing all.                 |

(Corollary from Surah Al-Mulk 67:13–14): "And conceal your speech or declare it; indeed, He knows what is within the breasts." | The moral order knows all, even what is hidden in the heart.    |

Nothing is hidden from the Divine."                                                                                                                                                                                               | The Divine is all-knowing and all-seeing.                       |

The virtuous are seen; the wicked are seen."                                                                                                                                                                                | The moral order is aware of all.                                |

They know the truth."                                                                                                                                                                                     | The ancestors see and know all.                                 |

#### **Principle 3: The Protection of the Faithful**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                 |

| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | The good person is ultimately protected and rejoices.    |

| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                                                                        | The moral order is sufficient and protects the faithful. |

The noble person trusts in the moral order's protection."                                                                                                                                                                                      | The moral order protects the virtuous.                   |

Those who honour the ancestors are protected."                                                                                                                                                                                                       | The ancestors protect the faithful.                      |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the moral order's knowledge and awareness and its protection of the faithful. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order sees, hears, and knows all.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's affirmation that the one who planted the ear and formed the eye sees and hears all (Psalm 94), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order sees and hears all, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order of karma knows all actions, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order is present in all beings, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order knows what is hidden in the heart, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is all-knowing, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is aware of all, or as the African teaching that the ancestors see all, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is aware of all actions and intentions****. Nothing is hidden from it.

****Second, the moral order protects the faithful.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order will not forsake its people and is a stronghold for the faithful (Psalm 94), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order will not abandon its heritage, as the Buddhist teaching that the good person ultimately rejoices, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine protects the faithful, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order protects the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors protect the faithful, all traditions teach that ****the moral order protects those who trust in it****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the moral order's awareness and its protection of the faithful provide a moral framework that encourages accountability and trust. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are aware that their actions are seen and that the moral order protects the faithful. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****accountability and trust****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 94, Principle 2 ("The moral order's knowledge and awareness") and Principle 3 ("The protection of the faithful") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's affirmation that the one who planted the ear and formed the eye sees and hears all (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order sees and hears all, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order of karma knows all actions, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order is present in all beings (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order knows what is hidden in the heart, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is all-knowing, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is aware of all, and as the African teaching that the ancestors see all.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the moral order's awareness and its protection of the faithful.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order sees all and protects the faithful—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 95: The Ethics of Worship and the Warning Against Hardening Hearts**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 95 is a call to worship and obedience, inviting the people to sing joyfully to the moral order and to bow down in reverence. It celebrates the moral order as the great King and the Creator of all, the one who holds the depths of the earth and the heights of the mountains. The psalm also includes a warning: do not harden hearts as the ancestors did at Meribah and Massah, where they tested the moral order despite seeing its works. The psalm concludes with a solemn declaration that the rebellious will not enter the moral order's rest.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** O come, let us sing to the LORD; let us make a joyful noise to the rock of our salvation! ****2**** Let us come into his presence with thanksgiving; let us make a joyful noise to him with songs of praise! ****3**** For the LORD is a great God, and a great King above all gods. ****4**** In his hand are the depths of the earth; the heights of the mountains are his also. ****5**** The sea is his, for he made it, and the dry land, which his hands have formed. ****6**** O come, let us worship and bow down; let us kneel before the LORD, our Maker! ****7**** For he is our God, and we are the people of his pasture, and the sheep of his hand. O that today you would listen to his voice! ****8**** Do not harden your hearts, as at Meribah, as on the day at Massah in the wilderness, ****9**** when your ancestors tested me, and put me to the proof, though they had seen my work. ****10**** For forty years I loathed that generation and said, "They are a people whose hearts go astray, and they do not regard my ways." ****11**** Therefore in my anger I swore, "They shall not enter my rest."

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                        | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                       |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Call to Worship****                             | The people are called to sing joyfully, give thanks, and bow down to the moral order as the Creator and King.          |
| ****Principle 2: The Warning Against Hardening Hearts****            | The faithful are warned not to harden their hearts as the ancestors did at Meribah, where they tested the moral order. |
| ****Principle 3: The Promise of Rest and the Danger of Rebellion**** | The obedient will enter the moral order's rest, but the rebellious will not.                                           |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 95**

#### **Principle 1: The Call to Worship**

| --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 95 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a call to worship and obedience.                   | The faithful are called to worship the moral order.                |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order is to be followed with joy and reverence.                | The faithful are called to follow the path with joy and reverence. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 18:66): "Do not fear."                                    | The faithful are called to worship the moral order with devotion.  |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:177): "Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward the east or the west, but \[true\] righteousness is \[in\] one who believes in Allah... and gives wealth... to relatives, orphans, the needy..." | The faithful are called to worship and act righteously.            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee worships the Divine with joy and reverence. Worship is the foundation of devotion."                                                                                                    | The faithful are called to worship the Divine.                     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who reveres the moral order and seeks to align with it. Worship and reverence are essential."                                                                                  | The faithful are called to revere and worship the moral order.     |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The faithful honour the ancestors with reverence and worship. To honour the ancestors is to honour the moral order."                                                                                      | The faithful are called to honour and worship the ancestors.       |

#### **Principle 3: The Warning Against Hardening Hearts**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                  |
| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 95:8): "Do not harden your hearts, as at Meribah, as on the day at Massah in the wilderness."                                              | The faithful are warned against hardening their hearts through rebellion. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 62): The fool is troubled by attachment and ignorance. | Hardening the heart through ignorance leads to suffering.                 |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 16:18–20: "Given over to egoism, power, arrogance, lust, and anger, the demonic hate Me... I hurl these haters, cruel and the vilest among men, into demonic wombs in the cycle of birth and death."                                                            | Hardening the heart through arrogance leads to destruction.               |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:61): "And \[recall\] when you said, 'O Moses, we can never endure one \[kind of\] food. So call upon your Lord to bring forth for us from the earth its green herbs and its cucumbers and its garlic and its lentils and its onions.'"            | The people hardened their hearts through complaint and lack of gratitude. |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee does not harden their heart but remains open to the Divine. The stubborn heart leads to separation."                                                                                                                        | Hardening the heart leads to separation from the Divine.                  |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is open and receptive; the small person is stubborn and closed. The closed heart leads to error."                                                                                                                          | Hardening the heart through stubbornness leads to error.                  |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors warn against hardening the heart. A closed heart cannot hear the ancestors' voice. Openness and humility are essential."                                                                                                         | Hardening the heart closes one off from the ancestors.                    |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the call to worship and the warning against hardening hearts. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful are called to worship the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's call to sing joyfully and bow down to the Creator (Psalm 95), as the Jewish teaching that worship is due to the moral order, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is to be followed with joy and reverence, as the Gita's teaching that devotion and worship are essential, as the Qur'anic teaching that righteousness includes worship and action, as the Sikh teaching that worship is the foundation of devotion, as the Confucian teaching that reverence and worship are essential, or as the African teaching that honouring the ancestors is worship, all traditions teach that ****worship of the moral order is a fundamental duty****.

****Second, the faithful are warned against hardening their hearts.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's warning not to harden hearts as at Meribah (Psalm 95), as the Jewish teaching that hardening the heart leads to rebellion, as the Buddhist teaching that ignorance leads to suffering, as the Gita's teaching that arrogance leads to destruction, as the Qur'anic teaching that complaint and ingratitude harden the heart, as the Sikh teaching that the stubborn heart leads to separation, as the Confucian teaching that the closed heart leads to error, or as the African teaching that a closed heart cannot hear the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****hardening the heart through rebellion, arrogance, or ingratitude is a grave danger****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach worship and warn against hardening hearts provide a moral framework that encourages openness, humility, and obedience. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are less likely to rebel against the moral order and more likely to remain open and receptive. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****worship and openness****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 95, Principle 1 ("The call to worship") and Principle 3 ("The warning against hardening hearts") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's call to sing and bow down (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that worship is due to the moral order, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is to be followed with joy and reverence, as the Gita's teaching that devotion is essential (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that righteousness includes worship, as the Sikh teaching that worship is the foundation of devotion, as the Confucian teaching that reverence is essential, and as the African teaching that honouring the ancestors is worship.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the call to worship and the warning against hardening hearts.**** The ethical lesson—that worship is a duty and that hardening hearts is a danger—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 96: The Ethics of Universal Praise and the Coming Judgment**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 96 is a call to universal praise, inviting all the earth to sing to the moral order. It declares that the moral order is great and greatly to be praised, more to be feared than all gods, for the gods of the peoples are idols. The psalm celebrates the moral order's creative power, its glory, and its majesty. It calls upon the families of the peoples to ascribe glory and strength to the moral order, and it announces that the moral order is coming to judge the earth with righteousness and truth.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** O sing to the LORD a new song; sing to the LORD, all the earth. ****2**** Sing to the LORD, bless his name; tell of his salvation from day to day. ****3**** Declare his glory among the nations, his marvelous works among all the peoples. ****4**** For great is the LORD, and greatly to be praised; he is to be revered above all gods. ****5**** For all the gods of the peoples are idols, but the LORD made the heavens. ****6**** Honor and majesty are before him; strength and beauty are in his sanctuary. ****7**** Ascribe to the LORD, O families of the peoples, ascribe to the LORD glory and strength. ****8**** Ascribe to the LORD the glory due his name; bring an offering, and come into his courts. ****9**** Worship the LORD in holy splendor; tremble before him, all the earth. ****10**** Say among the nations, "The LORD is king! The world is firmly established; it shall never be moved. He will judge the peoples with equity." ****11**** Let the heavens be glad, and let the earth rejoice; let the sea roar, and all that fills it; ****12**** let the field exult, and everything in it. Then shall all the trees of the forest sing for joy ****13**** before the LORD; for he is coming, for he is coming to judge the earth. He will judge the world with righteousness, and the peoples with his truth.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                                   | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                               |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Call to Universal Praise****                               | All the earth is called to sing to the moral order and ascribe glory and strength to it.                       |
| ****Principle 2: The Greatness of the Moral Order and the Futility of Idols**** | The moral order is great and more to be feared than all gods; the gods of the peoples are idols and worthless. |
| ****Principle 3: The Coming Judgment****                                        | The moral order is coming to judge the earth with righteousness and truth.                                     |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 96**

| --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 96 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a call to universal praise. | All peoples are called to praise the moral order.                            |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The path of the moral order is open to all.                   | All who follow the path rejoice and praise.                                  |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:11: "As people approach me, so I receive them. All paths, Arjuna, lead to me."                                                                                                                    | All peoples, through their various paths, ultimately praise the moral order. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:115): "To Allah belongs the east and the west. So wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah."                                                                                     | All peoples are called to praise the moral order.                            |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The One Divine is praised by all peoples. All are children of the One."                                                                                                          | All peoples praise the One Divine.                                           |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is universal and is honoured by all peoples who seek virtue."                                                                                                   | All peoples honour the moral order.                                          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "All peoples praise the ancestors in their own way. The moral order is honoured by all communities."                                                                                | All peoples honour the moral order through their ancestors.                  |

#### **Principle 3: The Coming Judgment**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                                       |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 96:13): "He will judge the world with righteousness, and the peoples with his truth."                                                                                                                                                             | The moral order will judge the earth with righteousness and truth.                             |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): "The good person may experience suffering as long as his good has not come to fruition. But when his good bears fruit, then the good person experiences happiness." | The moral order brings about the fruits of deeds. Judgment is inherent in the order of things. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                                                                                                                                    | The moral order intervenes in history to bring judgment.                                       |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:185): "Every soul will taste death, and you will only be given your reward in full on the Day of Resurrection."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | The moral order will judge on the Day of Resurrection.                                         |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the judge of all. The proud will be humbled, and the humble will be exalted. Judgment belongs to the Divine."                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | The Divine will judge all.                                                                     |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is the ultimate judge. It brings down the arrogant and exalts the virtuous."                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | The moral order judges, bringing down the arrogant and exalting the virtuous.                  |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors judge the community. The proud and oppressive will be brought down; the humble and just will be lifted up."                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | The ancestors judge, bringing down the proud and exalting the just.                            |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the call to universal praise and the coming judgment. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, all peoples are called to praise the moral order.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's call to all the earth to sing to the moral order (Psalm 96), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is worthy of universal praise, as the Buddhist teaching that all who follow the path rejoice and praise, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that all belongs to the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that all peoples praise the One, as the Confucian teaching that all peoples honour the moral order, or as the African teaching that all peoples honour the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****praise of the moral order is a universal duty****.

****Second, the moral order will judge the earth with righteousness and truth.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is coming to judge the earth (Psalm 96), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order will judge with righteousness and truth, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order brings about the fruits of deeds, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order intervenes to deliver the good and destroy the wicked, as the Qur'anic teaching of the Day of Resurrection, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is the judge of all, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order brings down the arrogant and exalts the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors judge, all traditions teach that ****the moral order will ultimately judge all with righteousness and truth****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach universal praise and the coming judgment provide a moral framework that encourages worship and accountability. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are motivated to praise the moral order and to live in accordance with its standards. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****worship and accountability****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 96, Principle 1 ("The call to universal praise") and Principle 3 ("The coming judgment") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's call to all the earth to sing (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is worthy of universal praise, as the Buddhist teaching that all who follow the path rejoice, as the Gita's teaching that all paths lead to the moral order (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that all belongs to the moral order, as the Sikh teaching that all peoples praise the One, as the Confucian teaching that all peoples honour the moral order, and as the African teaching that all peoples honour the ancestors.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the call to universal praise and the coming judgment.**** The ethical lesson—that all peoples are called to praise the moral order, and that it will judge the earth with righteousness and truth—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 97: The Ethics of Judgment of Idols and Preservation of the Faithful**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 97 is a royal psalm celebrating the moral order's reign over all the earth. It opens with the declaration that the moral order is king, and that the earth should rejoice and the islands be glad. The psalm describes the moral order's awesome majesty—clouds, darkness, fire, and lightnings surround it—and its judgment over the nations. The psalm declares that all who worship idols are put to shame, while the faithful who hate evil are preserved. The psalm concludes with a call to rejoice and give thanks to the moral order.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** The LORD is king! Let the earth rejoice; let the many coastlands be glad! ****2**** Clouds and thick darkness are all around him; righteousness and justice are the foundation of his throne. ****3**** Fire goes before him, and consumes his adversaries on every side. ****4**** His lightnings light up the world; the earth sees and trembles. ****5**** The mountains melt like wax before the LORD, before the Lord of all the earth. ****6**** The heavens proclaim his righteousness; and all the peoples behold his glory. ****7**** All who worship images are put to shame, those who make their boast in worthless idols; all gods bow down before him. ****8**** Zion hears and is glad, and the daughters of Judah rejoice, because of your judgments, O God. ****9**** For you, O LORD, are most high over all the earth; you are exalted far above all gods. ****10**** The LORD loves those who hate evil; he guards the lives of his faithful; he rescues them from the hand of the wicked. ****11**** Light dawns for the righteous, and joy for the upright in heart. ****12**** Rejoice in the LORD, O you righteous, and give thanks to his holy name!

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                 | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                          |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Reign and Majesty of the Moral Order**** | The moral order reigns over all the earth with righteousness and justice as the foundation of its throne. |
| ****Principle 2: The Judgment of the Wicked and Idols****     | Those who worship images and worthless idols are put to shame. The moral order judges the nations.        |
| ****Principle 3: The Preservation of the Faithful****         | The moral order preserves the faithful who hate evil and rescues them from the hand of the wicked.        |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 97**

#### **Principle 2: The Judgment of the Wicked and Idols**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                           |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 97:7): "All who worship images are put to shame, those who make their boast in worthless idols."                             | Those who worship idols and false gods are condemned. The moral order judges them. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): The wicked will ultimately suffer. | The wicked will be judged and will suffer the consequences of their deeds.         |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 7:20: "Those whose intelligence has been stolen by material desires surrender to other gods and follow various rituals, driven by their own nature." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 16:18–20): The arrogant and wicked face destruction.           | Those who worship false gods and act wickedly face destruction.                    |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:256): "There is no compulsion in religion. The right path has become clear from the wrong path. So whoever disbelieves in false gods and believes in Allah has grasped the most trustworthy handhold."                              | Rejecting false gods and worshipping the true moral order leads to salvation.      |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee worships the One alone. All other objects of worship—wealth, power, status—are illusions. Those who worship illusions are condemned."                                                                         | Those who worship false objects of trust are condemned.                            |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is concerned with virtue; the small person is concerned with profit. The noble person trusts in the moral order; the small person trusts in false things."                                                   | Those who trust in false things are condemned.                                     |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are the true protectors. Those who trust in charms, magic, or false spirits have turned away from the ancestors. They are judged."                                                                                 | Those who trust in false spirits are judged.                                       |

#### **Principle 3: The Preservation of the Faithful**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                          |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 97:10): "The LORD loves those who hate evil; he guards the lives of his faithful; he rescues them from the hand of the wicked." | The moral order preserves and rescues the faithful who hate evil. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 120): The good person ultimately experiences happiness.                                                              | The good person is preserved and rejoices.                        |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 6:40): The sincere striver does not perish.                                         | The devoted and sincere are preserved.                            |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                   | The faithful are preserved and protected.                         |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine preserves the faithful who hate evil. Those who trust in the Divine are rescued from the wicked."                                                                                                                      | The Divine preserves and rescues the faithful.                    |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order protects the virtuous. The noble person who hates evil is preserved."                                                                                                                                            | The moral order preserves the virtuous.                           |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors protect the faithful who hate evil. Those who honour the ancestors are preserved from harm."                                                                                                                          | The ancestors preserve the faithful.                              |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the judgment of the wicked and idols and the preservation of the faithful. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, those who worship idols or false gods are condemned, and the wicked are judged.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that those who worship images are put to shame (Psalm 97), as the Jewish teaching that idolaters are condemned, as the Buddhist teaching that the wicked will suffer, as the Gita's teaching that those who worship false gods are deluded and face destruction, as the Qur'anic teaching that rejecting false gods leads to salvation, as the Sikh teaching that those who worship illusions are condemned, as the Confucian teaching that those who trust in false things are condemned, or as the African teaching that those who trust in false spirits are judged, all traditions teach that ****false worship and wickedness are judged by the moral order****.

****Second, the faithful who hate evil are preserved and rescued.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order loves those who hate evil and guards the lives of the faithful (Psalm 97), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order preserves the faithful, as the Buddhist teaching that the good person ultimately rejoices, as the Gita's teaching that the devoted and sincere are preserved, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful are preserved, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine preserves the faithful, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order protects the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors preserve the faithful, all traditions teach that ****the faithful who hate evil are preserved and rescued by the moral order****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the judgment of the wicked and the preservation of the faithful provide a moral framework that encourages the hatred of evil and trust in the moral order. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are motivated to reject evil and to trust in the moral order's protection. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****hatred of evil and trust in protection****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 97, Principle 2 ("The judgment of the wicked and idols") and Principle 3 ("The preservation of the faithful") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration that those who worship images are put to shame (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that idolaters are condemned, as the Buddhist teaching that the wicked will suffer, as the Gita's teaching that those who worship false gods are deluded (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that rejecting false gods leads to salvation, as the Sikh teaching that those who worship illusions are condemned, as the Confucian teaching that those who trust in false things are condemned, and as the African teaching that those who trust in false spirits are judged.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the judgment of the wicked and the preservation of the faithful.**** The ethical lesson—that the wicked are judged and the faithful are preserved—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 98: The Ethics of Praise and the Certainty of Just Judgment**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 98 is a hymn of praise celebrating the moral order's victory and salvation. It calls upon all the earth to sing a new song, for the moral order has done marvelous things. The psalm declares that the moral order has made known its salvation and revealed its righteousness to the nations. The psalm calls upon the sea, the rivers, and the mountains to join in praise, for the moral order is coming to judge the earth with righteousness and equity.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Psalm.****

****1**** O sing to the LORD a new song, for he has done marvelous things. His right hand and his holy arm have gotten him victory. ****2**** The LORD has made known his victory; he has revealed his vindication in the sight of the nations. ****3**** He has remembered his steadfast love and faithfulness to the house of Israel. All the ends of the earth have seen the victory of our God. ****4**** Make a joyful noise to the LORD, all the earth; break forth into joyous song and sing praises. ****5**** Sing praises to the LORD with the lyre, with the lyre and the sound of melody. ****6**** With trumpets and the sound of the horn make a joyful noise before the King, the LORD. ****7**** Let the sea roar, and all that fills it; the world and those who live in it. ****8**** Let the floods clap their hands; let the hills sing together for joy ****9**** at the presence of the LORD, for he is coming to judge the earth. He will judge the world with righteousness, and the peoples with equity.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                 |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Principle 1: The Call to Sing a New Song****             | The people are called to sing a new song to the moral order for its marvelous deeds and victory. |
| ****Principle 2: The Moral Order's Victory and Salvation**** | The moral order has made known its salvation and revealed its righteousness to the nations.      |
| ****Principle 3: The Coming Judgment****                     | The moral order is coming to judge the earth with righteousness and equity.                      |

(Corollary from Psalm 98 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a call to sing a new song. | The faithful are called to sing a new song and praise the moral order. |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 179): The well-doer rejoices in praise.                            | The faithful rejoice and praise the moral order.                       |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:11: "As people approach me, so I receive them. All paths, Arjuna, lead to me." (Corollary from Bhagavad Gita 9:22): The devoted praise the moral order.                                          | The devoted praise and rejoice in the moral order.                     |
