And be grateful to Me and do not deny Me."                                                                                               | The faithful are called to praise and be grateful.                     |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee sings the praises of the Divine. A new song is always on the lips of the faithful."                                                                            | The faithful sing praises to the Divine.                               |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who praises the moral order and rejoices in its works."                                                                                                | The noble person praises the moral order.                              |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The faithful sing new songs of praise to the ancestors for their deeds."                                                                                                          | The faithful sing praises to the ancestors.                            |

#### **Principle 3: The Coming Judgment**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                      |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 98:9): "He will judge the world with righteousness, and the peoples with equity."                                               | The moral order will judge the earth with righteousness and equity.           |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): The good person ultimately experiences happiness. | The moral order brings about the fruits of deeds. Judgment is inherent.       |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                  | The moral order intervenes to bring judgment.                                 |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:185): "Every soul will taste death, and you will only be given your reward in full on the Day of Resurrection."                                                                                                                          | The moral order will judge on the Day of Resurrection.                        |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the judge of all. The proud will be humbled, and the humble will be exalted."                                                                                                                                       | The Divine will judge all.                                                    |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is the ultimate judge. It brings down the arrogant and exalts the virtuous."                                                                                                                                     | The moral order judges, bringing down the arrogant and exalting the virtuous. |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors judge the community. The proud and oppressive will be brought down; the humble and just will be lifted up."                                                                                                           | The ancestors judge, bringing down the proud and exalting the just.           |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the call to sing a new song and the coming judgment. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful are called to sing a new song and praise the moral order for its deeds.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's call to sing a new song for the moral order's marvelous deeds (Psalm 98), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is worthy of praise, as the Buddhist teaching that the well-doer rejoices and praises, as the Gita's teaching that the devoted praise the moral order, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful are called to be grateful and praise, as the Sikh teaching that the faithful sing praises to the Divine, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person praises the moral order, or as the African teaching that the faithful sing praises to the ancestors, all traditions teach that ****praise is the appropriate response to the moral order's deeds****.

****Second, the moral order will judge the earth with righteousness and equity.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order will judge the world with righteousness and equity (Psalm 98), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order will judge with righteousness, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order brings about the fruits of deeds, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order intervenes to deliver the good and destroy the wicked, as the Qur'anic teaching of the Day of Resurrection, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is the judge of all, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order brings down the arrogant and exalts the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors judge, all traditions teach that ****the moral order's judgment is certain, just, and equitable****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach praise and the certainty of just judgment provide a moral framework that encourages worship and accountability. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are motivated to praise the moral order and to live in accordance with its standards. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****worship and accountability****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 98, Principle 1 ("The call to sing a new song") and Principle 3 ("The coming judgment") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's call to sing a new song (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order is worthy of praise, as the Buddhist teaching that the well-doer rejoices and praises, as the Gita's teaching that the devoted praise the moral order (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful are called to praise, as the Sikh teaching that the faithful sing praises to the Divine, as the Confucian teaching that the noble person praises the moral order, and as the African teaching that the faithful sing praises to the ancestors.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the call to praise and the certainty of just judgment.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order is worthy of praise and will judge the earth with righteousness and equity—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 99: The Ethics of Justice and Divine Responsiveness**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 99 is a royal psalm celebrating the moral order's holy reign and its just rule. It declares that the moral order is king and that the peoples should tremble and the earth quake. The psalm praises the moral order's justice, equity, and righteousness, and recalls the faithful leaders of the past—Moses, Aaron, and Samuel—who called upon the moral order and were answered. The psalm concludes with a call to exalt the moral order and worship at its holy mountain.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****1**** The LORD is king; let the peoples tremble! He sits enthroned upon the cherubim; let the earth quake! ****2**** The LORD is great in Zion; he is exalted over all the peoples. ****3**** Let them praise your great and awesome name. Holy is he! ****4**** Mighty King, lover of justice, you have established equity; you have executed justice and righteousness in Jacob. ****5**** Exalt the LORD our God; worship at his footstool. Holy is he! ****6**** Moses and Aaron were among his priests, Samuel also was among those who called on his name. They cried to the LORD, and he answered them. ****7**** He spoke to them in the pillar of cloud; they kept his decrees, and the statutes that he gave them. ****8**** O LORD our God, you answered them; you were a forgiving God to them, but an avenger of their wrongdoings. ****9**** Extol the LORD our God, and worship at his holy mountain; for the LORD our God is holy.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                             | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                 |
| --------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Principle 1: The Holy Reign of the Moral Order****    | The moral order is king and holy; the peoples are called to tremble and worship.                 |
| ****Principle 2: The Moral Order's Justice and Equity**** | The moral order loves justice and has established equity. It executes justice and righteousness. |
| ****Principle 3: The Moral Order's Responsiveness****     | The moral order responds to those who call upon it, as it did to Moses, Aaron, and Samuel.       |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 99**

#### **Principle 2: The Moral Order's Justice and Equity**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                           |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 1:18: "Rabban Shimon ben Gamliel said: On three things the world stands: on justice, on truth, and on peace." (Corollary from Psalm 99:4): "Mighty King, lover of justice, you have established equity."                                               | The moral order loves justice and has established equity.          |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 119: "The evil-doer may enjoy good fortune as long as his evil has not come to fruition. But when his evil bears fruit, then the evil-doer experiences suffering." (Corollary from verse 120): The good person ultimately experiences happiness. | The moral order brings about justice through the fruits of deeds.  |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 4:7–8: "Whenever there is a decline in righteousness and an increase in unrighteousness, O Arjuna, then I manifest myself. I appear from age to age to deliver the good and to destroy the wicked."                                                  | The moral order intervenes to establish justice and righteousness. |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:177): "Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward the east or the west, but \[true\] righteousness is \[in\] one who believes in Allah... and gives wealth... to relatives, orphans, the needy..."                           | The moral order loves righteousness and justice.                   |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is the lover of justice. The Divine has established equity and righteousness."                                                                                                                                         | The Divine loves justice and has established equity.               |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order loves justice and has established equity. The noble person aligns with this justice."                                                                                                                            | The moral order loves justice and equity.                          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are the guardians of justice and equity. The community is held together by these."                                                                                                                                    | The ancestors uphold justice and equity.                           |

#### **Principle 3: The Moral Order's Responsiveness**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 99:6): "They cried to the LORD, and he answered them."                             | The moral order responds to those who call upon it.     |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 168): "Rouse yourself, be diligent! Follow the teaching of the Buddha." | The path of the moral order responds to the diligent.   |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                      | The moral order responds to the devoted.                |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:186): "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the supplicant when he calls upon Me."                                                | The moral order is near and responds to those who call. |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine hears the cries of the faithful and responds. Those who call upon the Divine are answered."                                                                               | The Divine responds to those who call upon it.          |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order responds to the virtuous. The noble person who calls upon the moral order is heard."                                                                                | The moral order responds to the virtuous.               |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors respond to those who call upon them. The faithful are heard."                                                                                                            | The ancestors respond to those who call upon them.      |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the moral order's justice and equity and its responsiveness to those who call upon it. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the moral order loves justice and has established equity.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is a lover of justice and has established equity (Psalm 99), as the Jewish teaching that the world stands on justice, truth, and peace, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order brings about justice through the fruits of deeds, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order intervenes to establish righteousness, as the Qur'anic teaching that righteousness is the foundation of justice, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine loves justice, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order loves justice, or as the African teaching that the ancestors uphold justice and equity, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is characterized by justice and equity****.

****Second, the moral order responds to those who call upon it.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that Moses, Aaron, and Samuel cried to the moral order and were answered (Psalm 99), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order responds to those who call, as the Buddhist teaching that the path responds to the diligent, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order responds to the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that the moral order is near and responds, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine hears and responds, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order responds to the virtuous, or as the African teaching that the ancestors respond to those who call upon them, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is responsive to those who call upon it****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the moral order's justice and its responsiveness provide a moral framework that encourages individuals to seek justice and to call upon the moral order in times of need. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are motivated to pursue justice and to trust in the moral order's responsiveness. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****justice-seeking and trust in responsiveness****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 99, Principle 2 ("The moral order's justice and equity") and Principle 3 ("The moral order's responsiveness") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order loves justice (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that the world stands on justice and truth, as the Buddhist teaching that the moral order brings about justice, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order intervenes to establish righteousness (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that righteousness is the foundation of justice, as the Sikh teaching that the Divine loves justice, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order loves justice, and as the African teaching that the ancestors uphold justice and equity.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the moral order's justice and its responsiveness.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order loves justice and responds to those who call upon it—transcends its religious packaging.

## **Psalm 100: The Ethics of Glad Service and the Goodness of the Moral Order**

### **Introduction**

Psalm 100 is a short but powerful psalm of praise and thanksgiving, often referred to as the "Old Hundredth." It is a call to all the earth to make a joyful noise to the moral order, to serve it with gladness, and to come into its presence with singing. The psalm affirms that the moral order is God, that it has made us, and that we are its people and the sheep of its pasture. The psalm concludes with an exhortation to enter its gates with thanksgiving and its courts with praise, and to give thanks and bless its name, for the moral order is good, its steadfast love endures forever, and its faithfulness extends to all generations.

### **Full Biblical Text**

****A Psalm of Thanksgiving.****

****1**** Make a joyful noise to the LORD, all the earth. ****2**** Worship the LORD with gladness; come into his presence with singing. ****3**** Know that the LORD is God. It is he that made us, and we are his; we are his people, and the sheep of his pasture. ****4**** Enter his gates with thanksgiving, and his courts with praise. Give thanks to him, bless his name. ****5**** For the LORD is good; his steadfast love endures forever, and his faithfulness to all generations.

### **Extraction of Core Ethical Lessons**

| **Principle**                                                                   | **Ethical Core (Plain English)**                                                                                          |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Principle 1: The Call to Praise and Glad Service****                        | The people are called to make a joyful noise, serve with gladness, and come into the moral order's presence with singing. |
| ****Principle 2: The Recognition of the Moral Order as Creator and Shepherd**** | The moral order made us, and we are its people and the sheep of its pasture.                                              |
| ****Principle 3: The Goodness and Steadfast Love of the Moral Order****         | The moral order is good; its steadfast love endures forever, and its faithfulness extends to all generations.             |

### **Comparative Analysis: Ethical Equivalents to Psalm 100**

#### **Principle 1: The Call to Praise and Glad Service**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                                          |
| --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 100 itself, part of Jewish tradition): The psalm is a call to serve God with gladness and thanksgiving. | The faithful are called to serve the moral order with gladness and thanksgiving.  |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 168): "Rouse yourself, be diligent! Follow the teaching of the Buddha."                      | The faithful are called to follow the path with diligence and joy.                |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                           | The faithful are called to serve the moral order with devotion and love.          |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Baqarah (2:152): "So remember Me; I will remember you. And be grateful to Me and do not deny Me."                                                                                                                         | The faithful are called to remember and be grateful to the moral order.           |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The true devotee sings praises to the Divine with joy and serves with gladness. Service is a form of worship."                                                                                            | The faithful are called to serve the Divine with joy and gladness.                |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The noble person is one who reveres the moral order and serves it with sincerity and joy."                                                                                                               | The faithful are called to serve the moral order with sincerity and joy.          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The faithful honour the ancestors with joy and serve the community with gladness. Service is worship."                                                                                                      | The faithful are called to honour the ancestors with joy and serve the community. |

#### **Principle 3: The Goodness and Steadfast Love of the Moral Order**

| **Religion / Text**         | **Ethical Equivalent**                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | **Reduced Ethical Core**                                       |
| --------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ****Judaism****             | Pirkei Avot 3:1: "Akavya ben Mahalalel would say: Reflect upon three things and you will not come to sin..." (Corollary from Psalm 100:5): "For the LORD is good; his steadfast love endures forever, and his faithfulness to all generations."                                              | The moral order is good, steadfast, and faithful.              |
| ****Buddhism****            | Dhammapada, verse 16: "Here he rejoices, hereafter he rejoices; the well-doer rejoices in both existences." (Corollary from Dhammapada, verse 204): "Health is the greatest gift; contentment is the greatest wealth; a trusted friend is the best relative; Nirvana is the greatest bliss." | The path of the moral order is good and leads to enduring joy. |
| ****Hinduism****            | Bhagavad Gita 9:22: "To those who are constantly devoted to me and who worship me with love, I give what they lack and preserve what they have."                                                                                                                                             | The moral order is good and preserves the devoted.             |
| ****Islam****               | Qur'an, Surah Al-Imran (3:173): "Those to whom the people said, 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them.' But it only increased them in faith, and they said, 'Sufficient for us is Allah, and He is the best Disposer of affairs.'"                                             | The moral order is good, sufficient, and faithful.             |
| ****Sikhism****             | Guru Granth Sahib (summarised): "The Divine is good, and its steadfast love endures forever. The faithfulness of the Divine extends to all generations."                                                                                                                                     | The Divine is good, steadfast, and faithful.                   |
| ****Confucian****           | Confucian Analects (summarised): "The moral order is good and faithful. The virtuous trust in its goodness."                                                                                                                                                                                 | The moral order is good and faithful.                          |
| ****African Traditional**** | (Oral tradition, documented): "The ancestors are good, and their love endures. Their faithfulness extends to all generations."                                                                                                                                                               | The ancestors are good, steadfast, and faithful.               |

### **Discussion of Ethical Equivalence**

The comparisons above reveal a striking convergence across all eight traditions regarding the call to praise and glad service and the goodness and steadfast love of the moral order. Two principles emerge as near-universal:

****First, the faithful are called to praise and serve the moral order with gladness.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's call to make a joyful noise and serve with gladness (Psalm 100), as the Jewish teaching that service to God is to be done with joy, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is to be followed with diligence and joy, as the Gita's teaching that devotion is to be with love, as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful are called to remember and be grateful, as the Sikh teaching that service is a form of worship with joy, as the Confucian teaching that service to the moral order is with sincerity and joy, or as the African teaching that honouring the ancestors is with joy, all traditions teach that ****worship and service are to be rendered with gladness****.

****Second, the moral order is good, its steadfast love endures forever, and its faithfulness extends to all generations.**** Whether framed as the psalmist's declaration that the moral order is good, steadfast, and faithful (Psalm 100), as the Jewish teaching that the moral order's love and faithfulness endure, as the Buddhist teaching that the path leads to enduring joy, as the Gita's teaching that the moral order preserves the devoted, as the Qur'anic teaching that "sufficient for us is Allah," as the Sikh teaching that the Divine is good, steadfast, and faithful, as the Confucian teaching that the moral order is good and faithful, or as the African teaching that the ancestors are good, steadfast, and faithful, all traditions teach that ****the moral order is characterized by goodness, steadfast love, and enduring faithfulness****.

This is ethically significant for our hypothesis. Religions that teach the call to glad service and the goodness and steadfast love of the moral order provide a moral framework that encourages joyful worship and trust in the moral order's enduring faithfulness. Societies that internalise these principles may be more stable, as individuals are motivated to serve with gladness and to trust in the moral order's goodness. The endurance of these traditions may therefore be partially explained by their capacity to instil ****joyful service and trust in faithfulness****—qualities that favour long-term social stability.

### **Conclusion**

Psalm 100, Principle 1 ("The call to praise and glad service") and Principle 3 ("The goodness and steadfast love of the moral order") find clear ethical equivalents in all seven other religions examined. The principles are expressed through different cultural lenses: as the psalmist's call to make a joyful noise and serve with gladness (Psalm), as the Jewish teaching that service to God is to be done with joy, as the Buddhist teaching that the path is followed with diligence and joy, as the Gita's teaching that devotion is with love (Hinduism), as the Qur'anic teaching that the faithful are called to remember and be grateful, as the Sikh teaching that service is a form of worship with joy, as the Confucian teaching that service to the moral order is with sincerity and joy, and as the African teaching that honouring the ancestors is with joy.

This convergence supports our central hypothesis: ****the world's most enduring religions contain a shared ethical core concerning the call to praise and glad service and the goodness and steadfast love of the moral order.**** The ethical lesson—that the moral order is to be served with gladness, and that it is good, steadfast, and faithful—transcends its religious packaging.
